<meta>This page serves as the title page for a Renaissance edition of the "Secret of Secrets," a famous text long attributed to Aristotle. This specific edition was edited by the philosopher Francesco Storella.</meta>

# THE SECRET
## OF SECRETS
### OF ARISTOTLE TO ALEXANDER
->The Great, with the same author’s Treatise on the<-
->Immortality of the Soul, now<-
->added for the first time.<-

---

->Corrected according to the authority of ancient copies, and<-
->illustrated with most beautiful annotations, by FRANCESCO<-
->Storella of Alessano <note>A town in the Apulia region of Italy</note>, Philosopher.<-

---

->The Letter of Alexander the Great to Aristotle concerning the<-
->wonders of India, corrected by the same Storella.<-

---

->In addition, two treatises are included, namely Hippo-<-
->crates’ *Secret of Secrets*, and Averroes’ <note>Ibn Rushd, the famous Andalusian polymath</note> *Little Book on Poi-*<-
->sons; which, having been nearly lost, are now recalled from<-
->the Underworld <note>original Latin: "ab Orco"; a classical metaphor for being rescued from death or total oblivion</note> after being corrected by the same Storella.<-

---

->TO HECTOR PIGNATELLI<-
->Most Illustrious Duke of Vibo.<-

<summary>This is the title page of a 16th-century Latin edition of the pseudo-Aristotelian "Secret of Secrets," featuring works on the soul, Indian wonders, and medical treatises by Hippocrates and Averroes. The page is dedicated to Hector Pignatelli and bears the historical ownership marks of a library in Capua and the National Library of Naples.</summary>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page continues from the title page, providing the formal dedication of the volume. The editor, Domenico Pizimentio, addresses Hector Pignatelli, the Duke of Vibo, who is the patron of this edition.</meta>

# DOMENICO PIZIMENTIO
## TO HECTOR PIGNATELLI
->Duke of the people of Vibo.<-

Once Troy was safe enough through its own Hector,
    And seemed not to fear the hands of enemies.
So this excellent book, called back from the Stygian shadows <note>original: "stygiis reuocatus ab umbris"; a classical metaphor for rescuing a lost ancient text from oblivion or the "Underworld."</note>,
    Will be safe here by the name of Hector alone.
You alone, Hector, the new glory of this great world,
    Good Duke, were worthy of this divine gift.
Caesar <note>Likely a reference to the Holy Roman Emperor Charles V, whom the Pignatelli family served with great distinction.</note> had entrusted to you the secrets of a powerful kingdom,
    But a greater honor rises for you in this time.
For Nature, our parent, sprinkled with ambrosial nectar,
    Calls these the secrets of the world's nature in this little book.
Others have given different things to the people; yet the printing presses
    Themselves admit this one work is more beautiful than all others.
Therefore, let bright Vibo <note>Vibo Valentia, a city in Calabria, Italy, which was the seat of the Pignatelli dukedom.</note> lift its head above other cities,
    Destined to be ruled by so distinguished a Prince.

->A couplet <note>original: "δίστιχον" (distichon)</note> by Nicola Maraschio of Galliano<-

The son of the vintage has released this for the sake of souls.
Storella <note>The Greek text uses "Staphella," a punning Greek transliteration of the editor Francesco Storella's name, possibly playing on the word for a dried grape or raisin to match the "vintage" theme.</note>, for he fled from fruitless labors.

<summary>This page contains a dedicatory poem by Domenico Pizimentio to the Duke of Vibo, Hector Pignatelli, using classical metaphors to praise the Duke as a protector of ancient knowledge. It characterizes the "Secret of Secrets" as a divine revelation of nature's hidden truths, rescued from oblivion.</summary>
<keywords>Domenico Pizimentio, Hector Pignatelli, Vibo Valentia, patronage, Renaissance poetry, Francesco Storella, Nicola Maraschio, classical allusion</keywords>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS HECTOR
## PIGNATELLI, OF THE VIBONESE

->Francesco Storella wishes happiness to the most distinguished Duke.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring a landscape scene with a building and foliage.</image-desc>

As I am about to publish the *Secret of Secrets* <note>original: "Secretum Secretorum"; a widely popular medieval treatise falsely attributed to Aristotle, presented as a guide for kings on topics ranging from ethics and governance to health and alchemy.</note>, clarified by our late-night scholarly labors, O most distinguished Prince—a work surely not at all unworthy to be written by Aristotle and sent to Alexander [the Great]—I see that you bear yourself as a second Alexander. This is evident through the nobility of your lineage, your excellence in arms, the love with which you support those professing the good sciences, and your knowledge of letters (let flattery be absent from my words, for I speak truths well known to all).

And since, with the prudence of governing most excellently (as befits a most Christian Prince), the holiness of your life, the integrity of your conscience, and the worship of true religion, you surpass him [Alexander] by no small amount—not in name alone, but also in deed—it is not without merit that I have chosen you alone out of so many knights <note>original: "equitibus"; referring to the noble or equestrian class.</note> under whose name I might dedicate this most precious work of Aristotle. If you receive this with that kindness of spirit (as I hope) with which you have been accustomed to admit other works delivered by your most humble servants on account of your innate humanity, I shall consider myself not only to have found a most fierce defender, but also to have attained the height of human happiness. Farewell.

<sig>† ij <note>A printer's signature mark used to keep the pages in the correct order during binding.</note></sig>

<summary>In this formal dedication, Francesco Storella presents his edited version of the "Secret of Secrets" to Hector Pignatelli, Duke of Vibo. Storella praises the Duke’s character and leadership, framing him as a superior, Christian version of Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Francesco Storella, Hector Pignatelli, Secretum Secretorum, Aristotle, Alexander the Great, Duke of Bivona, patronage, Renaissance scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material for the "Secret of Secrets." While the previous page was a personal dedication to Duke Hector Pignatelli, this page begins the formal "Preface to the Reader" by the editor, Francesco Storella. He shifts from addressing the patron to addressing the general audience, defending the book’s authenticity against famous critics like Pico della Mirandola.</meta>

# STORELLA’S PREFACE

Whoever you are who approach to read this work of secrets: after you have given great thanks to most glorious God—who has allowed such a most hidden and precious secret to come into your hands—and once you have purged your mind from every stain of wickedness (lest the sharpness of your intellect be fouled and darkened by the impurity of your conscience while trying to understand such subtle and pure precepts), I want you to be persuaded of this one thing, most kind reader: that before you turn to the author himself, you do not disdain to read this preface of ours, completed with nine annotations. You will perhaps find certain things therein that you will not regret having read.

->**First Annotation: That the book is indeed by Aristotle.**<-

Even if the first Prince of Mirandola <note>original: "Mirandulanus Princeps"; referring to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance thinker who famously attacked astrology and questioned the authenticity of many medieval texts.</note> in the first book of his work *Against the Astrologers*, and the Second Prince <note>Referring to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533), Giovanni’s nephew, who continued his uncle's skeptical scholarly work.</note> in the fifth book of his *On Pre-notions*, assert that this volume is not by Aristotle—an argument that could be supported by the fact that the author in this book acknowledges a "ninth sphere" <note>In the ancient geocentric model, Aristotle only recognized eight spheres (the planets and fixed stars). The "ninth sphere" or *Primum Mobile* was a later medieval addition to the cosmological system. Its presence here is a "tell" that the book was written much later than Aristotle’s time.</note> (which no one doubts was unknown to Aristotle, unless he had called the first sphere of the stars the ninth of the elements)—nevertheless, not a few philosophers, both recent and ancient, hold this...

<summary>In this preface, Francesco Storella addresses the reader, urging spiritual purity before engaging with the text and defending the work's Aristotelian authorship against the criticisms of the Pico della Mirandola family.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Pico della Mirandola, Secretum Secretorum, Ninth Sphere, Scholasticism, Cosmology, Authenticity, Preface</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Annotation," providing a list of famous scholars and works that cite the Secret of Secrets as a genuine work of Aristotle. It serves to counter the skepticism mentioned on the previous page by showing a long tradition of acceptance among established authorities.</meta>

...as they cite it as Aristotle’s. Among these are Michelangelo Biondo in his *Physiognomy*, Bartolomeo Moles in his
treatise *On Preserving Health*, Bartolomeo Cocles <note>Bartolomeo della Rocca (1467–1504), a famous Italian scholar of physiognomy and chiromancy.</note> in the
*Anastasis*, the Suessan <note>Referring to Agostino Nifo (c. 1473–1538), a celebrated philosopher from Sessa Aurunca known for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> in his physiognomic tables,
Alessandro Achillini when he debates the subject of Physiognomy and Chiromancy <note>original: "Chiromantiæ"; the practice of palm reading, which was often treated as a legitimate science alongside facial analysis.</note>, Cristoforo Marcello when
he treats of the soul in the second book, Michael Scot <note>A 13th-century mathematician and scholar who served as the court astrologer to Emperor Frederick II.</note> in his
question *On the Nature of the Sun and Moon*, the commentator on Boethius's
*On Consolation* and *On the Discipline of Scholars*, Arnald of Villanova
in his treatise *On the Regimen of the Elderly*, Cardinal
Ferdinand Poncetto when he discusses poisons in the
first book, first treatise, Peter of Abano, both in differences
123 and 155 of his *Conciliator* (though in 142 he cites it as a work
ascribed to Aristotle), and also very frequently in his treatise
*On Physiognomy*, Albert the Great <note>original: "Magnus etiam Albertus"; the famous 13th-century teacher and polymath who integrated Aristotelian philosophy with Christian thought.</note> in his work *On Animals*,
second treatise, and finally Pietro Bono in his
*Precious Pearl* <note>original: "preciosa Margarita"; an influential 14th-century alchemical text.</note> very often—yet most clearly when he says:
"And although some say this book was not Aristotle’s,
since it does not smell of his eloquence as in his works on
nature and Metaphysics, nevertheless, because common fame testifies
it was his, and because the subject matter of the book is more
narrative, and because we find the authorities of this book [quoted] elsewhere..."

<summary>This page lists numerous historical authorities—including Albert the Great and Michael Scot—who accepted the Secret of Secrets as a genuine Aristotelian text. It concludes with an argument that the book's simpler, narrative style (compared to Aristotle's complex metaphysics) is not proof of forgery but a result of its different subject matter.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Michelangelo Biondo, Bartolomeo Cocles, Agostino Nifo, Alessandro Achillini, Michael Scot, Arnald of Villanova, Peter of Abano, Albert the Great, Pietro Bono, authenticity, physiognomy, chiromancy</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of authorities who attributed the work to Aristotle and begins the "Second" and "Third" arguments for its value. It addresses the skepticism regarding authorship and potential religious concerns for Christian readers.</meta>

...[investigated] by Johannes Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian or Assyrian physician and influential figure in early Islamic medicine.</note> in the chapter on diseases of the eyes, and by Hali <note>Likely Ali ibn Ridwan or Haly Abbas, renowned medieval Arabic-speaking physicians whose works were standard in European universities.</note> in his book *On the Secret of Secrets*, therefore we believe it was his [Aristotle's] own.

<margin>Second.</margin>
*And even if it had not been published by Aristotle, it should not for this reason be embraced with any less enthusiasm.*

From that well-known saying of Aristotle in the *Ethics*: "Socrates is a friend, Plato is a friend, but truth is a greater friend" <note>original: "amicus Socrates, amicus Plato, magis tamen amica ueritas"; a famous proverb derived from Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*, suggesting that truth must take precedence over personal loyalty to one's teachers.</note>, we can derive that sentiment of Seneca: that one ought to attend not to *who* says a thing, but to *what* is said. Even before both of them, the divine Plato declared this in the *Phaedrus*. If this sentiment is true—to say nothing of the aspects of the planets, the divisions of time, certain miraculous virtues of plants and stones, the doctrine of warring and governing, the knowledge of physiognomy <note>Physiognomy: the practice of judging a person's character or future based on their facial features or body structure.</note>, the most secret secret of secrets, and many other things which are explained in this book—since in this golden volume it is established how to have **a sound mind in a sound body** <note>original: "mentem sanam haberi instituatur in corpore sano"; a reference to the classical ideal of holistic health.</note>, then even if it were never by Aristotle, who would be loath to read, reread, learn, and even commit to memory such a great work?

<margin>Third.</margin>
*No one should be turned away from reading this book, even if some things in it are found to be not entirely pure.*

Nor should this deter anyone from reading the book: that he will find several things which could offer a scandal to a faithful Christian. For what book of the pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum"; referring to the non-Christian classical authors of Greece and Rome.</note> is there, which in...

<summary>The author argues that the *Secret of Secrets* is valuable regardless of its true author because of its vast knowledge on health and science. He also begins a defense for Christians reading this "pagan" text, suggesting that its benefits outweigh potential theological concerns.</summary>
<keywords>authorship, Aristotle, Johannes Mesue, Hali, Seneca, Plato, sound mind in a sound body, physiognomy, ethics, pagan authors</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Argument" from the previous page, addressing the potential religious dangers of reading pagan or "secular" philosophy. It transitions from the physical benefits of the text to its intellectual and spiritual utility, provided the reader is selective.</meta>

...how can it be harmful in any way? Indeed, many things are found in the books of **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire, whose works dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years.</note>, **Alexander** <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (fl. 200 AD), a celebrated commentator on Aristotle.</note>, **Averroes** <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), a Spanish-Muslim polymath whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to medieval scholasticism.</note>, **Aristotle**, and even **Plato** that cause offense—indeed, that are most clearly repugnant to our true faith—and yet, for this reason, they are not left unread by the most religious and even the holiest of men. Instead, one ought to imitate the **little bee**, which, flying to every flower and every shrub, leaves behind what is harmful and extracts only that which is conducive to the making of honey.

This is also confirmed by **Saint Jerome** <note>Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> in his writing to Damasus, saying: "The type of this wisdom" (he was speaking, of course, of the doctrine of the gentiles <note>original: "de gentilium profectò doctrina"; referring to non-Christian/pagan philosophy.</note>) "is also described in **Deuteronomy** <note>Referring to Deuteronomy 21:10–13.</note> under the figure of the captive woman." Concerning her, the divine voice commands that if an Israelite wishes to have her as a wife, he should shave her head, trim her nails, and remove her hair; and when she has been made clean, then she may pass into the victor's embrace.

If we understand these things according to the letter, are they not ridiculous? Therefore, we are accustomed to do the same when we read the philosophers. When the books of secular wisdom <note>original: "sapientiæ secularis"; meaning worldly or non-religious knowledge.</note> come into our hands, if we find anything useful in them, we convert it to our own doctrine. But if anything is superfluous—concerning idols, love, or the care of worldly things—we shave these away, we induce baldness upon them, and we cut them off with the sharpest iron like the trimming of nails.

<summary>The author argues that pagan and secular texts should be read selectively, using the metaphors of a bee gathering honey and the biblical "captive woman" who must be purified before marriage.</summary>
<keywords>Christian humanism, pagan philosophy, Saint Jerome, biblical allegory, censorship, classical education, Aristotle, Plato, Galen, Averroes</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory arguments regarding the legitimacy and history of the text. While the previous page discussed the spiritual safety of reading pagan philosophy, this section (the "Fourth" point) transitions into a historical account of the manuscript's journey through different languages, specifically the "Secret of Secrets" attributed to Aristotle.</meta>

# Fourthly, how this migrated from the Greek language into the Latin speech which we now possess.

This book was originally composed by its author in the Greek tongue. However, it was translated from Greek into the Roman or Chaldean <note>original: "chaldæū"; in this context, it usually refers to Aramaic or Syriac, which often served as intermediary languages in the medieval translation movement.</note> language by a certain **John**; then, it was translated into Arabic by that same John. This is attested by John's own preface, which has been corrected according to the reading of a most ancient manuscript, as follows:

> "**John**, who translated this book, the son of Patrick <note>original: "filius patricij"; historically identified as Yahya ibn al-Batriq (died c. 815 AD), a prominent translator in Baghdad who specialized in medical and philosophical works.</note>, a most skilled and faithful interpreter of languages, says: 'I left no place nor temple in which philosophers were accustomed to compose and deposit their works and secrets that I did not visit. Nor did I fail to seek out any expert whom I believed to have some knowledge of physical writings <note>original: "scripturis physicis"; referring to works on natural philosophy or science.</note> until I came to the **Oracle of the Sun**, which **Asclepiades the Persian** constructed. There I found a certain solitary man, living an ascetic life and most skilled in philosophy, before whom I humbled myself. I served him as much as I could and devoutly pleaded with him to show me the secret writings of that oracle, which he freely handed over. Among other things, I found the desired work for which I had come to that place and labored for a very long time. Having obtained it, I returned home with joy, giving many kinds of thanks to the Creator. At the petition of the most illustrious King, I labored, studied, and translated it first from Greek into Roman <note>original: "romanā"; while usually meaning Latin, in the context of this specific legend, it often refers to Syriac, the language of the Byzantine "Romans."</note>, and then into Arabic. For first, just as I found it in the manuscript itself, I translated the book of the most expert **Aristotle**, in which he responds to the petition of **King Alexander** in this form.'"

We have spoken disjunctively <note>original: "disiunctiuè"; the author is noting he used the word "or" because the exact intermediary language—Roman or Chaldean—is debated.</note> regarding John's translation of this work from Greek into either the Roman or Chaldean language, for the common...

<summary>This page recounts the legendary "translation chain" of the text, claiming it was discovered in a sun temple by the translator John, son of Patrick, who translated it from Greek into Syriac and then Arabic. It establishes the work as a secret correspondence between Aristotle and Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>Secretum Secretorum, Yahya ibn al-Batriq, Aristotle, Alexander the Great, translation history, Greek-Arabic translation movement, Oracle of the Sun</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical overview of the "Secret of Secrets." While the previous page introduced John, son of Patrick (Yahya ibn al-Batriq), and his discovery of the text in the Oracle of the Sun, this page clarifies the transition of the text from Arabic into Latin by Philip of Tripoli and explains how this specific edition differs from the one previously printed in Pavia (Ticinum).</meta>

<meta>catchword: Fifth.</meta>
† †

...the corrected reading into Latin <note>original: "romanū"; referring to the Latin language</note>, and a most ancient reading into Chaldean <note>original: "chaldaeū"; referring here to Syriac or Aramaic, common intermediary languages for Greek-to-Arabic translations</note>, testifies that it was first translated by **John**. From the Arabic version, however, into which John had translated it, it was turned into this Latin speech which we now possess by a certain **Philip of Tripoli**, a cleric, as Philip himself testifies in his own preface. Since that preface contains nothing relevant to the matter at hand—except for a very confused index of the chapters of the work—it has been intentionally omitted by us so that the reader is not burdened with superfluous study.

->This volume of secrets differs from the version printed at **Pavia** <note>original: "Ticinij"; the Latin name for the city of Pavia, Italy, a major center of early printing</note>, and Aristotle's short treatise on the **immortality of the soul** is attached here.<-

We also believe it should be forewarned in this place that the book at Pavia, which is titled *The Secret of Secrets of Aristotle*, was printed in a form far different from the present one. Indeed, that book is divided into three parts in which three requests of **Alexander** are satisfied: in the first, it deals with the immortality of the soul; in the second, with the preservation of health; and in the third, with the governance of princes <term>regimine principū: a genre of "mirrors for princes" providing ethical and political advice to rulers</term>. These latter two parts were extracted from the present volume, although the order of chapters and some words were changed; nevertheless, there is almost no thought in those parts that was not drawn from the present work. However, the first part <note>regarding the immortality of the soul</note> is not contained in this volume at all; therefore, lest the eager reader, looking for the volume of secrets in this edition...

<summary>This page details the translation history of the "Secret of Secrets" from Arabic to Latin by Philip of Tripoli and compares this specific edition to a different version previously published in Pavia.</summary>
<keywords>Philip of Tripoli, Secret of Secrets, Aristotle, Alexander the Great, translation history, Pavia (Ticinum), immortality of the soul, governance of princes</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor's introduction and begins the supplemental treatise on the immortality of the soul. The opening word "tione" completes the word "editione" (edition) from the final sentence of the previous page, where the editor was discussing the differences between this text and the Pavia edition.</meta>

...[if the edi]tion should lack anything that I am able to provide, I have decided to append it here. I do so all the more willingly because it is filled with both great brevity and great utility. That little treatise is written below:

O ALEXANDER, in your letter you requested three things of me. Desiring to comply with your wishes, I will first speak about the eternity of the soul, and afterwards about the others. *Socrates* often spoke with his disciples about this; he corrected them greatly when they said that souls die with the body, because in that case, the good and the evil would be equal after death, and the entire reason for living would be removed. To what end should one toil for the sake of virtues and the pursuit of theoretical sciences <term>speculatiuas sciētias: disciplines like metaphysics or mathematics that seek knowledge for its own sake rather than for practical application</term>? Therefore, to believe that the rational soul is mortal is the cause of all evils that come to mortals. In the *Phaedo* <note>One of Plato’s most famous dialogues, depicting the final hours of Socrates and his arguments for the soul's immortality.</note> it is said that *Socrates* lamented that the soul was buried in the body and held there as if in a prison, and he cited that ancient saying which goes: "We were in a certain prison from the moment we arrived." For this reason, he called the body a cave <note>original: "spleū"; likely a contraction of *spelæum* (cave), referencing the Platonic idea of the body as a dark dwelling or the "Allegory of the Cave."</note>, and *Empedocles* <note>A Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher known for his theories on the four elements.</note> called it a grotto; he said that the soul’s departure from here was a loosening of bonds and a flight from the cave. And *Socrates* persuaded the suffering *Axiochus* <note>Refers to the *Axiochus*, a dialogue attributed to Plato in antiquity but now considered apocryphal, where Socrates consoles a dying man.</note> not to fear death because of the immortality of the soul, which, after departing from this mortal body, is rewarded according to its merit. Indeed, *Axiochus*, who through the power of his [Socrates'] words previously dreaded death and mourned being deprived of these [earthly] goods, now desired death so that his soul might be freed from this prison. Nor is it true, as was said in the *Timaeus* <note>original: "Thimeo"; Plato’s dialogue concerning the creation of the world and the soul.</note>, that souls return to new bodies to undergo punishments <note>A reference to the doctrine of metempsychosis, or reincarnation, which the author here rejects as illogical.</note>. For why should souls be snatched from the chains of the body if they are to be thrust back into another body? Death would then be pointless. For the life of evil men ought rather to be extended as long as their torments were to be continued. Father *Socrates* first taught the immortality of the rational soul; and [he taught] that after separation from the body, those souls that were endowed with virtues rejoiced in delight and heavenly harmony, and...

<summary>The editor introduces a supplemental treatise addressed to Alexander the Great, which uses Socratic and Platonic arguments to defend the immortality of the soul. It rejects the idea of reincarnation in favor of a soul that is freed from the "prison" of the body to receive its heavenly reward.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Alexander the Great, immortality of the soul, Phaedo, Axiochus, Timaeus, Empedocles, rational soul, metempsychosis, afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's immortality and the afterlife, completing the sentence from the previous page regarding the soul's relationship to the Creator. The catchword "Sexta" indicates the transition to the sixth section or leaf of the gathering.</meta>

...was pleasing to the first Maker of things <note>original: "rerum opifici"; likely a reference to the Demiurge or God as the craftsman of the universe.</note>. But those souls which were devoted to obscure deeds and beastly pleasures are cast down into a dismal and hellish orb, where they are tortured by darkness, sorrow, and perpetual fire.

Plato said at one time that rational souls, once released from their bodies, approach their companion stars <note>A reference to Plato's *Timaeus*, where each soul is assigned a star to which it returns if it lives a good life.</note>, and there they are made blessed or sorrowful according to their merits and demerits. We have said in our writings concerning the soul: that the human intellect is unmixed with the impassible <term>impassible: incapable of suffering or being changed by external forces</term> and incorruptible soul; and in the book *On the Nature of the Gods*, we taught that it remains after separation from the body, along with those affections it had acquired while in the body. Therefore, because the souls of the wicked remain with their passions for gluttony, lust, ambition, and greed, they desire to perform those acts again; and since they lack the bodies and organs suited for them, they are held back by this perpetual lust for acting, and thus they possess this perpetual misery. 

But the souls of the good, who possessed virtue and speculative sciences <term>speculative sciences: disciplines like metaphysics or mathematics that seek knowledge for its own sake rather than for practical application</term>, and were uplifted by heroic virtue, remain capable of blessedness after death. Thus they are made blessed by this: that God and the Intelligences <term>Intelligences: celestial beings or spiritual forces that move the heavenly spheres</term> cling to them as their forms, and they are most beloved by God. The place of blessedness is the companion star, the mistress of the horoscopic sign <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note> through which the soul descended; and since it descends through that star, it is fitting that it should return to it to be made blessed. Therefore, Alexander, you should prepare yourself with your whole mind to grasp virtue and the sciences, rejecting vices, lest after death your soul be sorrowful for all eternity.

This book is held by us to be incomplete <note>original: "mutilum"; literally "mutilated," a common bibliographical term for a manuscript or book missing pages or sections.</note>. **Sixth.**

It can easily be gathered that this book is not complete but is mutilated in some parts; indeed, the author promises in this very work that certain things are to be explained which are not found in it. **Johannes Mesue** <note>Also known as Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a 9th-century Christian physician who wrote in Arabic; his works were standard in European medical schools.</note> and **Alessandro Achillini** <note>An Italian philosopher and physician (1463–1512) who was a prominent "Averroist" Aristotelian at the University of Bologna.</note> also cite certain things from **Aristotle’s** book to **Alexander** which are missing here, so that greater faith might be placed in us...

<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on the blessedness of virtuous souls and their proximity to God. ...[they enjoy] and so that those things cited by those men might not be missing for those who will inspect this volume, we have decided to add them here; these are the words of Johannes Mesue <note>Johannes Mesue, or Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, was a famous 9th-century Persian physician whose works were foundational to medieval European medicine.</note> in the chapter on eye diseases:

> *Aristotle, instructing King Alexander, says: the frequent use of a comb upon the head drives out the vapors that hinder sight toward the skin, so that they do not obstruct the vision. Likewise he says: the sight of green things in a place of great mildness strengthens the vision. Likewise he said: looking into a polished mirror held straight and gazing into it for a long time strengthens the vision and removes eye fatigue. Likewise he enjoined: after eye fatigue, one should enter a dark house—though not of too much gloom—and look at windows in which there is some small bit of green, or look at green water and submerge the eyes in it. Likewise he said: the practice of reading medium-sized letters strengthens the vision and preserves the health of the eyes.*

### These, however, are the words of Achillini <note>Alessandro Achillini (1463–1512) was an Italian philosopher and physician known for his anatomical studies.</note> in the question concerning the subject of physiognomy and chiromancy <term>chiromancy: the practice of palm reading or interpreting the lines of the hand</term>:

> *Aristotle also said, in the book On the Governance of Kings <note>original: "Regum Regimine"; a reference to the "Secretum Secretorum," a popular pseudo-Aristotelian text on kingship and health.</note> to Alexander the Macedonian, that Alexander possessed many properties of the sun, because as the sun was rising from the horizon and traveling through its own house <term>house: in astrology, one of twelve divisions of the heavens that governs specific areas of life</term>, he was first brought forth into the light from his mother’s womb; for the heart of the lion <note>original: "cor leonis"; a reference to the star Regulus, the brightest star in the constellation Leo, traditionally associated with royalty and greatness.</note> flowed into him.*

...they are concluded, so that nothing which previous editions provide might be missing from this edition of ours; and even if we have decided to place it here in its most corrupt form <note>The editor admits that the version of the "Secret of Secrets" provided here may be philologically imperfect or "corrupt" compared to other known manuscripts.</note>, it is as follows:

> May Almighty God protect our king for the glory of believers and strengthen his kingdom for the defense of his divine law, and cause him to endure for the exaltation of the honor and praise of the good.

I, his servant, have carried out the command enjoined upon me, and I have given my effort to seek out the book of morals on the governance of a lord, which is named the *Secret of Secrets* <note>original: "Secretum Secretorum"; a highly influential medieval treatise, falsely attributed to Aristotle, covering topics from statecraft and ethics to alchemy and medicine.</note>, which Aristotle, the prince of philosophers, son of Nicomachus of Macedonia, produced for his disciple, the great emperor Alexander, son of Philip, king of the Greeks. This Alexander is said to have had two horns <note>This refers to the legendary "Two-Horned" Alexander (Dhul-Qarnayn), a common figure in Middle Eastern lore often associated with his depiction on ancient coins wearing the ram horns of the god Amun.</note>. Aristotle composed this book in his old age and in the weakness of his physical powers, after he was no longer able to endure daily labors and the dangers of travel, nor to carry out royal affairs. This was because Alexander had appointed him as his chief vice-regent and master, whom he had chosen and loved much, because he was a man of sound counsel and great learning: of piercing intellect, vigilant in legal studies, contemplative in high-minded and gracious morals and spiritual sciences, charitable, discreet, humble, a lover of justice, and a teller of truth. For this reason, many of the philosophers considered him to be among the number of prophets who were not sent to a specific nation, nor was a law given to them. It is also found in the ancient codices of the Greeks that the most high God destined his angel to him, saying, "I will rather name you an angel than a man." Truly, he performed many wonders, great miracles, and strange works, all of which would be too long to recount in order; whence regarding his death there were diverse opinions: one sect saying that he perished according to the custom of the earth and has his monument, while a certain sect which is called...

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory narrative of the *Secretum Secretorum*, shifting from the physical decline of the aging Aristotle to the legend of his mystical departure and his influential correspondence with Alexander the Great during the Persian campaign.</meta>

The Peripatetic school <note>original: "peripatetica"; the school of philosophy founded by Aristotle, so named because he often taught while walking about the "peripatos" or covered walkway of the Lyceum.</note> asserts that he himself ascended to the empyrean heaven <note>The highest, most celestial part of the heavens, thought in medieval cosmology to be composed of pure fire or light.</note> in a column as if of fire. While he lived, therefore, Alexander grew strong by observing his sound counsel and by following his instructions. For this reason, he subjugated cities and, triumphing, acquired all kingdoms for himself; he alone held the monarchy of the whole world, and his fame went out into all the earth. Throughout all the climates <note>original: "climata"; referring to the seven geographical zones into which medieval scholars divided the known world.</note> of the world, all peoples and diverse nations subjected themselves to his command and rule, including the Arabs and the Persians. Nor was there any nation that dared to resist him in deed or in word. Furthermore, Aristotle composed many letters on morality on account of the "love of hearts" and the Final Secret <note>A reference to the title of this work, the *Secretum Secretorum*.</note>. Indeed, one of his letters is this one, which he directed to Alexander. For when Alexander had subjugated the Persians and captured their nobles, he sent his letter to Aristotle in this form:

> Distinguished teacher, director of justice, I make known to your wisdom that I have found in the land of the Persians certain men possessing abundant reason and a penetrating intellect, who strive to dominate others and acquire a kingdom; wherefore we have proposed to kill them all. Whatever you decree as worthy concerning this matter, make known to us through your writings.

### Aristotle to Alexander.

> If you are able to kill them, the power is in your hand to do so for the sake of the kingdom; however, you cannot kill the land itself. But if you can imitate <note>The Latin *imitari* (to imitate) here likely suggests adapting to or harmonizing with the environment; in other versions of this text, the word is *mutare* (to change), suggesting the transformation of the people's nature through their environment.</note> the air and water of that land as well as the disposition of the cities, you will fulfill your purpose. If you wish to rule over them with goodness, listen to them with kindness. If you do this, I am confident that with God's help they will all be subject to your pleasure and command, and through the love they will have for you, you will rule over them in a peaceful triumph.

Therefore, Alexander, having received this letter, followed his counsel diligently, and the Persians were more obedient to his rule than all other nations.

<meta>This page continues the introductory matter of the volume, following the legendary correspondence between Alexander the Great and Aristotle. The editor now addresses the reader directly to explain the scholarly rigor applied to this new edition and introduces the supplemental texts included in the book.</meta>

->What has been provided for this volume of secrets. The Eighth.<-

I shall not wrap this in silence, most diligent reader: I would not want you to be troubled if you find this edition of ours differing from previous ones in nearly two hundred places. Know that this was not done rashly, but by our deliberate effort. Indeed, so that this edition of ours might be considered most excellent, we have labored not only to apply a system of orthography <note>original: "ortographiæ"; the conventional spelling of a language. The editor is claiming to have standardized the spelling, which was often inconsistent in medieval manuscripts.</note> that was lacking in others, and to add several notes as the needs of the passages required, but also, following the reliability of ancient manuscripts, we have corrected the common version in all places where we judged it either false or less pure than the reading taken from the most ancient copies. If only we had possessed ancient copies for every chapter of the book, for it would have emerged even more improved in many places.

### What has been provided by us in the three annexed short works. The Ninth.

To this *Secret of Secrets* <note>original: "secretorū secreto"; the Latin title is *Secretum Secretorum*, a pseudo-Aristotelian work that was immensely popular in the Middle Ages as a guide for kings.</note> we have attached three treatises. The first of these is about the wonders of India, which is as delightful to read as it is corrupted in its text. This can easily be shown from one example: the Tree of the Moon, responding to Alexander, predicts that he will die in the ninth month of the following year. Yet the Tree of the Sun affirms that this will happen a year and seven months from then. This discrepancy should be attributed not to the inconsistency of the oracle or the author, but to the carelessness of some scribe <note>The "Trees of the Sun and Moon" are famous legendary oracular trees from the "Alexander Romance" tradition, said to have foretold Alexander's death when he reached the ends of the earth.</note>...

<summary>The editor explains that this edition has been corrected against ancient manuscripts in over two hundred places and introduces three supplemental works, including a treatise on the wonders of India.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, orthography, Secretum Secretorum, Wonders of India, Alexander the Great, Tree of the Moon, Tree of the Sun, manuscript tradition</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor’s introduction. It finishes the discussion on the "Wonders of India" (the first of three annexed works) and introduces the final two: a work attributed to Hippocrates and a study on poisons by Averroes. The editor explains his editorial interventions, such as correcting spelling and organizing the text into chapters, while lamenting the fragmented state of the Averroes manuscript.</meta>

...while no one will doubt it. However, being deprived of the help of an ancient manuscript <note>original: "ueteris exemplaris"</note> for this specific work, we were unable to offer any assistance beyond the system of spelling <note>original: "orthographiæ rationem"</note>, which we have applied in most places.

Truly, there remain two other works: the treatise of Hippocrates, which is titled *The Secret of Secrets* <note>While the main *Secret of Secrets* was usually attributed to Aristotle, various medical "secrets" were often attributed to Hippocrates in this period.</note>, and the short book of Averroes <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), a famous Andalusian philosopher and physician.</note> on poisons. Calling these back from the underworld <note>original: "ex Orco reuocantes"; a poetic way of saying he rescued the texts from being lost or forgotten.</note> when they were nearly extinct, we have corrected their spelling and divided the first into individual introductions <note>original: "praenotiones"</note> and the second into chapters. We wished to publish them now for the public so that such rare little books might no longer remain hidden—although we consider Averroes's book to be incomplete. 

For while he had distinguished poisons into two kinds—namely, those that kill by their specific form <term>specific form: the essential nature or "occult" property of a substance that acts directly on the human spirit, rather than through basic physical qualities.</term>, and those that kill by their complexional form <term>complexional form: the combination of the four primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) that determines how a substance affects the body's temperament.</term>—and he had divided those of the second kind into three species—namely, those that act by heat, by cold, or by moisture—the text is lacking. Since he had declared the signs and remedies for those acting by specific form, as well as those acting by complexional form (such as heat and cold), it would follow, in order for the subject matter to be complete, that he should reveal the signs and remedies for those acting by moisture; which, alas, is missing in this treatise.

->The Preface Ends.<-

# THE SECRET OF SECRETS
## OF ARISTOTLE.
### Preface.

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut drop cap 'F' decorated with scrolling foliate and floral motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>Most glorious son, most

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s address to Alexander the Great in the preface of the Secret of Secrets. The author emphasizes the need for secrecy to prevent divine knowledge from falling into the hands of the unworthy, while beginning to outline the requirements for a stable kingdom.</meta>

...to know of any obstacle: for God has bestowed such grace upon you, as well as understanding through your quick wit and your education in literature and science. Furthermore, through my previous teaching which I handed down to you, you will be able to comprehend by yourself—and understand figuratively—all that you ask to be taught. For the desire of a fervent longing <note>original: "feruentis voluptatis"</note> will open the way for you to obtain your purpose and will lead you to your desired end, God willing. 

Indeed, this is the reason why I reveal my secret to you figuratively, speaking with you in parables, riddles, and signs. I fear greatly lest the present book should fall into the hands of the unfaithful and the power of the arrogant. Thus, this divine good and secret <note>original: "arcanum"</note> would reach those whom the Highest God has judged undeserving and unworthy. I would truly be a transgressor of divine grace, a breaker of a celestial secret and of a hidden revelation. Therefore, I disclose that sacred mystery <note>original: "sacramentum"</note> to you under the witness of divine judgment, in the same manner it was revealed to me. 

Know, therefore, that many misfortunes will soon follow him who discloses hidden things and reveals secrets; he cannot be secure from accidents or future evils. May the Lord, therefore, guard you and me from such a fate and from every dishonorable act. 

After all these things, I call back to your memory that health-giving instruction which I was always accustomed to explain to you, to form your most noble soul. This will be your solace and your saving mirror. 

Every king must, of necessity, have two supports <note>original: "iuuanima", likely "adiuvamina": helps or supports</note> sustaining his kingdom. One of these is a multitude of men, through whom his rule is protected and strengthened. He will not have this, however, unless the ruler governs in righteousness over the righteous, and the subjects uniformly obey the one who rules. Just as the power of the ruler and governor is weakened and brought low by the disobedience of subjects—allowing the subjects to dominate—I shall provide you with the reason why subjects are led to obey their ruler. The cause is twofold: one is external, and the other internal. I have recently declared the external cause to you, namely...

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the pseudo-Aristotelian advice to Alexander the Great. The previous page ended by mentioning that a king requires two supports for his rule. This page details how a king should manage wealth and the importance of self-knowledge.</meta>

<sig>A ij</sig>

he should dispense his riches wisely to them <note>The king’s subjects or officials, mentioned on the previous page.</note> and exercise generosity toward them, rewarding the merits of each individual. Along with all of this, it is necessary for kings to take another precaution, which I will mention to you in what follows—specifically in the chapter concerning riches and aids. **Second**: to incline their minds toward virtuous actions. This comes first and is of the highest degree, but this second point has two causes: namely, an intrinsic one and an extrinsic one. The extrinsic cause is that the king should exercise justice regarding the possessions and the earned money of his subjects by being sparing and merciful. The intrinsic cause, however, is the "secret of the philosophers" <note>original: "secretum philosophorum"</note> and the guide whom the glorious God has pre-elected and to whom He has entrusted knowledge. I commend this secret to you, along with certain others which you will find in various titles of this book. In these, you will find the greatest philosophy and doctrine on the surface <note>original: "extrinsecus"</note>, but internally the "final cause" <term>final cause: In Aristotelian philosophy, the ultimate purpose or goal for which something is done.</term> which is intended is contained. For therein lies your primary and final purpose. Therefore, when you have perceived the meanings of these sayings and the riddles of the examples, you will fully and perfectly achieve your desired goal. May the most wise and glorious God illuminate your reason and clarify your intellect to perceive the sacred mystery <note>original: "sacramentum"</note> of this science. May you deserve to become my heir and faithful successor in it, with the help of Him who pours out His riches abundantly into the souls of the wise and grants the grace of knowing to those who study; to whom nothing is difficult, and without whom nothing can be possessed.

->Three Annotations.<-

*First: When he prays to God to confirm Alexander in the way of knowing himself, he seems to suggest that precept which was commanded by Pythian Apollo and proclaimed as if by the public voice of a herald, namely: Know thyself <note>original: "Nosce te ipsum"</note>. If anyone desires to understand the meaning of this sentence, he should look to the sixth book of Joannes Ludovicus Vives <note>Vives (1493–1540) was a prominent Spanish humanist; his inclusion here shows this is a Renaissance edition of the text.</note> on the "Causes of the Corruption of the Arts"...*

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how a king or scholar should seek wisdom, moving from the study of specific philosophical texts to the ethical requirement of keeping "sacred" or "esoteric" knowledge hidden from the unworthy.</meta>

...search the book. Let him examine the second book of Giovanni Francesco Pico <note>Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533), a philosopher who wrote on the "Death of Christ and of oneself."</note> on reflecting upon the death of Christ and one's own death. And also let him read through the first book of Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A famous commentator on Aristotle (c. 200 AD).</note> *On the Soul*. For in this way his desire will be abundantly satisfied.

**Second.** That the sacred precepts of philosophy are not to be published to just anyone is the consensus of all philosophers. Indeed, the majesty of the Godhead is diminished (as Peter of Abano says in his *Conciliator*) by "He who publishes mystical secrets." <note>original: "Qui secreta mystica uulgat"</note> Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman author known for his commentary on the Dream of Scipio.</note> relates that Numenius, who was the most curious investigator of occult matters among the philosophers, had published an interpretation of the **Eleusinian mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>. Because of this, he saw in his dreams the Eleusinian Goddesses standing in the clothing of prostitutes before an open brothel. To Numenius, who was marvelling and asking the cause of such filthiness—so unsuitable for deities—the angry goddesses replied that they had been dragged by force from their sanctuary of modesty by him and prostituted to every passerby. For this reason, those philosophers took care to hand down such precepts as obscurely as possible.

Hence the professors of Alchemy have shrouded the innermost parts of their art in wonderful obscurities, as Petrus Bonus <note>An Italian alchemist of the 14th century.</note> quite elegantly explains in his *Precious Pearl*. And **Aristotle** wrote his "acroamatic" <term>acroamatic: Referring to Aristotle's "auscultatorios" books—lectures intended only for his advanced students, not the general public.</term> books, both those published and unpublished, to Alexander. Hence also the divine **Plato** says to Dionysius that one must speak of such things through riddles. Our author, observing this and being compelled by his obedience to Alexander to bring forth the "secret of secrets," wished to explain it through riddles and figures.

**Third.** This point must also not be passed over in this place: that although the philosophers agreed that "pearls are not to be cast before everyone" in the concealment of philosophy, they nevertheless (as Simplicius <note>A 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> says) differed in their mode of concealment. Indeed, the ancients did so through riddles, figures, and examples...

<summary>This page argues that philosophical and alchemical secrets must be kept hidden from the public to preserve their sanctity, using the cautionary tale of Numenius and the goddesses to illustrate the danger of over-exposure. It establishes that the "Secret of Secrets" uses riddles and allegories intentionally to protect its "divine" content.</summary>

<keywords>esoteric knowledge, Eleusinian mysteries, Alchemy, Numenius, Aristotle, Plato, Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola, secrecy, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the "obscurity" of philosophical texts. The previous page discussed how the alchemist Petrus Bonus and others used intentional confusion to protect their secrets; this page compares the specific methods of Plato (fables) and Aristotle (conciseness) before pivoting to the ethical qualities of kings.</meta>

[exem]ples, and they were accustomed to hide [the truth] in fables. But Aristotle, knowing that anyone could interpret such things differently according to their own whim, rejected this method of hiding knowledge. Instead, he pursued another method which consists of the obscurity and brevity of words, as if it were a safer path. Since the author of this book does not observe Aristotle’s method, but rather the former [Plato’s], he seems to press it in a wonderful manner. 

For that which Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman author known for his commentary on the *Dream of Scipio*.</note> says against Colotes the Epicurean <note>An ancient philosopher who criticized Plato's use of myths in philosophy.</note> while defending Plato does not seem entirely safe. Namely, that it is fitting for a philosopher to use fables—which should rather be called "fabulous narrations"—when they are brought forward not just for the sake of delighting the reader, but for the sake of teaching. These are explained not in obscene language, but in honest words and useful maxims. Indeed, the secrets of this book are wrapped in that very genre. 

I say this "does not seem entirely safe" for two reasons. First, because it perhaps does not defend Plato in every respect; for in the *Symposium* <note>Plato’s famous dialogue on the nature of love.</note> he brings forth such obscene fables that hardly any more obscene can be found. It is possible that those who do not profess the dogmas of Plato might say such things were cloaked—as Bessarion <note>Basilios Bessarion (1403–1472), a Byzantine scholar and Cardinal who defended Plato against accusations of immorality.</note> says—when they were published. Secondly, Averroes <note>The 12th-century Islamic philosopher Ibn Rushd, famous for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> at the end of his paraphrase on Plato’s *Republic* mocks that very fable which Macrobius affirms is fitting for a philosopher. Add to this that if we did not concede that this mode of hiding knowledge was controversial in itself, we would be forced to affirm that Aristotle used it only hesitatingly in this book, since it was neglected by him in his other works.

### On the manners of kings concerning generosity and greed <note>original: "De modis regum circa largitatem, & auaritiam"</note>

**KINGS** are of four kinds: The king who is generous to himself and generous to his subjects. The king who is greedy toward himself and greedy toward his subjects. The king who is greedy toward himself [and generous to his subjects...]

<summary>This page discusses the debate between using Platonic fables or Aristotelian brevity to hide philosophical secrets, citing critiques by Averroes and defenses by Bessarion, before beginning a taxonomy of kings based on their generosity.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Macrobius, Colotes, Plato, Averroes, Bessarion, Kingship, Generosity, Greed, Secrecy, Fables, Republic, Symposium</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from discussing the philosophical methods of Plato and Aristotle to the practical ethics of kingship. It introduces a famous comparison of how different cultures—Italians, Indians, and Persians—view royal spending, marking the beginning of the "Mirror for Princes" section of the text.</meta>

¶ A king who is generous to his subjects; a king who is generous to himself and greedy toward his subjects. The **Italians** <note>In the medieval *Secretum Secretorum*, these national comparisons were used to illustrate different political philosophies.</note> indeed said: it is not a vice in a king if he is greedy for himself and generous toward his subjects. The **Indians**, however, said: a king who is greedy for himself and his subjects is good. The **Persians**, asserting the contrary and contradicting both the Indians and the Italians, said that a king is worth nothing who is not generous both to himself and to his subjects. 

But among all these, in my judgment, the worst and most to be condemned is he who is generous to himself and greedy toward his subjects, because his kingdom will quickly be destroyed. Therefore, it is necessary for us to inquire subtly into these virtues and vices, and to show what <term>generosity</term> <note>original: *largitas*. In a medieval context, this refers to "liberalitas," the noble virtue of giving correctly.</note> is and what <term>greed</term> <note>original: *auaritia*.</note> is, and where the error of generosity lies, and what evil follows from the withholding of generosity. 

For it is clear that qualities are to be condemned when they deviate far from the **mean** <note>The "Golden Mean," an Aristotelian concept where virtue is the balance between two extremes (e.g., courage is the mean between cowardice and recklessness).</note>; and we know that the practice of generosity is difficult, and its transgression is easy. It is easy for anyone to practice greed or wastefulness, but it is difficult to maintain true generosity. If you wish, therefore, to acquire the virtue of generosity, consider your own means, the timing, the necessity, and the merits of the men you give to. You ought, therefore, to give gifts according to your means, with due measure, to men who are in need and who are worthy. 

He who gives otherwise commits a sin and exceeds the rule of generosity; for he who bestows his gifts upon those who are not in need acquires no praise. And one who gives out of season is like one sprinkling salt water upon the seashore <note>A metaphor for a completely useless and redundant action.</note>; whatever is given to the unworthy is lost. He who pours out his riches beyond measure will quickly come to the bitter shores of poverty, and he is likened to one who hands over victory to his own enemies. 

He who gives from his goods in a time of necessity to men who are in need—such a king is generous to himself and to his subjects. His kingdom prospers, and his commands will be obeyed; the ancients praised such a king, and such a man is called virtuous, generous, and moderate. 

However, he who pours out the goods of his kingdom to the unworthy and those not in need is a plunderer of the state, a destroyer of the kingdom, and unfit for rule. For this reason, he is called **prodigal** <note>The text suggests a folk etymology here: *prodigus* being derived from *procul* (far) and *regno* (kingdom/wisdom), implying the wasteful person's wisdom is "far off."</note>, because his wisdom is far from his kingdom. 

Truly, the name of **greed** greatly dishonors a king and is unsuited to royal majesty. If, therefore, any of the kings [is] either of these...

<summary>This page defines the "Golden Mean" of royal generosity, contrasting it with greed and wasteful prodigality through the lens of Italian, Indian, and Persian political thought.</summary>
<keywords>generosity, greed, kingship, Secretum Secretorum, Aristotle, ethics, prodigality, political philosophy</keywords>

# On avoiding <note>original: *utanda*. Context suggests this is a scribal variation or error for *vitanda* (to be avoided).</note> greed and wastefulness.

**O** Alexander, I say firmly to you: whichever of kings continues excessive gifts beyond what his kingdom can sustain—such a king is undoubtedly destroyed, and he destroys his kingdom. I say therefore again that which I have never ceased to say to your mercy: that the turning away from wastefulness and greed, and the acquisition of <term>generosity</term> <note>original: *largitatis*. This refers to the virtue of "liberality," or giving the right amount to the right people at the right time.</note>, is the glory of kings and the longevity of kingdoms. 

And this happens when the king restrains himself and withdraws his hand from the goods and possessions of his subjects. Hence I found written in the precepts of the great teacher **Hermogenes** <note>Likely referring to Hermogenes of Tarsus, a rhetorician, though in the *Secretum Secretorum* tradition, this name is often associated with legendary wise men.</note>: that the highest and purest goodness, clarity of intellect, the fullness of the law, and the sign of perfection in a king is abstinence from the money and possessions of his subjects. 

What was the cause of the destruction of the kingdom of the **Chaldeans**? Since the excess of expenses surpassed the revenues of the cities, and thus as revenues and expenses failed, the kings extended their hands to the property and revenues of others. Therefore, because of this injury, the subjects cried out to God, the high and glorious one, who, sending a mighty wind, afflicted them vehemently; and the people rose up against them and utterly deleted their names from the earth. And unless the glorious God had helped them and sent what He sent, that kingdom would have been utterly destroyed. 

Know therefore that riches are the cause of the duration of the <term>animal soul</term> <note>original: *animę animalis*. In medieval Aristotelian philosophy, the "animal soul" provides the powers of movement and sensation required for physical life.</note> and are a part of it; and such a living being cannot endure if such a life is destroyed. Therefore, one must beware greatly of overabundance and of the excess of expenses, so that...

It cites the fall of the Chaldeans as a historical warning against fiscal mismanagement.</summary>

...so that temperance and <term>generosity</term> <note>original: *largitas*. In the Aristotelian tradition, this is the "golden mean" of giving—avoiding both stinginess and wasteful extravagance.</note> may be acquired: foolish and superfluous giving must be avoided. It is of the very essence of generosity and virtue to overlook and not pry into hidden secrets, nor to constantly remind others of a gift already given. It is also of the nature of good things and the substance of the virtues: to reward those who have earned it; to forgive injuries; to honor those worthy of honor and venerate those worthy of veneration; to help those who plead for aid; to make up for the failings of the innocent; to respond to those who greet you; to restrain the tongue; to hide an injury for a time; and to pretend to be ignorant of a fool’s foolishness.

I have therefore taught you those things which I was always accustomed to teach, and to sow them in your heart. I have confidence, then, that this instruction will be a light always shining in your ways and works, and a sufficient knowledge for your governance throughout the whole time of your life. Nevertheless, I tell you: here is philosophical wisdom in brief, and even if I had never said anything else to you except the following instruction, it ought to suffice for all your works in this world and in the world to come.

# On the final intention of kings.

Know therefore that the <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *intellectus*. Here meaning practical reason or the capacity for moral discernment.</note> is the head of governance, the health of the soul, the preserver of virtues, and the mirror of vices; for indeed, in the intellect one sees those things which should be fled from, and through it we choose what should be chosen. It is the origin of virtues and the root of all good, praiseworthy, and honorable things.

The first instrument of the intellect is the desire for a **good reputation**; for he who truly desires a good reputation will be famous and glorious, while he who desires it only falsely will be confounded by infamy. A good reputation, therefore, is that which is principally and for its own sake sought in governance; for rule is not sought for itself, but for the sake of a good name. Therefore, the beginning of wisdom and intellect is the desire for a good reputation, which is held or acquired through rule and lordship. If lordship or rule is acquired or desired for any other reason, it will not be the acquisition of renown, but of **envy**. Envy, in turn, generates lying, which is the root of all things deserving of blame and the raw material of vices. Lying gene-

<meta>This page continues the moral instruction to Alexander the Great regarding the consequences of a ruler's character. It completes a logical chain showing how vice leads to the destruction of the state, contrasts it with a chain of virtues that build society, and begins a warning against carnal desires.</meta>

...generates <term>detraction</term> <note>original: *detractio*. In medieval ethics, this refers specifically to slandering or "backbiting"—the sin of unjustly damaging someone's reputation.</note>; detraction in turn generates hatred; hatred generates injury; injury generates <term>obstinacy</term> <note>original: *pertinacia*. This implies a stubborn refusal to be corrected or to seek peace.</note>; obstinacy generates wrath; wrath generates resistance; resistance generates enmity; enmity generates war; and war indeed dissolves laws and destroys cities. This is contrary to nature, and that which is contrary to nature destroys the whole.

Strive, therefore, to acquire a desire for a good reputation, because reason, through the desire for a good reputation, draws out the truth. Truth is the root of all things worthy of praise and the substance of all good things, for it is the opposite of lies. It generates a desire for justice; and justice generates confidence; confidence generates generosity; generosity generates familiarity; familiarity generates friendship; and friendship indeed generates counsel and help. By these things, indeed, the world was constructed and laws were established for men. This is in accord with reason and with nature. It is clear, then, that the desire to rule for the sake of a good reputation is a lasting and praiseworthy good.

## On the evils that follow from carnal appetite.

O Alexander, turn away from the impulses of beastly pleasures, for they are corruptible. For carnal appetites incline the mind toward the corruptible iniquities of the "beastly soul" <note>The "beastly" or animal soul refers to the part of human nature governed by instincts and physical desires, rather than reason.</note> without any prior discernment; and thus the corruptible body rejoices, while the incorruptible <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *intellectus*. The higher reasoning faculty that connects humans to the divine or universal order.</note> is saddened. 

It must be known, therefore, that the impulse of beastly pleasure generates carnal love. Carnal love generates <term>avarice</term> <note>original: *avaritia*. Greed or an excessive desire for wealth.</note>; avarice generates a desire for riches; the desire for riches generates shamelessness; shamelessness indeed generates presumption; presumption generates infidelity; infidelity generates <term>robbery</term> <note>original: *latrocinium*. This can refer to banditry or illegal seizure of goods, often associated with a breakdown in social order.</note>; and robbery generates disgrace, from which is born captivity, which leads to the harm of the law, the destruction of social bonds, and the ruin of the whole body. This, too, is contrary to nature.

<summary>This page presents two "chains of causality": one showing how social discord leads to the destruction of cities, and another showing how carnal lust leads to the ruin of the individual and the law. It emphasizes that a ruler’s pursuit of a good reputation is the foundation of a stable, just society.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the warnings against carnal desires to the specific virtues required of a monarch, specifically focusing on the relationship between wisdom, religious devotion, and the rule of law. It continues the advice-to-kings genre common in medieval literature.</meta>

### On the Wisdom and Religion of a King.

First and most importantly, it is fitting for a king—as far as it concerns his own person—that the fame of his name be spread abroad for its praiseworthy **wisdom**, and that he should reason wisely with his subjects. From this, he is praised and honored; and from this, he is feared by men: when they see him eloquent in his wisdom and acting prudently in his works. Furthermore, it can be easily known and perceived through certain signs whether **wisdom** or **folly** rules within a king. 

For any king who subjects his kingdom to the <term>divine law</term> <note>original: *divine legi*. This refers to the medieval concept that a monarch is not above God's laws, but is their first servant.</note> is worthy to rule and be honored, and to govern with honor. But he who reduces the divine law to slavery, subjecting it to his own kingdom and power, is a transgressor of the truth and a despiser of his own law. He who despises his own law will be despised by men, because he stands condemned by that law. 

I say again what the wise philosophers, speaking with divine inspiration, have said concerning **royal religion**: that it first of all becomes the <term>royal majesty</term> <note>original: *regiam maiestatem*. A formal title for the king's person and office.</note> to obey **legal institutions**, not merely in a feigned appearance, but through the evidence of his deeds. This is so that everyone may recognize that he fears the High God and is subject to divine power. For men are usually inclined to revere and fear a king when they see that he himself reveres and fears God. 

If, therefore, he shows himself to be religious only in outward appearance, while in his works he is an evildoer—since it is difficult for wicked deeds to remain hidden or unknown to the people—he will be rejected by God and despised by men. His reputation will be brought to infamy, his power will be diminished, and the <term>crown of his glory</term> <note>original: *diadema glorie*. This refers to his symbolic and literal authority.</note> will lack honor. What more, then, shall I say? There is no price, there is no treasure that can buy back a **good reputation**. 

In addition, it is fitting for a king to honor lawmakers, to venerate the religious, and to exalt the wise and consult with them. He should propose difficult questions to them, ask questions honorably, and <term>be deliberate in his answers</term> <note>original: *differe respondere*. This implies that a king should not speak hastily, but should weigh his words with gravity.</note>. He should honor those who are wiser and more noble according to what is appropriate for the status of each person.

<summary>This page outlines the necessity of a king appearing both wise and genuinely religious to maintain his authority and reputation. It emphasizes that a ruler must subject himself to divine law and legal institutions rather than forcing the law to serve his own whims.</summary>
<keywords>royal wisdom, divine law, reputation, royal religion, legal institutions, justice, Alexander the Great, Mirror for Princes</keywords>

### On the prudence of the king.

A king must also think ahead to the future and meet future events with **prudence**, so that he might endure adversity more easily. It is also fitting for him to possess <term>piety</term> <note>original: *pietatem*. In this context, it refers to a sense of duty and religious devotion that manifests as emotional self-control.</note> and to restrain his anger and the impulses of his soul, lest an unforeseen agitation proceed into action without deliberation. He should reasonably recognize his own error and wisely correct it, because the highest **wisdom** in a king is to **rule** himself. Indeed, when a king sees that something good or useful must be done, let him do it with discretion—neither too slowly nor too quickly—lest he appear either negligent or impulsive.

### On the adornment of the king.

It is very fitting for royal **dignity** to be dressed honorably, to always appear with beautiful equipment, and to excel others in grace. Therefore, it is appropriate to use expensive, beautiful, and exotic garments. Indeed, it becomes a king to surpass others by a certain prerogative, so that through this his **dignity** may be adorned, his power may not be undermined, and due reverence may be paid to him. It is also fitting for a king to be eloquent and affable, and to have a clear voice, which is of great benefit in times of battle.

### On the silence of the king.

O **Alexander**, how beautiful and honorable it is in a king to refrain from excessive talking <note>original: *multiloquio*. Literally "much-speaking." The text argues that a king's words are more valuable when they are rare.</note> unless necessity demands it. For it is better that the ears of men should always be thirsty for his speech than that they should be glutted by his feelings. For when ears are glutted, minds are also sated, and they no longer look upon the king with pleasure. It also becomes the king to refrain from and not frequently seek out the company of his subjects, especially of low-born persons, because excessive **familiarity** among men breeds contempt for honor. For this reason, there is a beautiful custom of the <term>Indians</term> <note>original: *indorum*. Medieval European writers often attributed ideal systems of government or ancient wisdom to the people of "India," which functioned as a semi-mythical land of sages.</note> regarding the arrangement of the kingdom and the ordination of the king, which established...

<summary>This page outlines the practical requirements for a king's public image, emphasizing self-control (prudence), the importance of majestic dress to command respect, and the strategic use of silence to maintain royal mystique.</summary>
