<meta>This page continues the discourse on kingship, transitioning from general virtues to the specific example of the King of the Indians' annual public appearance. It describes the ceremonial display of power, the exchange of praise between the monarch and the people, and the economic policies regarding merchants.</meta>

¶ The king should appear before everyone only once a year with royal splendor and an armed army. He sits most nobly upon his <term>warhorse</term> <note>original: *dextrario*. A "destrier," the high-bred horse of a knight or monarch.</note>, beautifully adorned with the finest armor. They make the common people stand a little ways off, while the nobles and barons stand around him. It is then that he is accustomed to settle difficult business and declare various past events and outcomes; he shows the care and labor which he has faithfully performed for the <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: *rem publicam*. This refers to the state or the "public thing," emphasizing the king's duty to the people.</note>. Indeed, on that day he is accustomed to distribute gifts, release the less-guilty from prisons, and lighten heavy burdens, performing many pious works. When his speech is finished, the king will sit, and immediately one of the high-ranking officials who is nearby will rise—one who is considered the wisest and most eloquent of all—and he will deliver an oration in the king's honor and praise. He gives thanks to the glorious God, who has so well ordered the Kingdom of the Indians and who has adorned the fatherland with such a wise king, ¶ and who has strengthened the laudable people of the Indians in obedience and harmony. 

After these lengthy praises and royal commendations, the speaker shall turn himself to the praise of the people, commemorating their good customs and winning their goodwill. He leads them by examples and reasons toward humility, obedience, reverence, and love for the king. Once this is done, the whole population will strive to extol the king’s praises, to commend his good works, and to pray to God for the king's life; the cities and families will recount the king’s labor and wisdom. Because of this, they teach their children from infancy and lead them toward love, honor, obedience, and fear of the king. In this way, therefore, the king’s fame is published and grows both in secret and in public. 

They are also accustomed at such a time to punish evildoers and the wicked, removing them from this world so that the path of doing harm is closed off to the balance of the presumptuous, and the rest may be corrected. They are also accustomed at such a time to alleviate tributes and to make deals with the <term>merchants</term> <note>original: *mercatoribus*. In the medieval mind, protecting trade was essential for a kingdom's "fame" and wealth.</note>, remitting parts of their rents to them, and diligently defending and guarding them along with their goods. And this is the reason why India is so very populous. For merchants from everywhere flock there gladly and are well received; the rich and the poor, the citizens and the foreigners, all make a profit. From this, it follows that the royal tributes and revenues are increased. Therefore, one must beware of injuring or offending merchants, for they are indeed the bearers of praise and the fame of men...

Therefore, each person should be given what is his own; for in this way, cities are fortified, revenues are multiplied, and the king’s honor and glory increase. In this way, enemies tremble and are restrained, and the king lives peacefully, quietly, and securely; thus, he attains his noble desires.

### What a man should seek.

O Alexander, do not desire that which is corruptible and transitory, for you must soon leave such things behind. Seek instead incorruptible riches, an immutable life, an eternal kingdom, endurance, and glory. Therefore, direct your thoughts toward the good. Always conduct yourself so as to be venerable and glorious. Avoid the ways of lions and beasts in their impurities. Do not be cruel or inflexible in showing mercy to those over whom you have gained victory. Reflect upon the future and <term>unforeseen events</term> <note>original: *accidentalibus casibus*. This refers to the philosophical concept of "accidents"—chance events or changes in fortune that one cannot always predict.</note>, for you do not know what the coming day may bring forth.

### Concerning chastity.

Do not indulge your desires in gluttony, drinking, sexual intercourse, and excessive sleep <note>original: *fono*, likely a transcription of *somno* (sleep). The "four excesses" of eating, drinking, sex, and sleep were common themes in medieval moral philosophy.</note>. O merciful emperor, do not incline yourself toward the company of women, for such intercourse is a trait belonging to <term>pigs</term> <note>A common medieval trope comparing lust to the "wallowing" of swine.</note>. What glory will there be for you, then, if you practice the vices of irrational beasts and the acts of brutes? Believe me without a doubt: such intercourse is the destruction of the body, the shortening of life, the corruption of virtues, and the transgression of the law. It produces feminine motions and habits <note>A medieval medical and moral belief that excessive sexual activity depleted a man's "vital heat," making him physically and temperamentally more like a woman.</note>, and ultimately leads to that evil which we have warned of before.

### Concerning musical solace.

It befits the imperial majesty to have private, faithful companions with whom he may take delight in various instruments and types of organs when he is at leisure. For the human soul naturally delights in such things; the senses are revived, and anxiety and <term>restlessness</term> <note>original: *curiositas*. In this context, it refers less to "learning" and more to a distracted or troubled state of mind.</note> vanish.

then the body is invigorated. Therefore, if you wish to delight in such things, persevere in this lifestyle for at most three or four days, or as many as you see fit. And it is always better and more honorable that this should be done privately. Thus, when you are in that <term>upper room</term> <note>original: *solario*. A private, sunlit chamber or balcony often used for relaxation.</note>, abstain from drinking yourself and allow others to drink as much as they please. Pretend to be warmed by the wine, for then you will be able to perceive and hear many secrets. And let this not happen frequently, but only twice or three times a year. You should also have special members of your household around you who may report to you those things which are done and said throughout your kingdom. When you are among good barons, honor the wise and those whom you see are worthy of honor. Keep each person in his own rank. Invite one today and another tomorrow; clothe one today and another tomorrow; honor each person according to what befits his degree. Let there be no one among your nobles who does not feel your generosity. Let the mercy of your majesty and the nobility of a liberal mind be open to all.

### Concerning the avoidance of excessive laughter

He teaches the king, among other things, to have discretion and self-control, and to abstain greatly from laughter, because frequent laughter takes away reverence and <term>brings on old age</term> <note>In medieval medical theory, excessive laughter was believed to deplete the body's "vital spirits" and "radical moisture," leading to premature aging.</note>. Moreover, you ought to know how much more strongly you must honor men in your court and in your assembly than elsewhere; for then it is your concern that if anyone does an injury to another, he must be punished according to the quality of his person, so that others may fear and learn to abstain from injuries. For a noble and high-born person must be punished in one way, and a common and lowly person in another. It is good to observe rigor and self-control, and let there be a distinction of persons between the king and his subjects. For it is written in the <term>book of the Asclepians</term> <note>original: *libro Esculapiorum*. Likely a reference to the "Asclepius," a Latin translation of a Greek Hermetic text on philosophy and divinity.</note> that the king who is praiseworthy and beloved is he who resembles an eagle ruling among birds, not he who resembles one of the subject birds. If someone, therefore, presumes to perpetrate an injury or commit an offense in the court or in the presence of royal majesty, it must be considered with what intent he does it: whether in jest, to please you and provoke minds to joy, or in contempt and to the dishonor of your dignity. If in the first way, let him be lightly corrected. If in the second way, he shall <term>die the death</term> <note>original: *morte morietur*. A biblical idiom meaning he shall certainly be put to death.</note>.

It also emphasizes a hierarchical justice system where punishments are tailored to social rank and intent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructions to Alexander the Great regarding the maintenance of his authority and the perception of his subjects. <header>In what the obedience of a Lord consists</header>

# In what the obedience of a Lord consists

O Alexander, the obedience toward a ruler is observed in four ways: in religiosity, in love, in courtly manners, and in reverence.

¶ Alexander, turn the minds of your subjects toward yourself; remove injuries and injustices from them. Do not give men cause to speak against you, because whatever the <term>common people</term> <note>original: *vulgus*. Referring to the general populace or lower classes whose collective opinion was seen as both volatile and powerful.</note> can say about you, they can also do. Therefore, restrain yourself so that they can say nothing against you, and by this, you will avoid their <unclear>silent discontent</unclear>. Moreover, know that the discretion of maturity is the glory of dignity, the reverence of lordship, and the exaltation of a king. Indeed, it is the highest prudence that reverence for you should dwell in the hearts of your subjects, even more than love. For it is read that a king in his kingdom is like rain upon the earth, which is the grace of heaven, the blessing of God, the life of the land, and the help of the living.

Because through rain the way is prepared for merchants and help is given to builders. Nevertheless, sometimes within the rain there are thunders, lightning strikes, torrents swell, the seas roar, and many evils occur, through which many living things perish. Yet these <term>accidental evils</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, "accidental" refers to secondary or incidental effects that are not the primary purpose of a thing—here, the storm is an "accident" of the life-giving rain.</note> do not prevent men from praising the glorious God in His majesty, considering the signs of His grace and the goods of His mercy, because through the rain new things are brought to life, plants sprout, and blessing is poured into all living things. And therefore men give praise to God and forget the past evils that happened to them.

The example of the king matches the example of the winds, which God sends forth in abundance from the treasury of His mercy; and through them He brings the clouds, the crops grow, the fruits of the trees ripen, spirits regain their strength, the desired water is received, and the way is opened to those who sail. And many other good things follow from the winds. Yet they also bring hindrances and various dangers, both on land and in water; they bring pains to the inner parts, they scatter and dissipate the riches of men in the waves of the sea, and through them corruptions of the air are generated, deadly poisons are nourished, and many other inconveniences follow. And when the lower creatures implore the mercy of the Creator to take these evils from them, He nonetheless allows the winds to complete their course and keep the order He established for them; for He guides all things with His wisdom in equal weight; certainly...

<summary>This page advises Alexander to secure his subjects' obedience through justice and reverence, using metaphors of rain and wind to explain how a king’s power remains a divine blessing even when it causes incidental harm.</summary>
<keywords>obedience, kingship, justice, metaphors of nature, divine right, Alexander the Great, Secretum Secretorum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analogy comparing a king's rule to the natural elements. in order: he destined and ordained these things for his servants, so that they might serve; and this he brought forth out of his immense mercy and inexpressible goodness. Indeed, this same <term>parable</term> <note>original: *parabola*. The author uses the patterns of nature—the seasons and weather—as a teaching story or metaphor for the complexities of political rule.</note> applies to winter and summer: their cold and heat he established with the highest prudence and unchangeably for the generation, propagation, and durability of natural things; yet many inconveniences and mortal dangers arise from the cold of winter and the heat of summer. So it also happens with a king: for many inconveniences are accustomed to come from him, which displease his subjects and which they bear with difficulty; nevertheless, the greatest utility will reside in them.

### On the mercy of the King

O Alexander, look into the poverty and the needs of wretched and weak persons; assist the needy in their indigence out of your clemency; choose a man knowing their language, eloquent, and a lover of justice, who may be able to fill your place and to rule and love them mercifully. For in this is the observance of the law, the joy of men, and the good pleasure of the creator.

### On prudence and its utility

O Alexander, store up for yourself <term>legumes</term> <note>original: *leguminibus*. This refers to beans, peas, and pulses which were vital for long-term storage as they did not spoil as quickly as other foods.</note> and useful grains for eating, which may suffice for your lands in the time of famine and want; so that when a year of famine and want comes—as is accustomed to happen—your prudence may be able to assist your people. And in a time of necessity, cities must be succored; and then you ought to open your storehouses and make known throughout the kingdom and the cities the <term>corn</term> <note>original: *frumentum*. In this medieval context, this refers generally to any cereal grain like wheat or barley, rather than modern American maize.</note> and stored grains. This will be great caution and the highest prudence: a fortification for the kingdom, the salvation of the people, and the protection of the cities; then your command will penetrate, then your shield will prosper, and by your providence, salvation will come to everyone. Then all will know that your eyes see far off. And because of this, they will appreciate your clemency and be careful not to offend your majesty.

<meta>This page transitions from the practicalities of economic foresight (such as storing grain for famines discussed on the previous page) to the ethical and spiritual foundations of kingship: the prohibition of unjust killing and the absolute necessity of keeping one’s word.</meta>

### On the evils that follow from unjust homicide.

<note>The Latin header uses the spelling *homoicidio*, a medieval variant of *homicidio*.</note>

O Alexander, I have frequently warned you, and I still warn you: that you guard my teaching. If you observe it, you will achieve your purpose, and your kingdom will remain durable. Take care that human blood is not shed by you, since this belongs to God alone, who knows the secrets of hearts and the hidden things of men. Do not, therefore, assume for yourself the divine office, because it is not given to you to know the divine secret. Beware, therefore, as much as you can, of shedding human blood. For the excellent teacher <term>Hermogenes</term> <note>original: *Hermogenes*. A figure often cited in medieval wisdom literature as a legendary source of ancient philosophical and alchemical knowledge.</note> wrote, saying: when a creature kills a creature like itself, all the powers of the heavens cry out to the divine majesty, saying: "Lord of the universe, your servant wishes to be like you." And if he kills him unjustly, the exalted Creator will respond: "Leave him be; for he who kills shall be killed. Leave vengeance to me, and I shall repay." And as many times as the powers of heaven present the death of the slain in their praises, so long until vengeance is taken upon the killer, which will be for those persisting in eternal punishments. 

O Alexander, you have knowledge of all punishments; you have learned many kinds of evils through experience. Recall to memory the deeds of the fathers; look into the paternal annals. From there you can extract many good examples; for past events provide certain <term>instruction</term> <note>original: *documenta*. In this context, it refers to lessons or evidence derived from history to guide future behavior.</note> for future ones. Do not despise your subordinates; for one who is lowly and of little account, as is accustomed to happen, can quickly rise to honors and riches, and then he will be stronger and more powerful to do harm.

## On keeping faith.

Beware lest you break a given promise and confirmed treaties, since this is characteristic of unfaithful youths and harlots. Keep your promised faith faithfully, since a bad end follows every unfaithful person. And even if some good might come from breaking treaties, nevertheless the hope itself is evil, a reprobate example from the genus of evils. Know, therefore, that through faith comes the gathering of men, the inhabiting of cities, the communion of men, and the union of kings; through [faith...]

<meta>This page continues the ethical instruction to Alexander, moving from the prohibition of murder to the sacred importance of fidelity and oaths. By fidelity, the same camps are held, cities are preserved, and kings live. Indeed, if you take away faith, all men return to their original state—that is, to the likeness of brutes and the similarity of beasts. Therefore, most faithful king, beware of breaking your given word. Keep your oaths and treaties firmly, even if they were granted out of favor. You do not know that, as <term>Hermogenes</term> <note>original: *Hermogene*. Refers to the legendary philosopher and master of secrets previously mentioned.</note> testifies, there are two spirits who guard you: one of whom stands at your right hand, and the other at your left, guarding and knowing your works, and reporting to your Creator whatever you decide to do. In truth, this alone ought to restrain you and anyone else from every dishonest deed. 

What compels you to swear so frequently? This must not be done except for a great cause and necessity. Indeed, a king ought not to swear unless he is greatly petitioned and frequently requested. You do not know that it is not fitting for your dignity, and you detract from your honor when you swear; it is for subjects and servants to swear oaths. If you ask what was the cause of the destruction of the kingdom of the Hebrews and the <term>Scythians</term> <note>original: *cytarū* / *Scytharum*. The Scythians were an ancient nomadic people of central Eurasia, often cited in medieval texts as a once-powerful nation.</note>, I would answer you: it was because their kings used oaths for fraud and the deception of men and neighboring cities, breaking treaties which had been established for the safety and benefit of the human race. Those wicked and unfaithful men abused oaths for the subversion of their neighbors, and therefore the equity of the most just Judge did not wish to endure them any longer.

## On the organization of the empire, and of one’s own household.

O most learned son Alexander, I want you to know: that in the organization of the empire and government, there are certain very specific and highly moral teachings pertaining to you alone, for the governance of your own household and all the people. But they do not have a place here; nevertheless, I will deliver them to you in a certain place in this book, and they will be life-giving teachings, abbreviated and very useful to you, in the observance of which you shall prosper, the Lord granting. Do not <term>repent of things past</term> <note>original: *poenitere p reperta*. This suggests a king should not be wavering or prone to second-guessing his own decrees, which was seen as a sign of weakness.</note>, for this is characteristic of weak women. Manifest your integrity, preserve courtly manners, and practice goodness; in this lies the defense of the kingdom and the destruction of the enemy.

### Annotation.

When he instructs that you should not repent for a past thing, he seems to offer poison, for one might suspect that in this way all repentance for sins is taken away. However, I do not believe the author’s statement should be understood so absolutely; rather, I think he wishes to persuade Alexander that he should not repent of a past matter which was completed as a good work (as those who are inconstant are prone to do). Therefore, to the poison that could be drunk from this, he applied an antidote below, saying that those seeking and imploring pardon for their commissions, and repenting for their offenses, can pray to Divine clemency to turn away from them that which they dread. <note>The commentator is clarifying a potentially controversial point: the text isn't telling the King to avoid religious penance for sins, but rather to avoid the "buyer's remorse" or indecision that plagues weak leaders after they have done something good.</note>

## How a King ought to organize studies.

Prepare <term>gymnasia</term> <note>original: *gymnasia*. In this context, these are schools or centers for higher learning, modeled after the classical Greek tradition.</note>: establish centers of study in the cities of your kingdom: permit and command your people to teach their sons the knowledge of letters: and make them study in the noble and <term>liberal sciences</term> <note>original: *liberalibus scientijs*. The "Liberal Arts," such as grammar, logic, and rhetoric, which were considered the essential education for a free citizen and a leader.</note>: and your <term>providence</term> <note>original: *puidentia*. Here meaning the king's foresight and his financial provision or patronage.</note> ought to assist them in their necessities: grant some special privilege to those who study well and make progress, so that by these means you may provide an example and motivation for other scholars to be diligent: hear their petitions: receive and pay attention to their letters: praise those who deserve praise: and reward those who deserve reward. 

In this way you will stir up learned men to chronicle your praises and to perpetuate your deeds in writing. This method is commendable, and this prudence is to be praised; for by this the empire is honored and the whole kingdom is adorned, the court is enlightened, and the annals and royal deeds are better committed to memory. What elevated the kingdom of the Greeks? What spread the fame of their deeds perpetually through the whole world? Surely it was the diligence of students and the integrity of wise men, who beyond mo... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; "supra mo-" likely intended to continue as *supra modum* (beyond measure) or *supra montem* (above the mountain/world).</note>

### On the guarding of one's own person from women.

**O** Alexander, do not by any means trust in the works and services of women: do not entrust yourself to them. And if necessity requires it: entrust yourself to her who is believed to be faithful and beloved by you. For while a woman treats your person, know yourself to be a certain deposit committed to her care, because your life is in her hands. Beware of deadly poisons. For it is not a new thing for men to be poisoned. It is known how great a multitude of kings and lords have anticipated their day of death and succumbed to poisonous potions.

### That one should not trust in a single Physician.

**O** Alexander, do not trust in a single physician: for a single physician is powerful enough to do harm: and he might easily dare to presume wickedness and bring a crime to fulfillment. Therefore, if it can be done, let there be ten, and no fewer: but make them come together as one. And if medicine must be taken: do not take it except by the counsel of many. And then the king ought to have one faithful man among them who knows the types of <term>ingredients</term> <note>original: *specierum*. In a medical context, this refers to the "species" or raw medicinal components like herbs and spices used to create a compound.</note> and their qualities: who ought to collect all things that are necessary in the composition: and by the counsel of the physicians, with a certain weight and measure, prepare the whole as is fitting.

O Alexander, recall the deeds of the Queen of the Indians when she sent you, as a token of friendship, many gifts and graceful presents: among which was sent that most beautiful girl, who from her infancy had been nourished and imbued with the poison of serpents; thus, her very nature had been converted into the nature of serpents. And had I not at that hour wisely inspected her and judged her by the <term>magic art</term> <note>In this context, the "magic art" often refers to physiognomy or natural philosophy—the ability to read a person's internal nature through their external appearance and behavior.</note>—for she fixed her gaze boldly, horribly, incessantly, and shamelessly into the faces of men—I perceived indeed that she would kill men by her bite alone. This fact you later learned and proved by experiment. And had I not most certainly demonstrated this, your death would have followed in the heat of intercourse <note>original: *in ardore coitus*. Medieval medical theory suggested that bodily spirits and "vapors" were intensely exchanged during intimacy, making the "Poison Maiden" especially lethal in such a moment.</note>. For "Magi," as **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher who wrote extensively on the natural sciences.</note> says in his commentary on the second chapter of Matthew, are grammatically called "great ones." Nor are Magi to be considered evildoers <note>original: *malefici*—sorcerers or those who practice harmful black magic.</note>, because "magus" is properly a name for a great man who, possessing knowledge of all things, and conjecturing from the coming events and effects of nature, sometimes foretells and brings forth the wonders of nature. From this, evildoers—hiding the unspeakable crime of their art—have attributed to themselves the venerable name of Magi, though they clearly deserve to be called not "magi" but "mali" <note>original: *mali*—meaning "evil ones." The author is making a pun on the phonetic similarity between the two words.</note>. trouble and great injury. For example, when a winter 
prepares itself to be too cold, men prepare warm 
lodgings, provide clothing, and acquire coal and wood; and 
thus they fortify themselves against many other things. Because 
of this, when winter and cold come, they are not harmed. They 
also do this in the summer heat. For through cold foods and 
various remedies, they escape the heat of summer. 
Similarly, we can also foreknow years of famine and 
want; through the conservation of grain and the 
provident arrangement of resources, they endure the time of 
famine less painfully and more lightly. It is, therefore, very 
beneficial to foreknow coming events because they are better 
avoided. And because men, when they foreknow future 
events, can implore the high Ordainer <note>original: *ordinatorem excelsum*—referring to God as the one who sets the universe in order.</note> 
with all their prayers, that by His power He might turn 
future events away from them and order things differently.

through prayers, petitions, devotions, fasts, sacrifices, 
almsgiving, and many other good deeds, begging for 
forgiveness for their sins and repenting for their 
guilts; and then it is likely that Almighty God will 
turn away from them what they fear and dread.

## Five Annotations.

**First.** He asserts too superstitiously, both here and elsewhere in this book, that faith should be placed in astrology. Lest a Christian person be deceived by the superstition of a pagan philosopher, let it be investigated how faith is to be applied to this field by a follower of Christ, according to <term>St. Thomas</term> <note>Original: "D. Thoma," referring to St. Thomas Aquinas. The commentator is cautioning the reader to view astrology through the lens of Aquinas’s theological limits on astral influence.</note> who explained this matter in a specific short work.

**Second.** See how even a pagan philosopher acknowledges that pious works are in some way the causes by which we obtain something good from God (as the truth of the most holy Roman Church determines)—a fact which the <term>Lutheran heretics</term> <note>Original: "Luterizantes haeretici." This annotation shows the text being used as a weapon in the religious conflicts of the 1500s, arguing that even "pagans" understood the value of good works, which the Reformers questioned.</note> (because their hearts are blinded) refuse to understand.

**Third.** Not only does the Church sing that God can be moved by prayers, fasting, almsgiving, and other good works, but even the heathen philosopher now concedes it. Yet if God is immutab- <note>The text ends mid-word. The catchword "lis est" indicates the word is "immutabilis," meaning "unchanging." This likely begins a theological debate on how prayer can affect an unchanging God.</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the mathematical study of stars (astronomy) and their interpretive use (astrology), while introducing a section on preventative medicine. A later commentator adds notes to ensure the text aligns with Catholic doctrine and refutes Lutheran views on good works.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, astrology, science of judgments, star catalog, preventative medicine, Thomas Aquinas, Counter-Reformation, Lutheranism, good works</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and technical definitions from the previous section. While the previous page focused on medical preservation and star counts, this text shifts to a Neoplatonic defense of prayer before returning to the formal distinctions between astronomy and astrology.</meta>

...the question <note>Original: "lis est," referring to a scholarly dispute or point of inquiry.</note> is, how can we say that prayers are heard and that God is moved by them? Or does the same thing happen to us that usually happens to sailors? Those who pull on a rope by which a ship is tied to a rock seem to pull the rock toward themselves, when in reality—while the rock remains unmoved—they themselves, along with the whole ship, move toward it. Furthermore, if they strike the rock with force, it will seem that the rock flees from them, although it does not move, but they are the ones distanced from it.

So also we, through prayer and other good works, as if by a **golden chain** <note>Original: "catenam auream." This is a famous Neoplatonic metaphor, originating in Homer but adapted by Dionysius to describe the connection between the divine and the earthly.</note> (as **Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian philosopher whose works blended Christian doctrine with Neoplatonic thought.</note> says) hanging from heaven to earth, approach God while He remains unmoved. For although it may seem to us that these things pull God toward us, the truth of the matter is that they lead us to God. In this same way, such means directed toward God, performed according to the requirements of the law, bring our petitions to fulfillment—while God, who had arranged for that result to be obtained through these means, remains unmoved—although it may seem to us that those means move the unchanging God to grant that result. But if we commit vices, we become further removed from the most pure God through them, although it seems to us that He is the one who retreats from us.

Regarding this matter, one may read certain things in the works of the **Prince of Mirandola** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a celebrated Renaissance humanist known for his "Oration on the Dignity of Man."</note> in the third book *On Love* which he wrote in the Italian language, and in the "Divine" **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote extensively on Egyptian rituals and theurgy.</note> explaining the *Mysteries of the Egyptians*. However, the most eloquent **Nicolaus Leonicus** <note>Niccolò Leonico Tomeo (1456–1531), a Venetian scholar who taught at Padua and translated many Greek works into Latin.</note> left behind much more on this same subject in his dialogue *On Prayers*; and **Simplicius** <note>Simplicius of Cilicia (c. 490–560 AD), one of the last great pagan philosophers of late antiquity.</note> wrote on this while expounding upon the *Enchiridion* <note>The "Handbook" of Stoic ethics.</note> of **Epictetus**.

Fourth, there is a common distinction among Astrologers between <term>Astronomy</term> <note>The mathematical study of the positions and movements of celestial bodies.</note> and <term>Astrology</term> <note>The study of the influence of celestial bodies on human affairs.</note>, namely that the former [studies] the courses of the stars, the motions of the heavens, and the distances of the signs...

...explanations and other such things; but through this one, the faculty of judging is held, as the author assigns in the present context. However, the ancients, and especially **Aristotle** himself, do not observe this distinction; hence when in the *Posterior Analytics* <note>Original: "primo post," referring to Aristotle's *Analytica Posteriora*, a foundational text on logic and scientific demonstration.</note> Aristotle says ("and **Astrology** likewise"), **Philoponus** <note>John Philoponus, a 6th-century commentator known as "the Grammarian."</note> not without reason says that he means **Astronomy**. And when in the second book of the *Physics* it is said ("and furthermore if Astrology is another thing"), the same Grammarian asserts he calls it Astrology, which we properly call Astronomy; for among the ancients the names were confused and both were used interchangeably for one another.

The fifth point affirms that there are one thousand and twenty-nine **fixed Planets**. But this passage sends out a "sting" <note>Original: "stimulum," a metaphor for a sharp intellectual problem or contradiction.</note>; for if planets, by everyone's consensus—as the name itself indicates <note>The word "planet" comes from the Greek *planetes*, meaning "wanderer," which contradicts the idea of being "fixed."</note>—are distinguished from the fixed stars, how can "fixed planets" or "fixed stars" be called planets? Because of this, there is a conjecture—though perhaps it is entirely deceptive—that the text should be read: "know therefore that they are the **fixed [stars] and the Planets**."

## On the preservation of health.

It should be known, therefore, that there is no way to do or acquire any thing except through **power**; and there is no power except through **health**; and there is no health except through the **equality of complexions** <note>In medieval medicine, "complexion" refers to the specific balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>; and there is no equality of complexions except through the **tempering of the humors** <note>The four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were believed to govern human health and temperament.</note>. And glorious God ordained the method and remedy for the tempering of the humors and the preservation of health, and for acquiring many things; He revealed them to **philosophers**, and **holy prophets**, and certain others whom He chose beforehand and illuminated with the spirit of divine wisdom and endowed with the gifts of knowledge. From these sources, the followers of philosophy among the **Indians**, the **Latins**, the **Persians**, and the **Greeks** took their beginning and origin; they translated from them and wrote down the principles and secrets of the arts, because in their writings nothing foolish and nothing reproachable is found.

You must know, however, that the glorious God, among the other prophets <note>original: "prophetas"; in this context, the author likely refers to the great sages or teachers of antiquity</note>, especially enlightened the Greeks for the purpose of acquiring knowledge and understanding the various types of natural things. Regarding this, we have provided you with proof elsewhere; therefore, we have proposed to proceed according to their teachings in this book, God willing.

## On the composition of man.

Thus the wise and the <term>natural philosophers</term> <note>scholars who studied the physical world and biology before the modern division of sciences</note> agree: that man is composed of opposing elements and of four contrary <term>humors</term> <note>blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile; the balance of these was thought to determine health</note>, which always require food and drink. If a man lacks these, his substance is corrupted; and if he uses them excessively or insufficiently, he falls into weakness, illness, and many other troubles. If, however, he uses them temperately, he will find help for his life, strength for his body, and the health of his entire substance. 

Therefore, they have unanimously agreed that whoever transgresses the proper measure in fullness and emptiness <note>repletion and evacuation, or eating and fasting</note>, in sleep and wakefulness, in movement and rest, in the loosening and retention of the bowels, and in the drawing of blood <note>bloodletting or phlebotomy</note>, will not be able to escape the fury of sickness and the distress of infirmities. I intend to determine the truth concerning all these matters with appropriate brevity, handing down the most certain doctrine regarding the types of illnesses and their remedies. 

Thus all agree that he who guards himself against excess and deficiency by preserving equality and <term>temperance</term> <note>the "golden mean" or ideal balance of the body's internal state</note> will attain health-giving wellness and length of days. For my part, I have not found any of the philosophers disagreeing with this opinion: that all the delightful things of this world—whether luxuries, honors, or pleasures—are all sought for the sake of endurance <note>original: "durabilitatem," referring to the preservation and longevity of the individual</note>. He therefore who desires to live or to endure should study to acquire those things which are suited to endurance and preserve life, and he should renounce his own [unregulated] desire.

of the will. Nor should he add meal upon meal.

I have heard of Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> that he observed strict diets, for which reason he suffered bodily weakness. To him, his disciple said: "Distinguished teacher, if you were willing to eat well, you would not endure such great bodily weakness." Responding, however, Hippocrates said: "Son, I eat to live; I do not live to eat." For nourishment is to be sought for the sake of endurance, and not endurance for the sake of nourishment. Indeed, I have known many who reduced their nourishment and eating, abstaining from their appetites, sparing their gluttony, and living temperately through diets.

¶ Because of this, they were of the healthiest body, of longer life, of good appetite, of lighter movement, and of better function. And this is manifest among the Arabs, and those often traveling through deserts and long journeys. This is, therefore, a great argument: that to abstain from excessive eating and to purge superfluity <note>superfluity refers to the excess waste or "bad humors" produced by overeating</note> is the height of health.

¶ Alexander <note>The text is likely part of the "Secret of Secrets," a popular medieval treatise framed as a letter of advice from Aristotle to Alexander the Great.</note>, in medicine are contained the most certain and true teachings, which consist principally of two things for the preservation of health. The first is: that a man should use foods suitable to his age, the season in which he is, and the custom of his nature; so that he may use the foods and drinks with which he is accustomed to be nourished, and he will strengthen his substance. The second is that he should purge himself of that which is generated from superfluity and from corrupted humors. 

It must be known, therefore, that the bodies of men, which are the receptacles of food and drink, are diminished and dissolved—both the bodies themselves that receive, and the nourishment itself which is received. They are dissolved, therefore, first by <term>natural heat</term> <note>the internal vital flame believed by medieval scientists to power digestion and life</note>, which dries the moisture of the body and is nourished and fed by that same moisture. They are dissolved, secondly, by the heat of the sun and the wind, which dries the moisture of all bodies and is fed by the moisture of both bodies and rivers. Therefore, when a body is hot and vaporous, heavy foods are beneficial for it, since that which is dissolved and emitted from such a body will be of great quantity and thick because of the excessive heat and vapor of the body. But when a body is dense and dry, thin and moist foods are beneficial for it. Since indeed that which is dissolved from such a body is small, which...

It is therefore a certain teaching for the preservation of health that a man should use foods suitable to his complexion <term>complexion: in medieval medicine, this refers to the specific balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within an individual</term> while in a state of health. For example, if someone is of a hot nature, temperate hot foods are suitable for him; but if he is of a cold nature, temperate cold foods suit his complexion. I say exactly the same regarding a moist or a dry body. If, therefore, heat should be increased and inflamed by excessive inflammation—either because of hot and strong foods, or because of a dominant and conquering external heat—then opposites and contraries are helpful: namely, cold things <note>This follows the ancient medical principle of "contraria contrariis curantur" or "opposites are cured by opposites."</note>.

When the stomach is hot, strong, and good, strong and coarse foods are better for such a stomach; because such a stomach is like a powerful fire able to burn many thick logs. When, however, the stomach is cold and weak, subtle <note>refined or easy to digest</note> and light foods are better for it; because it is compared to a fire that burns reeds and thin wood.

->Annotation.<-

The author attributes that maxim—*I eat that I may live, I do not live that I may eat* <note>original: "comedo ut uiuā, non uiuo ut comedā"</note>—to Hippocrates; but Laertius <note>Diogenes Laertius, a biographer of Greek philosophers</note> in his second book affirms it was said by Socrates.

### On the signs of a good and bad stomach.

The signs of a good stomach are these: lightness of the body, clarity of the intellect, and a stirred appetite. The signs of a bad stomach and weak digestion, however, are these: heaviness of the body, softness <note>likely referring to a lack of muscle tone or a feeling of puffiness</note>, laziness, swelling of the face, frequent gaping of the mouth <note>yawning</note>, heaviness of the eyes, and foul or bad belching—specifically when one belches up something sour, tasteless, bitter, watery, or stinking. Because of this, gases <term>ventositates: internal "wind" or flatulence believed to cause various ailments</term> and inflammations are generated in the belly, and the appetite is diminished. When the condition is present in a greater quantity, then there occur hawkings <note>clearing the throat or spitting</note>, the stretching of the extremities, the twisting of the limbs, tremors of the body, and the opening... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing "the opening of the mouth" in a yawn.</note>

...foulness of the mouth and certain other evils which are altogether contrary to health: they are destructive to the body and corruptive to nature. Therefore, most merciful King, you must diligently guard yourself against the aforementioned unsuitable things.

# On the regime of health.

Since the body is corruptible and decay happens to it because of the opposition of the complexions <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within an individual</term> and humors which are within it, it seemed right to me to write for you in the present work certain useful and entirely necessary things from the secrets of the medical art. If you are satisfied with these—especially since it is unseemly for a king’s every infirmity to be exposed to a physician—and if you consult this manual <note>original Latin: "exemplari"</note> and live according to this most precious order, you will not need a physician, except for accidents of war and other things that cannot be avoided at all.

# What should be done after sleep.

O Alexander, when you have risen from sleep, it is necessary for you to walk a little, to stretch your limbs evenly, and even to comb your head. For the stretching of the limbs strengthens the body, and the combing of the head draws out the vapors <note>In medieval medicine, "vapors" were gasses produced by digestion that were thought to rise from the stomach to the brain, causing various ailments if not released.</note> that ascended to it from the stomach during the time of sleep. In summer, wash with cold water, because it constricts and retains the heat of the head; this will also serve to stimulate the desire to eat. Then clothe yourself in the best garments and adorn yourself with the most beautiful ornament, because your mind will naturally take delight in such a sight and beauty, and the strength of a splendid life will be comforted and greatly delighted thereby. Then you shall rub your teeth and gums with the bark of a tree that is hot and dry and of a bitter flavor. This indeed helps much: for it cleanses the teeth, dissolves the foulness of the mouth, makes the tongue eloquent, and clarifies and prepares the speech...

<meta>This page continues the "Regime of Health" for Alexander the Great, moving from morning hygiene (combing and washing) to the stimulation of appetite, the use of scents for the soul, medicinal electuaries, and the proper order of eating different types of food to ensure healthy digestion.</meta>

...thoroughly excites the desire for eating. Then you shall perform fumigation <note>Fumigation in this context involved inhaling the smoke of burning herbs or resins for medicinal purposes.</note> with fumigations suitable to the season. For this is very beneficial: it opens the blockages of the brain, makes the neck and arms thicker, and makes one healthy and robust; it brightens the face and countenance, delays graying of the hair, and strengthens the senses.

After this, however, you should be anointed with the most excellent and fragrant ointments, suitable to the season in which you find yourself. For in every scent the soul is refreshed; and every scent is its most sweet food. And when the soul has been refreshed and strengthened by that which delighted it, the body will be invigorated, the heart will rejoice, and the blood will begin to course through the veins from the joy of the soul.

After this, you shall take *alatros* <note>original: "alatros"</note>—that is, an electuary <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made of herbal powders mixed with honey or syrup</term> of aloe wood, which is found in the books of physicians—and then *er road* <note>original: "er road"</note>—that is, of rhubarb <term>rhubarb: highly valued in medieval medicine as a digestive aid and "purgative" of bad humors</term>—the weight of four denarii <term>denarius: a small silver coin used here as a standard unit of weight</term>, because this is very beneficial. For it draws away phlegm from the mouth of the stomach, excites the heat of the body, drives away windiness <note>Essentially gas or bloating, which medieval doctors believed was caused by cold or damp humors.</note>, and restores a good taste. 

Then sit with nobles and speak with wise men; do what is fitting to do according to custom. And when the desire to eat arises, near the usual hour, engage in moderate physical labor—namely, either by riding, or by walking, or by doing something else. For this helps the body greatly: it drives away windiness, strengthens and lightens the body, kindles the heat in the stomach, tightens the joints, liquefies the remaining superfluous humors, and makes the phlegm descend from the stomach [along with] burnt food <note>original: "incesum"; referring to food that has been over-digested or "over-cooked" by the stomach's heat</note>, that is, food that is too hot and dried out. 

If many foods are placed before you, eat from those which you have chosen according to your desire, with evenly leavened bread that has been perfectly fermented. And serve first those which you ought to serve first—for example, if a person takes at lunch a soft pottage <term>pottage: a thick soup or stew, usually made of vegetables and grains</term> that softens the belly, and something binding follows it, it will go well. If the soft food comes first, it makes digestion easier; but if he eats the binding food first and the soft food afterward, he digests both poorly. Similarly, if a person takes several soft pottages which can be quickly digested, it is necessary that something binding precedes them into the bottom of the stomach; because the depth of the stomach is hotter and stronger for soft...

...to be noted <note>original: "stendum," likely part of "notandum" or "advertendum"</note> because in that part there are fleshy parts, and it is near the liver <note>original: "epati"</note>, by whose heat foods are "cooked" <note>Medieval physicians believed the liver acted like a furnace that "cooked" or digested food through heat.</note>. And in eating, you must rule your hand—that is, cease from eating while the will or desire to eat still remains. For from an excess of food, the stomach is distressed, the body is weighed down, the spirit is harmed, and the food remains in the bottom of the stomach, heavy and harmful. Similarly, restrain your mind from the drinking of water during a meal until it becomes a habit for you. For the drinking of cold water upon food chills the stomach, extinguishes the heat of digestion, confuses the food, and generates obstructions <note>original: "impedimentum"</note> if much is drunk; nothing could be worse for the body. If, however, it is necessary that water be taken—either because of the heat of the season, the heat of the stomach, or the heat of the foods—let it be a small amount and quite cold. And when you have been refreshed, walk upon soft rugs <note>original: "stramenta mollia"</note>; then sleep moderately and rest for one hour upon your right side; then return to the left, and complete your sleep upon that side, because the left side is cold and therefore needs warming <note>Doctors of this era recommended starting on the right side so food would settle toward the liver (on the right) to begin "cooking," then switching to the left to complete the process.</note>. If, therefore, you feel heaviness in the stomach and twisting pains in the belly, then the medicine is: to place a warm, heavy shirt over the venter <term>venter: the abdomen or belly</term>, or to embrace a warm and beautiful girl <note>This was a traditional, if literal, medieval prescription for "natural heat" transfer to a patient.</note>. If, however, you feel a sour belching, it is a sign of coldness in the stomach. The medicine for this is: to drink warm water with sour syrup <term>sour syrup: likely oxymel, a mixture of honey and vinegar used to cut through phlegm</term> and to vomit. For the trapping of corrupt food in the belly is a powerful destruction of the body. And movement before lunch excites the heat of the stomach, but for a long time after lunch it is harmful, because then the undigested food descends into the lower parts of the stomach, and from there blockages and other evils are generated.

<meta>This page continues the physiological advice given to a ruler (traditionally Alexander the Great), moving from the physical act of eating and the positions for sleeping to the specific timing of rest and the importance of routine in maintaining the "natural heat" necessary for digestion.</meta>

It seems that the Conciliator <note>Referring to Pietro d’Abano (c. 1250–1316), a famous medieval physician and philosopher nicknamed "the Conciliator" for his attempts to reconcile the differences between Greek and Arabic medical traditions.</note>, in the one hundred and twenty-third difference <note>original: "differentia centesima uigesima tertia"</note>, by examining this matter intently, appears to say that the Author’s opinion was insufficient.

### On the effects of different types of sleep.

**Know** that sleeping before the midday meal <term>prandium: the first substantial meal of the day, usually taken at noon</term> makes the body lean and dries out its moisture. However, after a meal <note>original: "post refectionem"</note>, it fills, strengthens, and nourishes the body. For while a person sleeps, the body rests, and then the <term>natural heat: the internal vital energy that medieval doctors believed fueled all bodily processes</term>—which had been spread throughout the whole body—is drawn inward to the stomach. Then the stomach is strengthened for the "cooking" <note>original: "decoctione," referring to the medieval concept that the stomach acted like a pot over a fire, literally "cooking" food into nutrients through internal heat.</note> of food; at that time, the natural power seeks its rest. 

For this reason, certain philosophers have said that the evening meal <note>original: "vespertina comestio"</note> is more beneficial than the midday meal, because the midday meal is affected by the heat of the day. Since the senses are active and the mind is busy with what a person hears, reasons, and thinks about, and because of many disturbances that surround the body due to heat and movement, the natural heat is dispersed through the outer parts of the body during the midday hour. Consequently, the stomach is then greatly weakened and becomes incapable of "cooking" the food. 

In the evening meal, however, the opposite occurs. For then the body experiences tranquility from labor and the heart is at peace; then there is rest for the senses and the soul. Furthermore, the coolness of the night is present, which leads the natural heat back to the interior of the stomach. 

Likewise, you should not be unaware that if someone is accustomed to eating twice a day and changes to eating once, harm will certainly come to him. The same happens to one who is used to eating once a day and begins to eat twice; for his stomach is unable to digest the food, and the nourishment remains undigested. And he who is accustomed to eating at a certain hour and moves his meal to another hour will quickly perceive that it does not benefit his nature, but rather greatly harms it, because custom is a second natu- <note>The text likely ends with "natura" (nature), referencing the ancient proverb that "habit is second nature."</note>.

<summary>This page explains the medieval medical theory of "natural heat" in digestion, arguing that evening meals and post-meal rest are superior because they allow internal heat to focus on the stomach rather than being dispersed by daytime activity.</summary>
<keywords>Pietro d'Abano, natural heat, digestion, midday meal vs. evening meal, medical regimen, habit and custom, medieval physiology</keywords>

...fū. <note>This likely completes a word from the previous page, such as "confusio" (confusion/disturbance).</note> If, therefore, some necessity—which knows no law <note>original: "necessitas: que legem non habet"</note>—compels you to do that which custom refuses, this should be done discreetly and wisely; namely, so that the change of custom happens gradually, one step after another, and thus, with God's help, it will go well. And take care not to eat again until you know the stomach to be completely empty and purged of the previous food; and you will know this by desire—that is, when you begin to have a will to eat—and by the thinness of the saliva running into the mouth. For if anyone takes food without bodily necessity—that is, with a reluctance to eat—he will find the <term>natural heat: the internal vital energy believed to fuel digestion</term> to be very cold. But if he takes food when he has desired it, then he will find the heat like a lit fire. And when you begin to have the desire to eat, you ought to eat immediately; because unless you eat immediately, the stomach will be filled with the worst <term>humors: the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that medieval doctors believed governed health</term>, which it draws to itself from the body's wastes. These will disturb the brain with a foul vapor <note>Medieval physicians believed that "fumes" from bad digestion could rise to the head, causing mental confusion or illness.</note>. And when food is later added, it remains lukewarm and does not benefit the body.

### On the four seasons of the year. On spring.

It is our intention in this book to briefly determine: the four seasons of the year, the quality and property of each season, and the variation of the same. There are four seasons of the year, which are distinguished as follows. **Spring** <note>original: "Ver"</note> begins then, when the sun enters the sign of **Aries** <note>The Ram; the first sign of the zodiac.</note>; for then is the beginning of spring, and it lasts for ninety-two days and one and a half hours: and this is from the tenth day from the end of March <note>original: "decima die exeunte martio," referring to the spring equinox around March 21st.</note> until the tenth day of June. In this time, day and night are made equal. In these regions, the season grows sweet and the air grows mild; the winds blow, the snows melt, and torrents run between the mountains; springs bubble up, and moisture is raised to the tops of trees and the tips of the branches; seeds rise, crops grow, meadows turn green, and flowers sprout and are decorated; trees are clothed in new leaves, the earth is adorned with grasses, and [living things] are generated...

<term>complexion: the specific balance of the four humors in an individual</term>—that is, temperate: and such things should be eaten in it: such 
as chicken pullets: and eggs—not in excess, namely up to 
six—and fresh: and wild lettuce: which the peasants call "scaria" 
and goat’s milk: for there is no time better for 
<term>bloodletting: the surgical removal of blood to restore humoral balance</term>: and in this season, the use and movement of the limbs is profitable: 
the loosening of the belly, the use of baths: and sweating: and spiced drinks 
for digestion: namely, purgatives should be taken: whatever 
indeed might happen through digestion, through purging, or through bloodletting 
<note>The OCR reads "munitionem," likely a typo for "minutionem" or bloodletting.</note> or the discharge of the body: this season, with its own humidity, 
restores it.

### On summer.

Summer begins then when the sun enters the first point 
of Cancer and it lasts ninety-two days and an hour and a half: 
and this is from the tenth day of June until the tenth day of 
September. In this time the days are lengthened: and the 
nights are shortened: in all regions the heat grows: the 
winds are stirred: there is tranquility on the sea: serenity in the air: the 
crops are dried: serpents are born: poisons are poured out: the 
virtues of bodies are strengthened and the world becomes like a bride 
with a full body, at a perfect age: inflamed with heat: the 
summer time is hot and dry: in which the <term>red choler: yellow bile, the humor associated with fire and the summer season</term> 
is stirred. And in this time one must beware: of that which is of a very 
hot and dry nature or complexion. For it stirs up the red 
choler. One must also abstain from excessive eating and drinking in 
summer: lest the natural heat be extinguished. You should eat in it restfully...

whatever is of a cold and moist <term>complexion: the specific balance of the four humors in an individual</term>: such as veal with vinegar, and gourds, and fattened chickens; likewise porridge made from barley flour, and fruit of a sharp flavor, and sour apples. Sexual activity <note>original: "venus"</note> should be sought sparingly. You should abstain from all bloodletting <note>original: "minutione"</note> unless it is absolutely necessary for the organs of the body; likewise, be moderate in the movement of the body. Baths should be taken sparingly.

## On autumn.

Autumn begins then when the sun enters the first degree of Libra: and it lasts ninety-one days and an hour and a half: that is, from the tenth of September until the tenth of December. In this time, the days and nights are made equal for the second time; in this time also, the night grows and takes from the day. The air becomes cold; the northern winds blow; the seasons change. The rivers decrease, the springs are diminished, the gardens wither, and the fruits fail. The face of the earth lacks its beauty; birds <note>original: "volatilia"</note> seek warm regions; animals seek out their caves; serpents their pits, where they gather food for the winter. And the world is compared to a woman of full age who is in need of clothes, as if youth has departed from her and old age hastens on. Autumn is a cold and dry season, in which <term>black bile: one of the four humors associated with melancholy and the earth element</term> <note>original: "colera nigra"</note> rises up. It is necessary that in this time, warm and moist things should be consumed, such as chickens and lambs, old wine, and sweet grapes, while abstaining from all things that generate black bile. Movement of the body and the practice of sex <note>original: "vsus veneris"</note> hurt more now than in the summer. Baths and purgatives, if they should be necessary, should be taken in this season; and if a person needs to vomit, let it be done at midday <note>original: "meridie"</note> and in the warmer hour of the day, because in such hours the <term>superfluities: excess fluids or waste products within the body</term> are generated in man. The purging of the belly should be done in this time through <note>These likely refer to specific medieval medicinal preparations or formulas.</note> astraton and agaton, and through all things which draw out black bile and repress the <term>humors: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to govern health</term>.

## On winter.

<summary>This page provides health advice for the transition from Summer into Autumn, focusing on the dominance of black bile and the necessary dietary adjustments to counter the cold, dry qualities of the season.</summary>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general dietary advice for Autumn into the specifics of the Winter season. It concludes the overarching seasonal discourse of the "Secretum Secretorum," shifting into a direct address to Alexander regarding the preservation of "natural heat" and the two types of human decline.</meta>

Winter then begins when the sun enters the first sign of Capricorn: and it contains ninety days and an hour and a half: from the tenth of December until the tenth of March. In this time the days are shortened and the nights are lengthened: the cold grows strong: the winds become harsh: the leaves fall from the trees: for the most part green plants die and are hardened as if into stone: and the majority of animals hide themselves in the belly of the earth and in the caverns of the mountains: and on account of excessive cold and humidity the air is darkened: the season grows black: the flocks and beasts of burden tremble: the powers of the bodies are weakened: and the world is like a weary old woman: decrepit with age: naked of clothing: near to death.

Winter is cold and moist: in which it is necessary for the chosen lifestyle—that is, to return to warm medicines and to warm foods: such as young chickens, hens, and the meat of rams, geese, and roasted meats, and all warm spices <note>original: "pigmenta"</note>. Also figs and nuts and the best red wine: and let warm electuaries <term>electuaries: medicinal pastes mixed with honey or syrup</term> be consumed. One should also abstain from purging the bowels and from bloodletting <note>original: "minutione sanguinis"</note>: use neither an abundance of the belly nor of food, lest the digestion be weakened:

<margin>
bath: in Greek *balaneion*, otherwise... <note>The marginalia provides etymological notes for the word "bath" in Italian and Greek.</note>
</margin>

anoint your body with warm ointments and use baths: excessive drink and sexual activity <note>original: "venus"</note> do not harm at this time, nor does excessive eating. For at this time, because of the great cold, the natural heat <term>natural heat: the innate bodily warmth believed in medieval medicine to drive digestion and vitality</term> is gathered together and enters into the inner parts of the body: therefore digestion is better in winter. In spring and summer, however, the belly is cold: because in these seasons the pores are opened by the heat: and the natural heat is spread through all parts of the body: and because of the scarcity of heat in the stomach, digestion is hindered and the humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health</term> are stirred up. Know these things, therefore, and may the Lord preserve you: farewell, Alexander: keep this precious regimen <note>original: "dietam"</note> provided to you by preserving your natural heat: for as long as a tempered heat persists in a person, health is maintained for a long time: for a person grows old and fails in two ways: one, that is, the natural way, which happens by the debt of nature, namely from old age: which conquers the body and destroys nature; or the accidental way, namely from illnesses and other most wretched causes.

<meta>This page continues the health advice given to Alexander the Great in the "Secretum Secretorum." Following the seasonal instructions for winter, the text now transitions into specific lifestyle habits—both physical and emotional—that influence body weight and "moisture," a key concept in medieval Galenic medicine.</meta>

# On those things that fatten the body.

These things fatten the body and provide it with moisture: rest and the satiety that comes from eating sweet and diverse foods; the drinking of warm, sweet milk and sweet wine; and sleeping after a meal upon soft bedding in places and at times that are appropriate and refreshed with fragrances. Also, entering baths of sweet waters <note>Fresh water as opposed to mineral or salt water.</note> and staying there only a short time, lest the body be weakened by the long duration of the bath. One should also smell fragrant odors, or whatever scent is pleasant, each in its proper season: as in winter, *alchibimum* <note>original: "alchibimū"; likely a reference to *alcibiadium*, a type of alkanet or bugloss used for its "warm" properties</note> which is a species of a certain flower and of a warm nature. In the summer, one should use roses and violets and whatever is cooling. Furthermore, vomiting should be practiced at least once every month, and especially in the summer. For vomiting washes the body and purges the stomach of the worst and most putrid humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that medieval doctors believed must be kept in balance for health</term>; and if there are few bad humors in the stomach, the natural heat for digesting will be strengthened. The body will be filled with moisture and healthy fat, and it will be much more beneficial if, along with this regimen, one happens to have joy, gladness, reason, glory, honor, and victory over enemies; hope and trust in one's people; and to delight in visions, to look upon beautiful faces, to hear delightful books, to be clothed in the best garments dyed in various colors, and to be anointed with ointments at appropriate times.

### On those things that thin the body

Conversely, these things make the body thin, and weaken it, and dry it out: eating and drinking very little; laboring much; going frequently into the sun; walking beyond measure; sleeping before the midday meal upon hard bedding; worrying the mind; being afraid; entering sulfurous water baths; eating salty foods <note>original: "falsos"; likely a scribal error for *salsos* (salty), as salty foods were traditionally thought to "dry out" the humors</note>; drinking much old wine; also frequent bowel movements; and bloodletting <note>original: "sanguinem minuere"</note>, especially exceeding the proper measure in doing so; and having the most wretched thoughts and sorrows. 

The Rule of Hippocrates

Wine and milk work in similar ways <note>This reflects a common medieval medical belief that mixing certain foods—especially milk and fish—caused a corruption of the humors leading to skin diseases like leprosy.</note>.

### Annotation.

The things that make the body thin <note>original: "Macrefacientia"</note>, which are declared in this chapter, and those which make it fat, which were explained in the preceding chapter, are found to be superfluously repeated a little further below in the designated chapters.

## On the division of the body. On the head.

The body is divided into four parts: the first is the head. Therefore, when superfluities <term>superfluities: excess or waste humors that have not been properly digested or expelled, thought to cause disease by "clogging" the body</term> accumulate in it, you will be able to know by these signs: dimness of the eyes, heaviness of the eyebrows, throbbing of the temples, ringing in the ears, and blockage of the nostrils. If anyone, therefore, feels this happening to himself: let him take perforated herb <note>original: "herbam perforatam"; likely St. John's Wort (*Hypericum perforatum*), named for the tiny holes or "perforations" in its leaves</note> and boil it in sweet wine with roots of pennyroyal <note>original: "pulegij"; a medicinal plant in the mint family often used to clear the head</note> until it is reduced by half. Let him hold this in his mouth every morning until he feels it doing him good. He should also use mustard seeds <note>original: "grano sinapis"</note> in his food, of which you should boil the weight of one drachm <term>drachm: an ancient unit of weight; in a medical context, roughly 3.9 grams</term> with a powder made of [unclear]... and ointments, and use this when he goes to sleep. If, however, he omits this and is negligent: he may fear dangerous infirmities, namely the corruption of his sight, pain in the brain, and many other sicknesses from which may the Lord protect you at all times.

---

## On the chest.

The chest is the second part of the body: if therefore superfluities—

...[superfluities] are gathered in it: these signs follow: the tongue may be heavy; the mouth tastes salty; at the orifice <note>likely the "orifice" or opening of the stomach</note> one feels bitter food; and there is the pain of a cough. Therefore, one must refrain from eating and use vomiting <note>In medieval medicine, therapeutic vomiting was a standard way to purge excess humors from the upper body.</note>. After vomiting, take sugar of roses <note>original: "zucharum rosarum"; a common medicinal preserve made of rose petals and sugar</note> with wood of aloes and chew it. After eating, take a portion the size of a nut of the **electuary enison**, which is made from wood of aloes and **causergam** <note>original: "causergam"; likely a transliteration of an Arabic medicinal ingredient, possibly sarcocolla</note>. Whoever does not do this can easily incur pain in the side or kidneys or many other illnesses. And the drinking of a decoction of hyssop is wonderful, and this should be done on an empty stomach.

## On the eyes.

The eyes are the third part of the body. He who labors to preserve the health of the eyes must protect them from dust, smoke, and things departing from balance <note>original: "ab aequalitate"; referring to the ideal temperate state of the four humors</note>, namely in heat and cold, and from evil winds. He should not stare steadily at a single thing without looking away, nor look much at unstable things. He should avoid excessive crying, excessive sexual intercourse, and an excessive fullness from drink and food—especially those drinks and foods that send a thick vapor to the head, such as leeks, cabbages, beer, beans, and the like. Nor should he sleep upon a full stomach.

The things that benefit the eyes are remedies <note>original: "spes," likely a shorthand or typo for *species*, meaning medicinal ingredients</note>, namely **echie** <note>viper's bugloss (*Echium*) was commonly used in eye salves</note>, and the waters or juices of fennel, **rhubarb** <note>original: "rei bene"; likely a corruption of *reubarbarum*</note>, roses, celandine, and rue; also the submerging of the eyes in clear water, opening the eyes within it, and looking out through it.

The signs of the causes of the condition fitting for the eyes are known by eight rules <term>rules: original "canonibus"; standard diagnostic principles or "canons" of medicine</term>. From touch; from its veins; from its shape and its own operations; from color; from clarity; from its sufferings <note>ailments or symptoms</note>; from what comes out of it; and from external things occurring according to heat and cold. Regarding the veins, if they are visible and full of humors, it is a cause of certainty [of diagnosis]; and illness is a cause which is known through heat and through pain: as if we [find] stinging blood, or a choleric, distensive, phlegma-

<meta>This page continues the diagnostic and prognostic discussion from the previous page, moving from general "superfluities" to specific observations of the eyes and the foundational principles of a healthy lifestyle (the six non-naturals).</meta>

...[the things that benefit the]... [phthi]sic <note>The word fragment "ticum" likely refers to a phthisic (consumptive) or arthritic condition, continuing from the lost sentence of the previous page.</note> you may feel a melancholic heaviness that moves about, as well as through windiness and other signs. 

And [the eye is judged] by its shape, as it ought to be round and full to the view in its appearance. And by its function: for eyes that move quickly are judged to be "hot," while those that move slowly are "cold," and those prone to tearing are "moist." Likewise, some see distant things from afar but not close up. Others, however, are the opposite: for those seeing only from nearby possess a **visual spirit** <note>original: "spiritum visibilem"; according to medieval physiology, vision occurred through "spirits" or vapors emitted from the eye to perceive objects</note> that is small and clear; because of its smallness, it does not suffice to extend to a distant object. Those who only see from a distance have a great amount of spirit, but it is not refined; rather, it is disturbed, thick, and moist. Therefore, they require a distance for their "passion" <note>original: "passionem"; here meaning the act of receiving a visual impression or image</note> so that the thick vapors may be separated from the spirit.

And [judged] by color, since color signifies the dominant humor <term>humor: the four vital fluids—blood, yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm—that were believed to determine a person's health and temperament</term>. Specifically: red signifies blood; yellow <note>original: "citrinus"</note> signifies yellow bile <note>original: "coleram"</note>; white signifies phlegm; and leaden-grey signifies melancholy <note>or black bile</note>. 

And [judged] by quantity: for a large size of the eyes, when proportional to their function and power, signifies an abundance of substance and a good **eucrasia** <term>eucrasia: a healthy balance or "good mixture" of the humors</term> of the eye. If, however, the size is large without good function, it demonstrates much substance but not a balanced one. When accompanied by faulty function, it shows small substance and a lack of balance. From the quantity [as seen] in behaviors: he whose eyes are large is lazy. He whose eyes are small is shameless, talkative, and dull-witted. He whose eyes are set deep in the skull is crafty and a deceiver. 

Diagnosis is also known from external things through the patient's own report. The cure for any of these conditions is achieved by applying the opposite of the illness <note>The medical principle of "contraries are cured by contraries"—treating a "hot" disease with "cold" medicine, or a "moist" one with "dry" remedies.</note>. Along with this, a good regimen should be observed regarding the **six non-natural things** <term>non-naturals: factors external to the body that affect health, including air, exercise and rest, food and drink, sleep and wakefulness, evacuation, and emotions</term>: air; exercise and rest; food and drink; sleep and wakefulness; sexual intercourse <note>original: "conu," an abbreviation for coitus</note>; and the "accidents of the soul" <note>original: "accidentibus anime"; refers to emotions or psychological states like anger, joy, or fear</note>. One should always keep the bowels loose, both in every illness and in cases of **repletion** <note>original: "repletione"; a state of being over-full or congested with food or humors</note>.
