# Concerning the third part of the body.

When superfluities <term>superfluities: in medieval medicine, these are excess or waste humors that the body has failed to properly digest or expel, leading to disease</term> have gathered in the belly, these are the signs: pain in the knees, bloating, stiffness, and circulating windiness <note>original: "uentositates"; refers to gas or flatulence moving through the digestive tract</note>. Therefore, one who feels these things ought to be purged with certain subtle softeners <note>original: "mollificatiuis"; likely referring to mild laxatives or emollient medicines</note>: and should use the regimen which we described above regarding the chest. Whoever does not do this will incur pain in the joints and the back, a flux of the belly <note>original: "fluxum uentris"; diarrhea</note>, corruption of the digestion, and obstruction of the liver.

### Concerning the testicles.

The testicles are the fourth part of the body: thus, when superfluity gathers in them, this sign follows: the appetite <note>original: "appetitus"; here referring to sexual desire or libido</note> cools. It is necessary for the one who feels this to do the following: take the herb called chamomile and the herb called melilot <note>original: "melilotum"; also known as sweet clover</note>, and from their roots; let him place the roots and herbs in fragrant white wine, and drink from it every morning, provided that it is tempered with water and honey; and let him abstain from excessive eating. For if he neglects this medicine, he may fear pain of the testicles and the lungs, and the danger of the stone <term>the stone: a common historical term for kidney or bladder stones (calculi)</term>. 

Indeed, it is read in the histories of the ancients that a powerful king gathered the finest physicians of the Indians, the Greeks, and the Abedons <note>original: "Abedonũ"; likely referring to Abyssinians (Ethiopians) or perhaps a specific medical sect</note> and enjoined them that each of them should strive to create a single medicine of such kind that, if a man uses it, it would benefit him so much that he would not require any other medicine. 

The Indian, for his part, indicated and said: that if one takes two mouthfuls of water every morning, it makes a man healthy and he will not need another medicine. The Abedon, however, affirmed: that on an empty stomach it is very beneficial to take grains of millet. 

But I, Aristotle the Greek <note>The text attributes this to Aristotle, though this material likely originates from the "Secretum Secretorum," a popular medieval book of advice falsely attributed to the philosopher</note>, say: he who sleeps only so much that he does not have a feeling of heaviness in his belly, let him not fear gout nor heaviness; he who eats seven drachms <term>drachm: an ancient unit of weight; seven drachms is roughly 25–30 grams</term> of good, sweet raisins <note>original: "uuæ passę"</note> every morning, will not fear in any way regarding infir- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword indicates "infirmitate," meaning "infirmity" or "illness"</note>

...infirmities of phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four humors, associated with cold and moisture; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness or respiratory issues</term>. And through this, his memory is improved, his intellect is illuminated, and whoever uses this at a time suitable to his complexion <term>complexion: the unique balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within an individual</term> will be safe and will not fear a quartan fever <note>original: "febrem quartanam"; a fever that recurs every fourth day, historically associated with an excess of black bile</note>. And whoever eats figs with nuts and a few leaves of rue—poison will not harm him on that day.

### Concerning the preservation of natural heat.

O most high King, you should strive by all means to guard and retain natural heat <term>natural heat: the vital warmth believed to be innate to the body from birth, which fuels digestion and life itself</term>. As long as warmth and natural moisture are tempered in a person, that heat is strengthened, because health consists in these two things. It should also be known in this place that the destruction and corruption of the body come from two causes: one is natural and the other is against nature. The natural cause comes from the resistance and contradiction of contrary qualities—specifically when dryness dominates the body <note>In medieval medicine, aging was viewed as a process of the body slowly drying out and cooling down</note>. Corruption against nature, however, comes from some accidental cause: whether from war, or from being struck by stones, or from other unfortunate accidents, or from sickness and bad advice.

### Concerning the qualities of foods.

Of foods, some are subtle <note>original: "subtilia"; light or easily digested</note>, some are gross <note>original: "grossa"; heavy, thick, or hard to digest</note>, and some are in the middle. Subtle foods generate subtle blood—clear and good—such as pottage <note>original: "pulmentum"; a thick soup or grain-based porridge</note>, well-nourished chickens, and fresh soft-boiled eggs. Gross foods, however, are good for hot-natured men, those working while fasting, and those who sleep after dinner. Middle foods generate neither inflammation nor superfluity <term>superfluity: excess waste or "soot" produced by the body when it cannot fully digest food</term>; such foods include the meat of lambs, rams, and wethers <note>original: "castratine"; the meat of a castrated ram, which was considered more balanced and less "hot" than an intact ram</note>, and all meats that are warm and moist. However, it seems to be deceptive regarding these meats when they are roasted, because they then acquire hardness, heat, and dryness. If, therefore, such meats are roasted, let them be eaten quickly, and then they are more [beneficial]...

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the dietary and environmental advice following the discussion of natural heat and bodily corruption. It moves from general categories of meat to specific advice on fish and water quality, likely part of a Regimen Sanitatis (a guide to health).</meta>

...[meats] are praiseworthy when sweet spices are added to them. Some foods, however, generate melancholy <term>melancholy: black bile, one of the four humors; an excess was thought to lead to sadness, heaviness, and digestive issues</term>, such as buffalo and beef, and all meats that are coarse, dry, and rough. Other animals, however, have delicate flesh: those that graze and are nourished in watery, damp, and shaded places; their meats are better and healthier. The same should be said of fish.

## Concerning fish.

It should be known, therefore, that the fish which is of small substance, thin skin, and easy to chew—those nourished in running brackish <note>original: "salmaticis"; salty or marshy</note> waters—is better and lighter than those born in the sea or fresh water. However, that which is born in the sea is healthier than that which is born in fresh water. One must therefore avoid those fish that are of large substance and tough skin, because such are often poisonous. And let these remarks about fish suffice for now, because in the book I composed concerning pottages <term>pottage: a thick soup or stew, often the staple of medieval diets</term> and medicines, you will find this matter determined abundantly.

## Concerning waters.

It should be known that light waters are useful to every living thing, both animals and plants. And remember that I have taught you sufficiently about waters, and showed you that all waters, both sweet and bitter, draw their origin from the sea, and of this I made a clear demonstration to you. Now it should be understood that the lightest and healthiest waters are those that run near cities. When the earth is pure from its banks and not abounding in fumes <term>fumes: vapors or "smoky" exhalations believed to rise from the earth and affect the air and water quality</term>, the water of that place is light, excellent, and praiseworthy. But water originating in stony ground that abounds in fumes is heavy, sickly, and harmful. And that in which there are frogs and serpents and other poisonous animals is in-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the quality of water, transitioning from the previous page's description of healthy running water to the dangers of stagnant water and proper drinking habits. ...solid, just like marsh water. Indeed, these are the signs of good waters: lightness, clarity, and a good color; also when they heat up easily and cool down easily. For nature delights in such things. But waters that are salty, bitter, and smoky are so called because they loosen the belly and dry it out. Marsh waters, however, are warm and heavy because they are stagnant and do not move. And because the sun remains over them for a long time, they generate yellow bile <term>yellow bile: also known as "cholera," one of the four humors; associated with a hot and dry temperament and digestive fire</term> and cause the spleen and lungs to enlarge. Those waters that bubble up for a long time in two types of earth are warm and weak because they contain earthy particles. 

Furthermore, drinking cold water on an empty stomach before a meal harms the body, for it extinguishes the "fire" of the stomach <note>The medieval "fire of the stomach" refers to the heat required for digestion; cold water was thought to douse this necessary metabolic heat</note>. However, drinking it after a meal warms the body and generates phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four humors; associated with cold and moist qualities and the respiratory system</term>. If too much is taken, it spoils the food within the stomach. You ought, therefore, to drink cold water in the summer and warm water in the winter, and not the reverse. This is because drinking heated water in the summer softens and weakens the stomach and destroys the appetite; just as drinking cold water in the winter extinguishes the internal heat, destroys the organs of the chest, harms the lungs, and generates many other injuries.

## Concerning wine:

Regarding the knowledge of wine: it should be known that wine whose grape cluster <note>original: "racemus"</note> was born on a mountain exposed to the sun is of a drier nature than that which is born on a plain in damp and shaded places. The former wine is beneficial for the elderly and for people who have an abundance of moisture and phlegm; however, it harms the young and those with hot temperaments. This first type of wine therefore warms the body and frees it from cold and thick superfluities <note>original: "superfluitatibus"; excess humors or metabolic waste that the body needs to expel</note>. 

Furthermore, the redder and thicker a wine is, the more it generates blood <term>blood: the primary humor, associated with the "sanguine" temperament; red wine was thought to transform directly into bodily blood</term>. But when it is very strong and of a potent bitterness, then it is called "pure blood" and primary nourishment, and it possesses the nature of a potion and a medicine; yet if taken for a long time, it harms greatly. When, however, wine of this type is sweet, it harms the stomach and generates windiness <note>original: "uentositatem"; gas or flatulence</note> and bloating. More praiseworthy and pleasant than any other wine, in regards to all co- <note>The text ends mid-word on a catchword; likely "colors" or "constitutions"</note>

<summary>This page provides health advice on water and wine, warning against stagnant water and extreme temperatures, while categorizing wine based on its origin and medicinal effect on the four humors.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of health and diet, moving from the properties of water to the specific characteristics of high-quality wine. It details the benefits of moderate consumption for digestion and mood, before pivoting to a stern warning about the physical and mental dangers of excessive drinking.</meta>

...of the more excellent [wines] <note>original: "pleriones," likely a corruption of "potiones" (drinks) or "meliores" (better ones)</note>: it is that which is produced in vast lands between mountains and valleys, whose grape cluster is of good sweetness, of perfect maturity, and of a subtle air <note>meaning a light or refined quality</note>. It is not harvested until the strength of its own substance is within it, the skin is moist, and the shoots and seeds are tasteless <note>suggesting the sugars have fully moved from the vine into the fruit</note>. Its color is golden—that is, midway between lemon-yellow and red—and its flavor is sharp and delightful. Its dregs are pressed to the bottom, while its finer parts are clear and bright. 

Therefore, when such wine is found, take of it moderately according to the age of your body and the quality of the season. For it strengthens the stomach, reinforces the natural heat <term>natural heat: the internal "fire" believed by medieval doctors to drive digestion and life force</term>, aids digestion, and preserves the body from corruption. It carries the food, digests it, and brings it—once purified—to all the limbs that are governed by it. It continues to digest the food within those same limbs until it is converted into substantial blood. Then, it ascends to the neck with a tempered heat; it makes the head secure from accidental falls <note>likely referring to dizziness, vertigo, or strokes</note>; furthermore, it gladdens the heart and makes the complexion <gloss>color</gloss> ruddy. It makes the tongue nimble, frees the mind from cares, makes a man bold, and excites the appetite for all things, doing many other good things besides.

## On the evils which follow from excessive drinking.

Wine, however, when it is taken in great quantity and in excess, produces the following evils: it obscures the intellect, hinders the senses, and disturbs the brain. It weakens the natural and animal virtues <term>natural and animal virtues: the "natural virtue" governed metabolic functions like growth, while the "animal virtue" governed the nervous system and senses</term>. It generates forgetfulness and harms all five senses by which the entire operation of the body is governed and directed. It drives away the appetite, weakens the "chains" and joints of the body, and generates trembling of the limbs and bleariness of the eyes. It ignites the choler <term>choler: also known as yellow bile; one of the four humors, associated with anger and heat</term> and destroys the liver because it makes its blood thicker and darkens the blood of the heart. From this comes horror, fear, trembling, talking in one's sleep, and fantastical visions. It leads to the corruption of the complexion, the weakening of the genitals, the destruction of the seed <note>referring to fertility</note>, and a loathing in the stomach; it throws the balance of the humors <gloss>complexion</gloss> into disorder.

...it generates a heaviness of the body; and what is worse, it induces leprosy <gloss>leprosy</gloss> <note>original: "lepra"; in medieval medicine, this often referred to a wide range of severe skin conditions believed to be caused by internal corruption</note>, and at that point, it belongs to the category of poisons. Care must be taken, therefore, that it is not consumed beyond measure, because wine changes one’s nature and constitution <note>original: "complexionem," referring to the balance of the four humors</note>. Consider rhubarb <term>rhubarb: highly valued in medieval medicine as a "life-giving" purgative for the liver</term>, which is the life of the liver <gloss>liver</gloss> and has many benefits described in medical books; nevertheless, it is sometimes a deadly poison, specifically for those who take it beyond measure and exceed a certain quantity and limit. Wine is also compared to the nature of serpents, from which an antidote is made and through whose medicine great injuries are expelled; yet, it is known to all that they carry deadly poisons within themselves.

### Concerning oxymel and the best remedy for drunkenness.

O Alexander, never let it weary you to take oxymel <term>oxymel: a medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar, used to "cut" through thick phlegm</term> in the morning on an empty stomach, or even when not fasting—specifically when the bodily humors are strengthened and abundant, and phlegm dominates. For it is very healing. Indeed, the wise Aristotle <note>Aristoteles</note> commended good wine when he said: it is a wonder how a man can fall ill or die if his food consists of bread from the best grain, commendable meats, and the drink of the good vine. This is true if he uses them temperately, abstaining from excessive eating, drinking, sexual intercourse <gloss>coitus</gloss>, and labor. How could sickness happen to a man under such conditions? Indeed, it is necessary for one who is intoxicated by wine taken beyond measure to wash himself with warm water and sit by the banks of running streams. He should have willows and myrtle nearby, and he ought to anoint his body with a sandalwood preparation and fumigate himself with cool and fragrant incense. This is indeed the best medicine for drunkenness.

If anyone proposes to give up the drinking of wine entirely, they must not...

...[one should for]bear from suddenly abstaining entirely from the use of wine; but instead, one should gradually transition from the drinking of wine to the drinking of raisin-wine <note>original: "uue passe" or *passum*, a sweet wine made from dried grapes</note>. And from that point, add water little by little, one day after another, until one arrives at pure water; because through this gradual order, the natural constitution <note>original: "complexio nature," referring to the balance of the four humors</note> is preserved from serious future illnesses.

## Concerning those things which strengthen, fatten, and moisten the body.

After these things, it must therefore be known: that some of these things strengthen, fatten, and moisten; some emaciate and dry out; some things give the body vigor and beauty; and some generate laziness and lukewarmness <note>original: "tepiditatem," a lack of vital energy or "animal heat"</note>. Indeed, those things which strengthen the body are pleasant, light foods that are suited to its nature, when they are consumed at a proper time and in the hour of necessity, as has been said before. Truly, those things which fatten and moisten the body are these: Rest of the body, joy of the mind, pleasant company, and warm and moist foods; the drinking of sweet wine, and the consumption of liquid honey that is gathered and placed in prepared spots within the hollows of an oak tree <note>This likely refers to a specific medicinal preparation of wild honey stored in oak to imbue it with certain properties</note>. And nothing is so effective for this purpose as sleeping after lunch upon sweet grasses in cool places, and bathing in hot baths <gloss>baths</gloss>, staying in them only a short time, lest the body’s moisture be dissolved in too great a quantity. For it is better for the body to receive moisture and wetting from the bath than the reverse; and to smell scents that gladden the soul and are suited to the current season. For example: smelling a cluster of grapes in winter, because it is of a warm nature; but roses and violets in summer. One should practice vomiting <note>In medieval medicine, therapeutic vomiting was a common "cleansing" technique used to prevent the buildup of undigested matter</note> twice or three times a month, especially in summer, because vomiting washes the stomach and purges it of bad humors and corrupt moistures. Therefore, when these corrupt materials are expelled from the stomach, the natural heat is increased and strengthened for digesting food. Govern your body well, therefore...

[...places] for your body, if you wish for it to grow: follow my advice. These indeed are the things by which men are strengthened <note>original: "impinguntur," likely a variant of *impinguantur*, meaning to be fattened or made robust</note>.

## Concerning those things which make the body lean.

The things which do the opposite—namely, those which emaciate the body—are these: the reduction of food and drink; frequent exercise and labor, especially in the heat and the sun; long periods of wakefulness; sleeping before lunch upon hard beds (because the natural heat is hindered on account of the excessive humidity that is in the body); bathing in sulfurous and salty waters, or in waters that are diluted, cold, or extremely hot; hunger; the drinking of old, pure wine; frequent purging of the bowels; frequent bloodletting <note>original: "diminutio sanguinis," the common medical practice of phlebotomy used to balance the humors</note>; excessive sexual activity <note>original: "nimius vsus veneris," literally "excessive use of Venus"</note>; poverty; worry; fear; bad thoughts; anxiety; frequent pains; and unfortunate events.

## Concerning the bath.

The bath is truly one of the wonders of this world, because it is constructed according to the four seasons of the year. For cold is attributed to winter; lukewarm to spring; hot to summer; and dry to autumn. Therefore, it is a matter of the highest foresight to arrange four chambers <note>original: "mansiones," referring to the specific rooms of a bathhouse, modeled on the Roman frigidarium, tepidarium, etc.</note> in the baths in an orderly fashion: so that the first is cold, the second lukewarm, the third dry, and the fourth hot. And when anyone wishes to enter, they must first stand for a little while in the first; then enter the other and delay there a little; and afterward enter the third...

And when you wish to leave, you must observe the same order, making a little delay in each of the chambers: nor should you go from excessive heat into excessive cold, or from excessive cold to excessive heat immediately; but rather, gradually <note>Medieval medicine emphasized "graduated" transitions to avoid shocking the humors of the body</note>. And let the bath be built in a high and windy place: and let it have large furnaces producing great and broad flames, and fresh water. And suitable scents must be used in it, according to the current season. Then, you should sit upon seats soaked in rose water, and afterwards have yourself wiped with a clean white linen cloth, one time after another.

Then leave gradually, just as we taught in the preceding instructions. Then wipe yourself until you are relieved.

Then, after a short hour, take prepared food with tranquility, and drink good wine moderately, with as much water as you are accustomed to drinking, and do not take much, but take it moderately. Then you shall perfume yourself with incenses suitable for that season. Then rest in delicate beds and take a good portion of sleep, because it helps much. Then you must continue the rest of the day in joy and quiet. This, indeed, is the order of health and of the nourishment of the bod[y...]

However, he who is old or is overcome by moisture and coldness <note>In the humoral system, old age was considered naturally "cold and dry," but an excess of "cold and moist" phlegm was seen as a danger to the elderly.</note> should be very careful about staying too long in the bath. ## Information on why there is no need for a physician.

O Alexander, when you have understood the given instruction and fulfilled it in practice, it will make you have no need of a physician during the time of your life, with the help of God. For you know that serious illnesses which are generated from heat, and from the periods and course of the Moon, are known as to whether they be short or long; and also from preceding signs, it is known to what end they must come. And I have taught you faithfully and shown briefly the divisions and recognitions of illnesses; and in the water <note>original: "in aqua"; referring to uroscopy, the medical examination of urine.</note> there is likewise a proven sign in these matters. But the preceding signs are truer and better, just as I determined in the books concerning waters. And these signs are sufficient for him who holds well in memory the teaching of that book; as is also contained in the book which I made concerning compound medicines, and artificial potions, and compound ointments and plasters according to the order and art of the Greeks, Italians, Indians, and Persians, in which no experiment was deceptive. Because, therefore, these useful secrets were hidden and buried, and were neither known nor found anywhere, I decided it would be unworthy if they remained hidden from your Clemency.

## Concerning the discovery and division of the Great Medicine.

It is worthy, therefore, that you should know the Great Medicine, which is useful...

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion on the origins of ancient wisdom to a specific recipe for a medicinal honey. The text references the 'Margarita Pretiosa Novella' by Petrus Bonus, a 14th-century alchemist, to debate the nature of the "Philosophers' Treasure."</meta>

This inestimable glory, which is truly the <term>Philosophers' Treasure<note>original: *thesaurus philosophorum*</note></term>: indeed, I have never perceived nor truly known who discovered it. For some assert that Adam was its inventor. Others say it was Aesculapius and Hermogenes the physicians; Iris, Donasticos, Ulacileos, the Hebrews, Dionysius, and Zarai—the most glorious philosophers who have lived. These are the ones to whom it was given to know the secrets of the sciences, which lay hidden from all other men. These are they who investigated and debated concerning things that are above nature: concerning the plenum and the vacuum <note>The concept of "the full and the empty" in natural philosophy</note>, and concerning the finite and the infinite. They came to a harmonious agreement regarding the preparation of this inestimable medicine, which they divided into eight parts. Some indeed confirm that Enoch knew this secret through a divine vision; they even wish to say that this Enoch was the great <term>Hermogenes<note>The author uses 'Hermogenes' to refer to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus, the "thrice-great" founder of alchemy and hermetic philosophy.</note></term>, whom the Greeks greatly commend and praise, attributing to him all wisdom of secrets and of the heavens.

### Two Annotations

First. <term>Petrus Bonus<note>Peter Bonus of Ferrara, an influential 14th-century alchemical writer.</note></term> considers in his *Pearl* <note>Referring to his famous work, the *Margarita Pretiosa Novella* or "New Precious Pearl."</note> that the great medicine whose inventor is being sought is alchemy. However, this does not seem true; for the composition—both of this work and of the eight parts from which it is composed—most clearly denies being secretly called "alchemy."

Second. Both here and elsewhere in this book, the Author mentions Hermogenes. However, the same Petrus Bonus understands Hermogenes (and rightly so) to mean Hermes. I add this sign because further down he affirms that there was a threefold Hermogenes in philosophy <note>A reference to the title 'Trismegistus' or 'Thrice-Greatest.'</note>; there is no one who doubts that this title is attributed to Hermes by everyone.

### A Most Beautiful Preparation of Honey

With God’s blessing, take the juice of a sweet pomegranate—

... 20 <term>rotuli<note>A unit of weight, roughly equivalent to a pound, common in Mediterranean and Arabic pharmaceutical traditions (the *ratl*).</note></term> of sour pomegranate juice; 10 <term>rotuli</term> of sweet apple juice; 10 <term>rotuli</term> of clear and sweet grape juice; and 5 <term>rotuli</term> of white and pure sugar. Let it be cooked until it is made like thick honey. This is indeed that best honey with which medicines are prepared. You must therefore use it as was previously described.

### Concerning the First Medicine

Let there be taken, with the blessing and help of God: one <term>rotulus</term> of red roses and a fourth part of a <term>rotulus</term> of violets. And let all these be placed in 10 <term>rotuli</term> of fresh water. Afterward, let there be placed in it a half <term>rotulus</term> of <term>elcoronga<note>original: *elcorongam*; likely referring to a distillate of citrus or a specific medicinal plant common in medieval apothecaries.</note></term> water, and a half <term>rotulus</term> of green mint water, and a fourth part of a <term>rotulus</term> of <term>mace<note>original: *maze fegos*; probably referring to the spice mace or a variation of fennel.</note></term> water, and one <term>rotulus</term> of <term>borage<note>original: *linguae bouine*, literally "ox-tongue," the common name for borage.</note></term> water. Let all these be gathered and cooked with one ounce of <term>nutmeg<note>original: *elmeg*, a corruption of the Arabic or Latin terms for nutmeg.</note></term> and an ounce of cloves. And all these must stand over the fire for one day and night, until all their strength comes forth. Then let it be placed over a gentle fire until a third part of the water is reduced; then let it be allowed to clear. Then let three <term>rotuli</term> of the previously prepared honey be placed in it, and let it be cooked until it becomes thick. Then let there be placed in it one and a half <term>drachms<note>A small measure of weight, about 1/8th of an ounce.</note></term> of good musk, and one drachm of ambergris, and three drachms of raw, ground, and moistened aloe wood. And this is the first medicine or potion, the effect of which is to strengthen the stomach, heart, and brain.

### The Third Medicine

Let there be taken one and a half <term>rotuli</term> of <term>emblic myrobalan<note>original: *emleg*, referring to *Phyllanthus emblica* (Amla), a fruit valued for its high vitamin content and restorative properties.</note></term>; a half part of one <term>rotulus</term> of Indian <term>chebulic myrobalan<note>original: *elileg indico*, likely *Terminalia chebula*, a staple of both Arabic and Ayurvedic medicine.</note></term>; and of <term>cinnamon<note>original: *darsani canele*; *darsani* is derived from the Arabic *dar sini*, meaning 'wood of China'.</note></term>, and of <term>galangal<note>original: *kalega galengal*; a root similar to ginger used for digestive warmth.</note></term>, and of nutmeg, one dram each. And let it all be ground, though not too finely, and placed in fresh water. And let ten <term>rotuli</term> of these portions be taken and remain in it for one day and one night; then let it be cooked over a gentle fire until half is reduced; then let it be mixed and strained until it clears; afterward let it be combined with three <term>rotuli</term> of <term>prepared honey<note>original: *mellis artificialis*; this refers to the "medicinal honey" recipe described on the previous page.</note></term>; and from there let it boil until it becomes thick. This is indeed the **third medicine**: whose property is to strengthen all the hidden organs, and especially the principal ones.

Let there be taken, with the help of God, the fruit-bearing water of palm shoots...

## The Fifth Medicine

Let there be taken half a pound of fresh <term>Schoenanthus<note>original: *escones*; likely *Schoenanthus* or camel grass, an aromatic medicinal herb from the Middle East.</note></term>, [and] half a pound of barberry, [and] green <term>ceterach<note>original: *citrac*; spleenwort, a fern traditionally used to treat blockages of the spleen and liver.</note></term>. And let it be placed in three pounds of fresh water; and let three pounds of anise be added; and let it stand for one day and night. Then let it be cooked down until half of the water is consumed; and let four pounds of prepared honey be taken; and let the whole be cooked until it thickens. This is the **fifth medicine**, the property of which is to expel <term>malice<note>original: *malitiam*; in medieval medicine, this refers to harmful or "bad" humours circulating in the body.</note></term>, to thin the <term>phlegm<note>One of the four humours; an excess was thought to cause congestion, lethargy, and respiratory issues.</note></term>, to remove superfluous moisture, to balance the stomach, to open <term>constipations<note>original: *constipationes*; here referring generally to "blockages" in the body's channels or organs, not just the bowels.</note></term>, and to expel gas.

## The Sixth Medicine

Let there be taken, with God’s help, of the liquid from strained <term>quince<note>original: *coptone*; from the Greek *kydōnion*, referring to quince seeds, which produce a soothing mucilage when soaked.</note></term> seeds; and of the liquid from strained quince seeds, half a pound of each; two drams of <term>Arabic gum<note>A natural gum made of the hardened sap of the Acacia tree, used as a binder and to soothe the throat.</note></term> [and] <term>tragacanth<note>original: *agomi*; likely a corruption or variant of *dragaganti*, a natural gum used as a demulcent.</note></term>; and let all the gums be dissolved in rose water; and let two pounds of prepared honey be placed in the water; and let it be thickened over a fire with a good thickening. This is the **sixth medicine**: the property of which is to soothe the chest, to strengthen and repair the <term>breathing<note>original: *respiramenta*; literally the "breathing apparatus" or the passages of the lungs.</note></term>, and it expels the pain of the kidneys.

### The Seventh Medicine

With God's help, let five drachms of <term>spikenard<note>original: *spica indica*; a costly aromatic herb from the Himalayas used in ancient and medieval pharmacy for its warming properties.</note></term> be taken, and an equal amount of long and short <term>glorybower<note>original: *clerad*; likely referring to species of *Clerodendrum*, which were used in traditional medicine to treat respiratory and digestive issues.</note></term>, one drachm [of each]. Let it all be placed in five pounds of fresh water, and let it stand for as long as it takes for its essence to be extracted. Then let it be ground and strained, and placed in three pounds of clarified honey. Let it be cooked over a gentle fire until it thickens. This is the **seventh medicine**, whose property is to restore the stomach and drive away gas.

### The Eighth Medicine

Let three drachms of solid and fresh <term>rhubarb<note>original: *reubarbaro*; in the medieval period, "rhubarb of the barbarians" was a highly prized medicinal root imported from the East, used primarily as a liver tonic and laxative.</note></term> be taken, and one drachm of caraway. Let it all be mixed, and let ten pounds of fresh water be added over it. Let it stand in that [water] until the essence is extracted. Then let it be lightly ground, and then let it be strained, and three pounds of prepared honey be added over it. Let it be cooked over a calm fire until it is thickened. This is the **eighth medicine**, whose property is to cleanse the <term>liver<note>original: *epar*; the Greek-derived term used in medical Latin for the liver.</note></term>, and to restore, strengthen, and comfort all the internal organs.

It brings much benefit to a person with difficulty breathing; it gladdens and generates joy; and it does many other good things which would be long to relate. But it has a special property for generating intellect and nourishing the brain. O **Alexander**, you need not take any other medicine at any time if you use this one.

## On laxative medicine with regard to the moon.

When you wish to take a <term>laxative<note>original: *laxatiuam*; a medicine used to purge the body of "corrupt" humors through the bowels.</note></term> medicine: let the 
moon consist in Libra or Scorpio or Pisces. Beware of the approach 
of the moon to Saturn, for then it causes the humors and the 
medicine to congeal in the body. And the further the moon is 
removed from Saturn, the better it is. Nor is it to be feared 
when it is in Mercury. Let the beginning of your work, therefore, 
be according to a good constellation of the moon: and its 
absence from harmful stars: and its prosperity in its ascent.

When you wish to give medicines: see in which sign the sun is:

¶ You should carefully examine the signs and months described in the present sphere <note>An astrological diagram or circular chart used for calculations.</note>. If both luminaries <note>The sun and the moon.</note> are received in or look upon <note>original: *aspexerint*; an astrological "aspect," or specific geometric angle between celestial bodies thought to transmit influence.</note> choleric signs, the medicine given at that time will have little effect. If indeed they are in a melancholic sign, it will loosen either nothing or very little. If the sun and moon are in phlegmatic signs, the medicine taken will work easily; and especially if the moon is increasing, because as Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (Pliny the Elder), a Roman author who wrote "Natural History."</note> says, as the moon increases, the humors of the entire body increase, as do the secretions and the <term>superfluities<note>original: *letamina*; literally "manure" or "fertilizer," used here metaphorically for bodily waste products.</note></term>. When the moon is decreasing, however, all these things decrease. Anyone who wishes to experience these secrets of nature which we have compiled in himself will undoubtedly be able to know them.

## Annotation.

I consider that it should not be passed over in silence in this place that the present chapter is incomplete. For the author says that by carefully inspecting the signs and months described in the present sphere, the sign in which the sun is found will be known; yet this sphere is missing <note>This indicates a missing illustration or diagram in the source manuscript used by the printer or scribe.</note>.

O Alexander, you already know that which I have previously treated and explained more than once: namely, that the substance of this world in its entirety—its superiority and height, its inferiority and depth, its priority and posteriority—is one. It has no difference according to its substance, because its diversity exists only in its <term>accidents<note>In medieval philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color, size, or temperature) that can change without changing the essential nature of the thing itself.</note></term>; its existence is in form, accidents, and colors. Therefore, if no thing differs from itself, it follows that the difference is something else. Whatever, therefore, exists physically of the natures in the world, which are first divided into four...

parts: and these are the **four elements**. Then, whatever is generated from these—whether of <term>inorganic substances<note>original: *originalibus*; in the medieval worldview, this refers to minerals and metals, the "origin" or base level of the material hierarchy.</note> or of **animals**—is always [influenced] by the world surrounding it. It appears, therefore, that the forms of lower things are governed by the **super-celestial, spherical, and spiritual forms**. After they recognized and investigated its utility, durability, and eternity, once they had seen it, they **hid and concealed it**, so that they would not be hindered by...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of how celestial bodies influence the physical world. ...their knowledge: those who do not possess the comprehension which [the learned] have grasped, nor the knowledge which they have known; such are the divisions of the Most High, whose power and gifts are glorified in His creatures, flowing according to His wisdom and according to His will over those things which they desired and approved. And the glorious God will not distance you from those who possessed **knowledge**, since you are among the number of those who desire to acquire something and who seek to possess victory—either according to your own intention toward that which is desired, or according to the suitability of the desired things for yourself.

Therefore, regarding those in this world who seek the <term>hidden secrets<note>original: *secretis occultis*; in medieval philosophy, "occult" simply meant "hidden from the physical senses," referring to the internal workings of nature.</note> and the **occult sciences**, both universal and particular, which men pass by and do not recognize nor attend to: this occurs because their intention and desire is focused on other things, and that which they [should] desire is foreign to them. 

And now I will show you that whatever you see in this world is evident to your senses through two things: namely, **matter** and **form**. Therefore, every durable **form** is perpetual within the <term>Creator<note>original: *operante*; literally "the one working," referring here to the divine architect or the primary moving force of the universe.</note> himself, without increase or decrease. However, all **matter** that exists in the state of <term>generation<note>original: *generatione*; the process by which things are born or come into physical existence.</note> does not exist permanently at any specific time. You see nothing except the **form** of **animals** and **vegetables** being less enduring in age; such matter receives into itself **corruption**, defect, increase, or decrease. This is because the form is absent and recedes due to the body and the matter carrying it, which is necessarily composed of a <term>mixture of parts<note>original: *compositione partium*; referring to the four elements—earth, air, fire, and water—which make up all physical bodies.</note>. These parts are divided by contrary motions within the body as long as it exists in a state of gathering together or falling apart, even while the formation durably persists.

The reason, however, why a **form** is found to be durable and perpetual is because it originates from the **planets**, which are themselves durable and perpetual. The cause of the corruption <note>original: *corruptionis*</note> of earthly things lies in their aggregation and separation; this arises from the influence of contrary planets when that influence persists in a single way. 

It appears, therefore, that the aforesaid things and **celestial forms**—when they are taken from the lights of the intermediate planets into the heavens themselves—cause the **elements** themselves to receive **humors** from them. For the elements must receive from them, and from these planets comes the durability possessed by that individual humor, the movement of forms and powers, and their specific works according to the **celestial complexion** <note>original: *complexionem*; the specific astrological balance or temperament of the stars at a given moment.</note> or...

What most ought to be known and practiced is to recognize the hours of the **benefic** and **malefic** <note>original: *benignorum et reproborum*; literally the "kind" and the "rejected" or "wicked" planets/hours.</note> ascendants, and what each holds in longitude, latitude, and its rising.

Then, similarly, [one must understand] every **Image** and every member existing within it, for every composite thing is understood according to how it is constituted and perceived. 

Therefore, when you desire—with the help of the Most High and Most Simple—to perform the **binding** <note>original: *ligationem*; a technical term for fixing celestial or spiritual powers into a physical object like an amulet.</note> of those super-celestial powers which work upon these bodies, and you already know what each planet possesses in the universe and in individuals and in composite bodies: consider the visions and consider the **time**...

It establishes the link between divine power and the inherent virtues of earthly materials, addressed to Alexander the Great in the tradition of the Secretum Secretorum.</meta>

# On the properties of origins and stones.

In the following chapters, we shall determine in a brief treatise the qualities, properties, and **virtues** <note>original: *virtutibus*; in this context, it refers to the inherent medicinal or magical powers of a substance rather than moral character.</note> of certain herbs, as well as their uses. Indeed, in our other books, we have fully explained the properties of stones, the powers of herbs, and the natures of plants; therefore, we will now speak only of what is sufficient for the present work concerning plants and stones.

But you should know, O Alexander <note>The text is addressed to Alexander the Great, reflecting the pseudo-Aristotelian tradition of the *Secretum Secretorum*, a popular medieval "mirror for princes" providing advice on governance, health, and alchemy.</note>, that just as there are different natures in plants and different powers divinely implanted in them, so too are different species and virtues found in stones. Their beauty and usefulness, which are of inestimable value, are most especially and principally suited to **Royal Majesty**. For the crowns of kings are decorated with precious stones, by whose beauty the sight is aided, the soul is delighted, and dignity is adorned; and by their power, the most serious illnesses are expelled from the body. Without these, medicine is of little effect; therefore, physicians use them in treatments to drive out the most severe diseases. Truly, a vast and marvelous power has been divinely bestowed upon both plants and stones, but it is hidden from the human race. However, we have more fully set forth their properties and virtues in our book on stones and plants.

### Annotation.

*Aristotle's books on **Stones** are now lost; however, **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a renowned medieval scholar who integrated Aristotelian philosophy with Christian thought and wrote extensively on the natural sciences.</note> says in his preface to **Minerals** <note>original: *mineralium*</note> that he saw them, though not in their entirety but as excerpted parts. Books **On Plants** are indeed circulated under Aristotle's name. We have explained more about these in the catalog of those works whose Aristotelian authorship is doubted.*

---

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions addressed to Alexander the Great, moving from the general virtues of stones and herbs to the specific preparation of the "Greatest Secret"—the Philosopher's Stone. It transitions from natural philosophy into the specific chemical and allegorical processes of alchemy.</meta>

<margin>The Philosopher's Stone <note>original: *Lapis phoz*, an abbreviation for *Lapis philosophorum*.</note></margin>

**I**n the first place, O Alexander, I wish to hand over to you the greatest secret of secrets: and may divine power aid you in perfecting your purpose and in concealing this mystery. Therefore, take the animal, vegetable, and mineral stone, which is not a stone, nor does it have the nature of a stone. And this stone is in some way likened to the stones of mountains, mines, plants, and and animals; it is found in any place, at any time, and in any man. It is convertible into any color and contains within itself all the elements; it is called the "lesser world" <term>microcosm: the philosophical concept that a single object or human being contains the same patterns and elements as the entire universe</term>. I shall call it by its own name, by which the common people name it—namely, the "limit of the egg" <note>original: *terminus oui*; referring to the *ovum philosophorum* or Philosopher's Egg, a symbolic vessel or the starting material for the alchemical work</note>; that is to say, the Philosopher’s Egg. Divide it, therefore, into four parts; each part has its own nature. Then compose it equally and proportionally, so that there is neither division nor conflict within it, and you shall achieve your purpose, the Lord granting it. This method is universal, but I shall divide it for you into specific operations. Thus, it is divided into four, and in two ways it is done well and without corruption. When, therefore, you have water from air, and air from fire, and fire from earth, then you shall fully possess the art. Therefore, arrange the airy substance by discernment, and arrange the earthy substance by moisture and heat, until they agree and are joined together such that they are neither divided nor discordant. Then add to them two operative virtues—namely, water and fire—and then your work will be fulfilled. For if you mix water alone, it will whiten; and if you assist it with fire, it will redden, the Lord granting it.

### is declared.

It should be known, however, that to know how to produce true silver and gold is impossible, since it is not possible to be equal

->Annotation.<-

I judge this chapter to be spurious for a threefold reason. First, because two of the most ancient manuscripts do not recognize it. Second, because **Bonus of Ferrara** <note>Petrus Bonus, an influential 14th-century alchemist</note>, who flourished in the year of our Lord 1338, says in his work *The Precious Pearl*—while maintaining that the book on minerals belongs to **Aristotle**—that even if **Aristotle** denied alchemy in that book as one who was young and inexperienced, yet having become an old man, he investigated it most subtly. Through the sayings of the ancients, he weighed it as true by possible reason, and he held the experience itself, saw it with his eyes, and touched it with his hands. Therefore, he wrote it for his disciple **King Alexander** in the book *On the Secret of Secrets*, in the chapter on precious stones, in the same manner as other ancient Philosophers have written: in hidden, figurative, and veiled speech. Indeed, the whole...

### On the making of a ring of wondrous power.

And you shall make a ring out of silver and pure gold, and out of **red agate** <term>acut rubeo: likely a Latinization of the Arabic *aqiq*, referring to carnelian or red agate</term>. And it shall be a net [to protect] against removals <note>that is, a protection against being displaced or losing one's position</note>. Those who see the one wearing this ring shall honor him, and they shall obey his voice in this world, and no one shall escape from his hand.

### On alchemy:

And our father **Hermogenes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy and Hermeticism</note>, who is "thrice-great" in philosophy, said in his excellent prophesying: the truth is such, and it is not a doubt, that lower things respond to higher things. The worker of miracles, however, is the one and only God, from whom every marvelous operation depends; just as every thing is created from one single substance by one single disposition. Its father is the Sun and its mother the Moon; the breeze carried it in its womb; however, the Earth was deprived of it <note>this phrasing differs from the standard Emerald Tablet, which usually states "the Earth is its nurse"</note>. He is called the father of enchantments, the treasury of miracles, the giver of power. From fire, earth was made; separate the earth from the fire, because the subtle is more worthy than the gross, and the rare than the thick. This is done wisely and discreetly. It ascends, indeed, from the earth—

To perform the miracles of the one thing. Just as all things were from one single meditation of the One, so all things were born from one single thing, by His decree. Its father is the Sun. Its mother is the Moon. The wind carries it in its belly. Its nurse is the Earth. The father of all the perfection <term>teleſmi: from the Greek *telesmos*, meaning completion or the power of a talisman</term> of the whole world is here. Its power is complete if it shall be turned into earth. # On the virtue of certain precious stones.

And among the wonders of this world is that stone which fights with waters and winds: you see it rise above the waters when the waters run with the winds. It is born in the Mediterranean Sea, and its property is this: if you take this stone and place it in another stone <note>Likely meaning to set it within a piece of jewelry or a secondary mount</note> and carry it with you, it is not possible that any army can endure against you or resist you; instead, it will flee headlong before you.

<margin>salt waters <note>original: *aquae salmaticae*; likely referring to brackish or brine-heavy waters</note></margin>
There are also two precious stones of marvelous virtue which are found in dark places: one of which is white, and the other red. They are discovered in salt waters. Their operations are these: the white stone begins to appear above the waters at sunset, and remains upon the surface of the waters until midnight, at which point it begins to descend downward; and at sunrise, it reaches the bottom. The red stone, however, works in the opposite way: for it begins to appear from sunrise until the hour of noon, and then begins to sink <note>original: *occidere*; literally "to set," as a celestial body sets below the horizon</note> until sunset. 

The properties of these stones are as follows: If you hang a weight of one drachma <note>A small ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.4 grams</note> of the red stone upon one horse of your army, the horses of your army will not cease to whinny <note>original: *hinnire*; the neighing of horses</note> until you take the stone away. And the operation of the white stone is the opposite, for the horse would never whinny until you remove it. Therefore, they are of great value for carrying out ambushes and for the work of an army. And another property of these stones is this: if two people should be quarreling with each other, place [the stone between them...]

They prepare it with heat, and because of this, it is thin to the touch. Its property is as an aid against the poisons of all plants and minerals, and against the bites and stings of small animals. ### Concerning Plants <note>original: *De uegetabilibus*</note>

O Alexander, you are fully aware from the preceding treatises—where I discussed with you the natural things and the secrets of the created world—that the rank and disposition of plants follow after the rank and disposition of minerals according to their essence. You also know that a plant receives its proper form according to its origin and nature from the superior natures <note>Refers to the celestial bodies or "higher" spiritual forces that medieval scholars believed influenced the physical world</note> that prevail and dominate over it. 

In plants, the prevailing nature is **watery**, whereas in mineral stones, the prevailing nature is **earthy**. Therefore, the nature of plants receives its "extension" <note>Growth or physical spreading</note> from the nature of water, just as water itself is moved and driven by the winds in its own place. And just as water can take on diverse shapes—for many forms are found within it—so it happens that all manner of shapes are found among plants. 

Since the prevailing element in plants is water, and it only spreads through diffusion, and since the process of dissolving water is perpetual and works unceasingly in its own "heaven"—namely, the planet **Mercury**—the following is universally true: each of the planets rules and organizes whatever fits and resembles its own nature. 

For example:
* **Saturn** governs the earth.
* **Mercury** governs water.
* **Jupiter** governs the air.
* **Sun** governs fire.

This correspondence is not found within the physical bodies of the planets themselves, but in their **operations** <note>The "work" or influence they exert on the material world</note>, which they perform continually and perpetually through a superior universal power that stands above all the powers of these operations. However, this is not the place to provide a full demonstration of these difficult matters.

<summary>This page concludes the lapidary (gemstone) section with a stone that protects against death and poison, then introduces a botanical treatise explaining that plants are primarily composed of water and governed by the planet Mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Alexander the Great, gemstones, toxicology, botany, four elements, astrology, Mercury, Saturn, Jupiter, Sun, medieval science</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the master’s instruction to Alexander regarding the natural world. Having previously established that plants are dominated by the element of water (whereas minerals are earthy), the text now transitions into the specific astrological influences that govern different types of botanical life and their effects on the human body and spirit.</meta>

But I have made mention of this because it is necessary and exceedingly useful for you regarding the following treatise, in which we shall define the species and individual types of certain botanical plants. Therefore, the knowledge of the properties of these things belongs to the philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to natural scientists or those who study the "why" and the essential nature of things</note>, but the knowledge of the natural operations <note>The "natural operations" refer to the practical effects or medicinal actions</note> of these things belongs to the physicians.

And I desire that it not escape your wisdom: that every plant lacking light or luster is ruled by **Saturn**, and it is attributed to him. And every plant that is flowery and bright is ruled by **Mercury**, and is assigned to him. And whatever plant is flowery but does not bear fruit is attributed to **Mars**, and is ruled by him. Every plant, however, that is both flowery and fruit-bearing is attributed to the **Sun**, and is ruled by him. 

Then, combine and join these divisions, and you may say: every plant bearing fruit but not flowering—such as the **Palm tree** <note>Medieval botanists often categorized plants based on conspicuous flowers; since palm flowers are often small or hidden, they were sometimes described as non-flowering</note>—is attributed to both Saturn and the Sun. And every flowery plant that does not bear fruit is created by Mercury and Mars. 

Likewise, some plants grow by branches, some by seeds, and some are born without seed and without planting. It is therefore clearer than light from what has been said: that every species of plant has its own proper disposition, through which it is "complexioned" <note>The "complexion" refers to the specific balance of qualities—hot, cold, moist, or dry—governed by the stars</note> and imitates the power of a single planet; it is likened to it or attributed to it. Yet it may have another property through which it is likened to and attributed to the power of two or more planets, according to what it is able to receive. 

And that which is attributed to it helps each individual property—<gloss>namely</gloss> that natural power which arranges that species in its color, smell, taste, and shape. The "natural soul" <note>original: *anima naturalis*; the vegetative soul responsible for growth and reproduction</note> collects all these properties and natural powers, since they run together from that soul and are imitated through it; it causes them to endure for as much time as they are defined and designated by its power. For there is no act without motion, nor any operation without a limit. 

And thus you will find a certain species of plant to be harmful, and another to be healing. A certain species generates joy, and another gladness; one creates love, and another hate. One grants reverence and honors to those who carry it; another grants abasement and contempt; and some vi... <note>The text cuts off mid-word</note>

### Annotation.

<margin>Note:</margin>
That not only plants but also stones—nay, even animals themselves—are ruled by the planets and receive miraculous virtues <note>In this context, "virtues" refers to inherent powers or spiritual properties</note>, Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on "theurgy" and the spiritual connection between the heavens and the earth</note>, among others, declares in many places. Among other things, he relates that some people claim the rooster <note>original: "gallum"</note> is greatly feared by the lion, and almost revered; the cause of which we cannot assign to physical matter or the senses, but only to the contemplation of the celestial order. 

This is namely because the presence of solar virtue <note>The spiritual power or essence of the Sun</note> suits the rooster more than the lion. This is evident from the fact that the rooster, as if with certain hymns, applauds the rising sun and calls out to it when it turns toward us from the midheaven of the Antipodes <note>A term for the opposite side of the world</note>. Furthermore, sometimes certain solar Angels have appeared endowed with such forms; and although they themselves were formless in their essence, they appeared in form to us, who are material beings. Sometimes even solar demons have been seen with the face of a lion; when a rooster was presented to them, they suddenly vanished. 

Many things regarding this matter are also recounted by Michael de Enulo in his *Medical Compendium* <note>original: "medicinali summa"</note>, which is preserved among us as a very ancient work. Among other things, it is held there that the mole <note>original: "talpa"</note> is assigned to the Sun, from which it derives the power to see acutely. Though this seems incredible, I have indeed seen through experience and known by the consideration of the small details <note>original: "calculorum," which could refer to anatomical stones or careful mathematical/observational reasoning</note> that it has very small eyes covered everywhere with hairs, so that they are not harmed by the earth...

## On the nine species of plants having miraculous virtues.

Indeed, that species of plant <note>original: "uegetabilium," referring to the vegetable or plant kingdom</note> which generates reverence and honors is a certain tree having curled leaves. Its leaf is round, and the fruit is likewise; the branches of this tree are moist, and the scent is most sweet. Therefore, whoever pulls it up in their own name and carries it with them certainly acquires honor and reverence for themselves.

There is another tree which rises up in height, on whose branches are long, moist leaves having white lines within them; whoever carries some of the substance of this tree will be exalted <note>He will be raised in status or social standing</note>.

There is another tree having huge leaves, and whose shoots extend across the ground, and whose scent is good. Whoever carries this with them will be joyful, upright, and bold. It is not good to wrestle, litigate, or fight with such a person, because their adversary will always yield before them, and they will triumph in their works.

And among the species of trees, there is one that has points and a shoot; it has oblong leaves and loses its leaves before it produces flowers; and it has three oblong red flowers of sweet scent. Whoever eats these flowers, joy and laughter come to them. But whoever pulls it up with its stem and leaves, and avoids the flower <note>or "flees from the flower"</note> while thinking of a certain woman, causes her to fall in love with him.

And another herb which is called *andrositon* <note>original: "andrositon"; likely a corruption of *androsaemon*, known as "man's blood" or Tutsan</note>, which grows in the land of Africa; it is full-grown, having dry and very small leaves, and very small seeds, round and white on the inside. If you take seven grains of the seeds in the name of someone...

And there is another plant called *naranison*, otherwise known as *nathafon* <note>original: "naranison alias nathafon"</note>; and this is most powerful for acquiring love and reverence.

O Alexander, I have completed for you what I promised, and I have fulfilled it with a full explanation. Be, therefore, always virtuous, and may the glorious God—whose goodness every creature perceives—direct, rule, and guard you.

### On justice and its commendation.

Justice is a praiseworthy condition among the attributes of the Most High, the glorious and simple Being. For this reason, the kingdom ought to belong to him whom God has chosen and established over His servants. To him must be committed the affairs and governance of his subjects; he must watch over and defend their possessions, their riches, and their blood.

And she is the cause of the intellect: because she brings into being its essence and its operation: and the intellect is its act: and it is the righteous judge. 

Therefore, the essence of justice and its root is the intellect: and it is the intellect that works and draws it forth: furthermore, it is its power and its operation: and it is its intention: and its contemplation of knowledge. The judge himself is working and receiving that which comes from the act in that which he receives: and this is justice.

Begin therefore from any division: and there will be given to you that than which nothing is more precious: namely the circle of the firmament: from which are the ordinations or universal governments in higher and lower things. And for the preservation of this world, it seemed to me that I ought to begin in the world in such a way: and this is the utility of this book and this is its figure.

The world is a garden or orchard: whose substance or species is judgment. Judgment is a ruler walled in by law. Law is the governance <term>regimen: the system of rule or management</term> by which a king rules. A king is a shepherd who is defended by nobles: nobles are stipendiaries <note>original: "stipendiarij," those who receive a stipend or pay, such as soldiers or officials</note> sustained by money. Money, truly, is the fortune which is collected from subjects. Subjects, moreover, are servants whom justice subdues. Justice, truly, is that which is sought for its own sake: in which lies the salvation of the subjects.

# On the origin of things into being, and their order.

<margin><unclear>In this part</unclear></margin>
Let it be known <term>that</term> <note>original: "quod," used here as a conjunction to introduce a foundational truth</note> the first thing which the Glorious Most High formed is a simple spiritual substance at the height of perfection, completeness, and goodness. Within this substance is the form of all things, and it is named the <term>Intelligence</term> <note>This refers to the "Divine Intellect" or "First Mind" in Neoplatonic philosophy, the first emanation from God</note>. Then, from that substance, there came forth another substance lower in its degree, which is called the <term>Universal Soul</term> <note>The "Anima Mundi" or World Soul, which animates the physical universe</note>. From that soul proceeded another substance called <term>hyle</term> <note>original: "hyle," the Greek term for "prime matter" or the raw material of the universe</note> before it was given the measurements of length, width, and depth, and within this, simple body was created. This body existed before the most noble of all shapes—the sphere—which surpasses all other figures and is more ancient in comparison. That which was purest and simplest remained in the region of the celestial spheres and the planets. Thus, the first <term>sphere</term> <note>The celestial orbs that medieval scholars believed carried the planets and stars</note> surrounds everything, extending down to the boundary of the sphere of the moon.

### On the motion and position of the heavens.

There are nine heavens, one inside the other. The first and highest of the spheres is the sphere surrounding [all], and then God <note>The text reads "Deum ipsum," likely referring to the Empyrean, the highest heaven where God was said to dwell</note> himself placed the sphere of the stars. After this comes the sphere of <term>Saturn</term>, and so on through the planets down to the sphere of the moon. Below the moon is the sphere of the four <term>elements</term>: which are fire, air, water, and earth. Therefore, the earth is the center of all the elements; it is reasonably the lowest of all bodies and the densest substance. After these spheres were arranged one within another, just as has been described, according to the wisdom and plan of the Glorious Simple One, in a wondrous arrangement and most orderly beauty, the spheres were set in circular motion. Their parts and the planets move above the four elements, and thus the successions of day and night, summer and winter, and heat and cold are brought upon them. Certain things were also mixed together, and the rare was tempered with the dense, the heavy with the light, the hot with the cold, and the moist with

It establishes the earth as the center of the universe, governed by the movements of the heavens and the mixing of the four elements.</summary>

Accordingly, those things then composed were formed over a duration of time: all species of composite bodies, which are either original elements, seeds, minerals, plants, or animals. The original composite things, therefore, are whatever is congealed in the bowels of the earth, in the depths of the seas, in the hollows of mountains, and within trapped fumes; they arise from ascending vapors and frozen moisture in the depths of caverns where "earthy air" is most dominant. These include gold, silver, copper, iron, lead, <term>tin</term> <note>original: "staneum," a common medieval spelling of "stannum"</note>, stones, <term>pearls</term> <note>original: "margaritȩ"</note>, coral, <term>tutty</term> <note>original: "tutia," an old term for zinc oxide used in alchemy and medicine</note>, vitriol, and alum, and similar things which are seen and known. An animal, however, is every kind that moves, feels, and travels from place to place by its own power, and in which <term>vitality</term> <note>original: "vivacitas," the life-force or energy of a living being</note> is more dominant. A vegetable composition is nobler than a mineral one. An animal, however, is nobler in its composition than a vegetable. Man is truly nobler in comparison to all other animals, and <term>fiery nature</term> <note>original: "igneitas." In medieval physiology, humans were thought to possess a higher degree of "vital heat" or "innate fire" than other creatures</note> dominates more within him. In his composition, all things and all universal matters are brought together, which are found in living, simple, and composite things. For man is made of a dense and balanced body and a simple, spiritual substance—the soul.

It is necessary for you, therefore—if you are a seeker of the sciences and the truth of existing beings—that you begin first with the knowledge of your own soul, which is closer to you than all other things. Only after this should you seek knowledge of other things.

One of these powers is the <term>perceptive</term> <note>original: "signum," likely referring to the power of receiving "signs" or impressions</note>, while the second is the <term>active</term> <note>original: "operans"</note>, which the Glorious God has illuminated with seven virtues: the attractive, retentive, digestive, expulsive, nutritive, <term>formative</term> <note>original: "informatiua," the power that gives shape to matter</note>, and vegetative powers. <meta>This page continues the discussion of human nature, moving from the physical composition mentioned on the previous page to the developmental stages of the human life cycle and the soul's eventual fate. It then begins a new section on the metaphorical structure of man as a "city."</meta>

...positioning of the human body begins with the reception of the seed in the womb; and in its development, it is cared for over seven months. After it has completed this time, which was measured out by the Most High, Glorious, and Sublime, then another <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>original: "anima sensibilis," the part of the soul responsible for sensation and movement, shared with animals</note> transforms it from that place until its exit from its dwelling <note>the womb</note>. It then acquires another <term>governance</term> <note>original: "regimen," referring to the guiding principle or developmental stage</note> until the completion of four years. Next, it hands him over to the <term>rational power</term> <note>original: "virtuti rationali"</note>, which changes the nature of sensory things, and it then acquires another governance until the completion of thirteen years. At that time, the intellectual power is added, which declares figures, similarities, and the interpretations of sensory things; it then acquires another governance until the completion of 30 years. Then the judicial or philosophical power arrives, which contemplates intellectual forms, and it then acquires another governance until the completion of 40 years. At that point, the royal and voluntary power falls to him, and he has then another governance until the completion of 55 years. Then comes the legal power that explains original principles, and from there he acquires another governance until the end of life. 

If, therefore, the soul is perfected and completed before its separation from the body, it is received by the power of the <term>Universal Soul</term> <note>original: "anime universalis," a Neoplatonic concept of a world-soul that connects the divine to the physical world</note>, and is raised up by it and led even to heavenly perfection. It then acquires another governance until it comes to the circle or firmament of intelligence with which it is well pleased. If, however, the soul has not yet been perfected, it slips down into the abyss of the hells; thereafter, it receives a governance from the beginning <note>original: "ex capite," meaning from the top or start</note> without hope of pleasing.

## On the formation of man.

When, therefore, God Most High created man, He made him the noblest of animals. He commanded him, and forbade him; He punished and rewarded him. He established his body like a certain city, and set his intellect to rule within it. He placed him in the more noble and higher place of the man himself, which is the head. He also established five "porters" <note>original: "baiulos," literally carriers or porters, here referring to the five senses</note> governing him and presenting to him those things which...

...to him, all things that are necessary for him [are provided]: both those things by which he is aided and those guarding him from afar and near from everything that might harm him. He has neither existence nor perfection except through these; and He established for each of these five <term>porters</term> <note>original: "baiulorum," literally porters or carriers; here used metaphorically for the senses that carry information from the outside world into the "city" of the soul</note> or senses a specific judgment of its own. By this, it differs and is separated from other structures, and possesses its own way in which any of the others communicates with it. From the agreement and collection of these judgments, the essence and perfection of their works is generated. Therefore, the five porters are the five senses: which are in the eye, in the ear, in the nose, in the tongue, and in the hand.

### On sight.

The objects perceived by the eye, therefore, are visible things, which comprise ten <term>species</term> <note>original: "species," used here in the Aristotelian sense of "types" or "categories" of perception</note>: namely light and darkness, color and body <note>meaning physical substance or volume</note>, figure and position, distance and proximity, motion and rest.

### On hearing.

The objects perceived by the ear or hearing are sounds, and there are two types: animate and inanimate. Animate sound <note>original: "animalis," meaning "of a living creature" (anima)</note> is twofold: rational and irrational. Rational sound pertains to human nature and speech; irrational sound is like the lowing of an ox, the neighing of a horse, the chirping of birds, and similar things. Inanimate sound is like the crashing of trees, the clashing of stones, and other things in which there is no life—such as thunder, the drum, and the pipe. Know, therefore, that every voice exists in its own universal order when the air carrying it is moved; and with it, a noble <term>spirituality</term> <note>original: "spiritualitas nobilis," likely referring to the subtle, non-material essence or "pneuma" that medieval philosophers believed allowed physical vibrations to be translated into a form the soul could perceive</note> is moved, so that one part does not mix with another until it reaches the furthest point of the sense of hearing. Then, it is carried through that sense to the <term>imaginative power</term> <note>original: "virtutem imaginatiuam," the internal faculty of the soul that receives and stores the "images" or impressions from the external senses</note>.

The sense of the tongue occurs by way of taste and flavor, and there are nine <term>species</term> <note>original: "species," meaning types or categories</note>: sweetness, bitterness, saltiness, oiliness <note>original: "unctuositas," also meaning fatness or richness</note>, acidity, tastelessness, pungency <note>original: "ponticitas," referring to a harsh, sour, or "unripe" flavor</note>, astringency <note>original: "stipticitas," the dry, puckering sensation caused by certain substances</note>, and sharpness.

### Annotation.

*The species of the pungent flavor was not listed in common manuscripts, but we have added it here, both following the reliability of a very ancient manuscript, and because otherwise only eight flavors would be counted by the author, even though he says there are nine. But someone might object: if he affirms here that there are nine, how did he say there are seven in the book **On the Sense and the Sensible**? <note>Referring to Aristotle’s "De Sensu et Sensibili," a foundational text in medieval science.</note> Is it because he categorized "the rich" (which he calls "oily" here) along with the "sweet," while he omitted "tastelessness" (if he did not include it with any of them) as being a certain privation of flavor? For this matter, I think it not useless to look at Pliny in Book 15 <note>Pliny the Elder’s "Natural History," an encyclopedic work of the 1st century AD.</note>, who presents ten types of flavors, and the **Conciliator** in Difference 145 <note>Referring to Pietro d’Abano (1250–1316), known as "The Conciliator," who sought to reconcile Greek philosophy with Arabic medicine.</note>, who explains several things regarding the distinctions of flavors.*

### On touch.

And the sense which is in the hand is the tactile or palpatory way; and its course consists in heat and cold, roughness and smoothness, moisture and dryness. It is a power contained between two skins: one of which is on the surface of the body, and the other in that which faces the flesh.
