<meta>This page continues the allegorical discussion of the body-as-a-kingdom. Having previously discussed the specific sensory organs (the "porters"), the text now explains how sensory data is transmitted to the brain and processed by the internal faculties (Imagination and Cognition). It then transitions into a numerological defense of the number five and begins a section on political/psychological advisors.</meta>

...thin, subtle membranes like spiderwebs. These serve as a veil and a curtain for this king <note>In this allegory, the "king" represents the rational soul or the intellect residing in the brain.</note>. Therefore, when the data held by each sense is presented and reaches those skins <term>membranes</term> <note>original: "pelles"; referring to the meninges, the protective layers surrounding the brain.</note> which are within the substance of the brain itself, the traces of all perceived things are then gathered at the **imaginative faculty** <note>In medieval psychology, the *virtus imaginativa* was the internal sense that stored images of objects even after the objects were no longer present.</note>. This faculty represents them to the **cognitive faculty** <note>The *virtus cogitativa* was the faculty responsible for analyzing, comparing, and judging the images provided by the imagination.</note>, which is located in the middle of the brain, so that it may observe them, collect them, and study their forms and examples. In this way, it may recognize what is harmful and what is helpful among them, and it may act according to the measure that proceeds from them.

### The Five Senses.

The constitution and existence of the body, therefore, consists in these five aforementioned senses. Indeed, perfection in any thing is found in five parts. For instance, there are **five planets** <note>The classical planets excluding the Sun and Moon: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> upon which the celestial spheres move in their circles. There are **five kinds of animals**: namely, humans, winged creatures, aquatic creatures, walking creatures, and those that creep upon their bellies. There are also **five things** without which no plant growing from the earth can thrive unless they concur and meet within it: the trunk, the branches, the leaves, the fruit, and the roots. There are also **five musical tones**, and if these five were not present, a song could not be harmoniously completed. Finally, there are **five most noble days** out of all the days of the year, occurring in the latter part of May.

### Concerning Counselors.

Therefore, let your "porters" <note>Referring back to the five senses as the gatekeepers of the body.</note> be five, and your **counselors** be five in the composition of your works. Let each one be separate and independent, for that is more useful for your affairs. Keep your secrets to yourself: do not begin to tell them what you hold in your heart, do not reveal <note>original: "offendes," likely a scribal error for "ostendes" (reveal/show).</note> to any of them the choice of counsel you are leaning toward, and do not suggest to them that you wish to "beg" for advice, because they will then hold you in contempt <note>original: "contenent," likely "contemnent" (will despise).</note>. Temper their various wills within your soul, just as...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the brain processes information and transitions into a series of instructions on governance, both for a literal king and as an allegory for the "king" of the body (the intellect). The focus shifts from sensory processing to the vetting of advisors and the influence of astrology on a person's character.</meta>

...which the brain does with those things that come to it from the senses. Do not turn away from their advice <note>Referring to the internal faculties acting as advisors to the intellect.</note> even when it contradicts your own desires. Because of this, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary sage often cited in medieval texts as a source of ancient wisdom.</note> said when he was asked: "Why is the judgment of the one who gives advice better than the judgment of the one who seeks it?" He replied: "Because the judgment of the one giving advice is stripped of personal will." This is a true statement. Therefore, when you gather them together to receive their counsel in your presence, do not mix your own opinions with theirs. Listen to what they agree upon. If they respond too hastily and with immediate agreement, then resist them in this and present the opposite view. This will force them to extend their thinking, ensuring that their deliberation on the final advice is not rushed.

Then, when you perceive the truth and the correctness of the advice in their words—or in the words of any one of them—let them rest. Do not reveal or show them where your own will lies until the plan is put into action and tested by experience. Consider subtly and diligently: who among them leads most toward the right path of counsel, based on the measure of love they have for you and their desire for the prosperity of your rule <term>regimen</term> <note>original: "regiminis"; in medieval thought, this referred both to the administration of a state and the medical regulation of the body's health.</note>. Receive their counsel, therefore, but take care not to favor one advisor over another. Treat them as equals in gifts and rank in all their works. For what is a greater cause for the destruction of a kingdom's affairs over the long passage of time than showing greater honor to certain stewards <term>officials</term> <note>original: "baiulorum"; literally 'porters' or 'carriers,' used here to mean high-ranking administrative officers.</note> over others? It is also worth knowing if the advice of the youth is healthy. However, I say this: judgment imitates the body. When the body is weakened, the judgment is weakened.

One must also consider the birth-chart <term>genesis</term> <note>original: "genesis"; the astrological state of the heavens at the moment of a person's birth, believed to determine their character and fate.</note> in certain individuals. This is because a person is often disposed according to the natures of the planets that were in control at the time of their birth. If it should happen that the parents of a child call him to a specific craft, his superior nature...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on natural inclination and destiny, specifically how planetary alignments at birth (astrological nativities) override upbringing and parental desires. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion on the "nature" of individuals.</meta>

...will often pull him back toward the art that is suitable for him.
For something similar to this happened to certain men who met in a particular village and were given hospitality by a certain tiler <note>A roof-maker or laborer.</note>. To him a son was born that night; the guests took his birth chart <term>genesis</term> <note>original: "genesim"; in this context, it refers to a natal horoscope or birth-figure.</note> and arranged his planets. The birth chart was thus in Venus and in Mars, existing in their degrees of Gemini and Libra. But the contrary and worst stars had not yet risen; therefore the birth chart showed that the boy was destined to be wise, courtly, quick-handed, of good counsel, and beloved by kings. They therefore hid this, concealing it from the father. And so the child grew up in prosperity, and his parents handed him over and strove for him to learn some trade related to their own work.

But he could learn nothing at all from them. However, by beating and whipping him most severely, they subjected him to their will. Therefore, the youth turned himself toward men possessing learning; he acquired knowledge, knew the courses and times of the celestial bodies, and the customs and administrations of kings. And behold, he became the king's steward <term>baiulus</term> <note>original: "baiulus"; though it can mean a porter, in a royal context it often refers to a high-ranking official or administrator.</note>. The opposite of this happened through the wretched works and dispositions of the planets and their natures, which occurred with the two sons of the King of India when compared to their birth charts. But it was hidden from the king. When one of the boys grew up, the king believed he should be educated in the sciences; he therefore sent him through India and through other provinces with honor, as befits the son of such a king. But the father's diligence profited nothing, because he could not incline his nature toward anything except the blacksmith's craft <note>original: "fabrile artificium"; referring to manual metalworking, considered a lowly occupation for royalty.</note>, on account of which the king was greatly disturbed. He therefore summoned the wise men of his kingdom, who, upon being consulted, all agreed on this: that the boy's nature was leading him. So it was done; and things similar to this have happened many times, as is well known.

<meta>This page continues the instructions to a king (modeled on Aristotle’s advice to Alexander the Great) regarding the selection and use of advisors. It transitions from the previous discussion on how a man’s inner nature and astrological destiny can lead him to high office—like the tiler’s son who became a steward—to practical advice on how to treat such learned men.</meta>

Do not, therefore, despise small stature in men whom you see love knowledge and abound in the way of wisdom and character, and who turn away from and avoid the path of vices. Love such men and keep them near you; and do so especially when you see that a man has exercised his mind in these virtues. For such a man is accustomed to being of good eloquence, well-mannered, courtly, and knowing the histories of those nations who preceded us. Do not put forward anything to be done, nor postpone any matter, without his counsel. Love his company, because such a man loves the truth and advises what befits royal majesty while removing what is contrary to it. He is firm in spirit, constant in heart, faithful, upright in his work, and good to his subjects; know this, therefore. For such counsel gladdens the stewards <term>baiulos</term> <note>As noted previously, these are high-ranking administrators or "carriers" of the king's burden.</note> and organizes the king's rule <term>regimen regis</term>. Nor should you do anything preposterously <note>Literally "backwards" or "inverted," from the Latin *prepostere*.</note> by putting forward what should be postponed, or postponing what should be put forward. Nor should you act at all without the counsel of philosophers; for the philosophers have said that counsel is the eye of future things.

### How counsel is to be sought from the stewards:

And it is read in the books of the Persians that a certain king of theirs sought counsel from his stewards <term>baiulis</term> concerning a certain very secret matter, which had been revealed to a certain queen who was beloved by him. One of them responded: "It is not fitting that the king should seek from us any counsel upon any deed or secret in a group; but rather, he should seek his secret from each one separately. For in their private secrets, such things are revealed to one person which they would not dare say before many." I conclude, therefore, and say: it is not fitting for this to happen in all works; rather, it is appropriate that this be done with discretion. It is also appropriate that they be called to your presence in their successes in "binding and loosing" <note>A legal and administrative phrase referring to the power to command or prohibit, or to make and undo laws.</note> as we have said before. For a certain one of the ancient philosophers said that the king's wisdom will be increased through the counsel of his stewards, just as the sea <note>original: "equor"; a poetic term for the level surface of the sea.</note> is increased by the reception of rivers and waters; and he will be able to acquire many things through prudence and counsel, which...

...he cannot have through the power of warriors. And in the book of a certain Mede <note>original: *medorum*</note> it was commanded to his son: "Son, it is necessary for you to have counsel, for you are but one man among men. Therefore, consult him who can deliver you from power; do not spare your enemy, but manifest your victory over him as much as you can, and at all times beware the power of the enemy."

### Annotation.

This seems to be too inhumane a command, since it orders that enemies who have been defeated should not be spared. A Christian <term>Christicola</term> <note>Literally "a worshipper of Christ." The commentator here is correcting the "pagan" advice of the text to align with medieval Christian values of mercy.</note> should therefore more safely embrace what the Author expressed to Alexander at the start of the work: that it is not cruel to show mercy to those over whom victory has been won.

## Concerning one's own counsel, and that a steward is not to be appointed in the King's place: Concerning the number of stewards, and concerning the groups of three and seven.

**D**o not place the abundance of your perception in your own self-belief, nor let the height of your status hinder you from within; rather, always add the counsel of others to your own. For if their advice is pleasing and useful, it should be embraced, and your own judgment will always remain yours. But if it differs from your own judgment, then it is for you to consider: if it is helpful and more useful than what you had considered, embrace it; if it is useless, refrain from it.

I warn you carefully and diligently and give you the best advice: never appoint a steward <term>baiulum</term> <note>As noted previously, this refers to a high-ranking administrator or regent who "carries" the king's burden.</note> in the government <term>regimine</term> in your place. For his counsel can destroy and corrupt your kingdom, your subjects, and your nobles; he may pursue his own interests and plot your downfall—along with many other things that would take too long to recount. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of counsel, moving from the human element (the number of advisors) to the mystical and philosophical significance of numbers—specifically three, five, and seven—in the order of the universe. It transitions into a scholarly commentary (Annotations) debating whether numbers possess actual power or merely represent a perfect proportion.</meta>

...let them be pleasing as we said before: let there be at least three and no fewer: since the greatest good arises from this, namely from the number three. For unless there were a third, nothing would be understood at all. Therefore, the first principle upon which all things are built is the Trinity; they are mediated through the group of five <note>original: *quinarium*</note>, and they are perfected through the group of seven <note>original: *septenarium*</note>. This is because there are seven heavens, and seven are the planets, and the cycle of the moon is sevenfold; there are seven days of treatment or medicine, and seven days in a critical period <note>In medieval medicine, the "critical days" were specific points in an illness when a patient's condition was expected to change.</note>, and many other things which regard the number seven, the listing of which would be long.

->Three Annotations.<-

<margin>
[...]
en
ad
de
ni
</margin>

*First.* It seems the Author implies that a certain power resides in numbers—extolling the group of five above, and here the groups of three and seven, and even below the group of four <note>original: *quaternarium*</note>. Whether this is some active power of the number itself that influences the form <note>original: *spetiem*</note> of things, or whether it is rather a "specific perfection" where one type of number surpasses another in quality, is no small question. (Scholars observe this not as something that causes an effect, but rather so that there is a proper proportion between the number and the object being counted—for it is fitting that more perfect things be counted by a more perfect number). Indeed, the Prince of Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance philosopher who explored the mystical power of numbers and Kabbalah.</note> in his *Apology* contends that it is an active power. However, Suessanus <note>Agostino Nifo (c. 1473–1538), a philosopher from Sessa Aurunca known for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> in the final part of his *Clarification* <note>original: *Dilucidarii*</note>, and Pedro Ingarfias of Ustellensis, a Bishop, in his masterly determinations, attempt to demolish Pico’s opinion; Zimara <note>Marcantonio Zimara (c. 1475–1532), an Italian philosopher and physician.</note> in his index <note>original: *tabula*</note> does not seem to stray from their view at all.

*Second.* The Author asserted, not without merit, that the Trinity is the first principle upon which all things depend. The supreme perfection of the Trinity can be gathered from many examples: the number of the Fates <note>The *Parcae*: the three goddesses of destiny in Roman mythology.</note> is three; there are three principal parts of the earth; a logical argument <note>original: *ratiocinatio*</note> is made of three terms; a triple propo- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword *propo-*, likely meaning *propositio* or "proposition."</note>

It highlights the tension between the mystical views of Pico della Mirandola and the more skeptical positions of later Renaissance Aristotelians.</summary>
<keywords>numerology, Trinity, seven planets, Pico della Mirandola, Agostino Nifo, Marcantonio Zimara, critical days, philosophical debate</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the practical advice for a ruler, moving from the symbolic importance of numbers to the concrete management of the state. It specifically addresses how to evaluate and select a "baiulus"—a high-ranking official or steward—by testing their loyalty and checking for the vice of greed.</meta>

there will be a corruption of the government <note>original: "regiminis"; referring to the system of rule or the king’s administration.</note>: and they will hate you beyond measure. If, however, he explains to you what he possesses and says: "This is what I have acquired through your grace and gift," offering it to you; that man is worthily to be commended and is worthy of all praise: as one who chooses and wishes for the sacrifice <note>The Latin "confusionem" here suggests a humble setting aside or "confounding" of his own private work in favor of the king's honor.</note> of his own work for your glory. You shall also test your stewards <term>baiulos: From the Latin for "carrier," this term evolved to mean a high official, bailiff, or governor who manages affairs on behalf of a sovereign.</term> in the making of gifts and presents: whichever of them you see striving and aiming for these things beyond measure, hope for no good in him. And that steward who pants after the acquisition of money <note>original: "pecunie"</note> and the keeping of treasures: do not trust in him, because his service is for the sake of gold, and he allows money to run along with the [baser] senses of men. 

And he himself is a depth without a bottom: and there is no boundary or end in him: because the more the money grows, the more the intention of acquiring and the anxiety grows. And this in a steward is a cause of royal corruption for many reasons: because perhaps the love of money and its heat will lead him to your death, or to serve the one who intends such things. Therefore, it would be fitting that your steward should not be far from your presence: but command him that he should not have dealings or frequent contact with any of the kings among other kings: and let him not read a letter to them, nor send them news. And when you perceive such things: change them without delay. Since minds are swift: and easily decline toward promises and contrary wills. The more useful among stewards is he who most loves your life and obedience: who most leads the subjects to love you: and who lays out his affairs and his very person for your pleasure: and because he possesses these virtues <note>original: "virtutes"</note> and habits <note>original: "mores"</note> which I shall enumerate:

First, he who has perfection of limbs: which are suitable for the works over which he presides, and for which he is chosen.

Second, that he has a goodness of apprehension: and speed in understanding that which is said.

Third, that he be of good memory regarding that which he has apprehended and heard: so that he commits nothing to oblivion.

Fourth, that he be considering and perceiving: as I said before, whenever a difficulty is introduced.

Fifth, that he be courtly <note>original: "curialis"; meaning possessing the manners of the court.</note>: affable: sweet of tongue: so that his tongue agrees with his heart, and responds to his thought; let him be also ep- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely intended to be "eloquens" (eloquent) or "equanimis" (even-tempered).</note>

<meta>This page continues the list of fifteen qualities required for an ideal high official or steward (baiulus) to the king. It concludes the fifth point regarding eloquence and details points six through fifteen, focusing on intellectual rigor, moral integrity, and social justice.</meta>

...on the feet of eloquence. <note>original: "pedite eloquentię." This phrase finishes a sentence from the previous page, likely describing how the official's wisdom is supported by his ability to speak well.</note>

**Sixth,** that he should have a piercing intellect in every science, and especially in the art of **arithmetic**: which is the most certain art and a true, demonstrative science <term>demonstratiua: In medieval logic, this refers to a field of knowledge that provides absolute, certain proof through logical deduction.</term> which is drawn from nature itself.

**Seventh,** that he be truthful in his words, a lover of truth, and a fleer of lies; of good moral character, healthy of complexion, sweet, mild, and easy to work with.

**Eighth,** that he be without the stain of gluttony or drunkenness in food and drink, and without the stain of lust; shunning idle games and pleasures.

**Ninth,** that he be magnanimous <term>magnanimus: From the Latin for "great-souled," this refers to a person of high ideals, courage, and a lack of pettiness.</term> in his purpose, and a lover of honor.

**Tenth,** that gold, silver, and other accidental things <note>original: "accidentalia"; in medieval philosophy, "accidents" are external qualities or worldly goods (like wealth) that are not part of a person's essential spiritual nature.</note> of this world be contemptible to him, and that his purpose and intention be focused only on those things that suit his dignity and the act of governance. And let him love both his neighbor and those who are far away.

**Eleventh,** that he love and embrace the just and justice itself, hating injury and offense; rendering to each what is their own, helping the oppressed and those who suffer injury; removing all injustice entirely, making no distinction between persons or ranks, whom God created equal.

**Twelfth,** that he be of strong and persevering purpose in those things he sees must be done: bold, without fear or faint-heartedness.

**Thirteenth,** that he know all the outgoings of expenses, and that no benefit belonging to the kingdom be hidden from him; and that the subjects do not complain of him in any way, except in those permitted cases where he can be of public use.

**Fourteenth,** that he not be overly talkative, nor prone to much laughter—because temperance is very pleasing to men—presenting himself courteously to people and treating them with kindness.

**Fifteenth,** that he not be among those who use wine; that his court be open to those arriving, and that he be intent on exploring and inquiring into news of all things, comforting his subjects, correcting their works, and consoling them.

<sig>D ij</sig>

<summary>This page completes a checklist of fifteen virtues for a royal official, ranging from mastery of arithmetic to a commitment to social equality and sobriety. It emphasizes that a leader's deputy must prioritize justice and the public good over personal wealth or frivolous pleasures.</summary>

# That man is a minor world.

<margin>

Know therefore, O Alexander, that God did not create any creature wiser than man: and He did not gather in any other animal that which He gathered in him: and there is no habit or custom to be found in any animal that you would not find in man. For man is bold as a **lion**; timid as a **hare**; generous as a **cock**; greedy as a **dog**; harsh and austere as a **raven**; pious as a **turtledove**; malicious as a **lioness**; private and domestic as a **dove**; crafty and deceitful as a **fox** <note>original: "versipellis," literally "skin-changer," used to describe someone shifty or deceptive.</note>; simple and mild as a **lamb**; swift and agile as a **roebuck**; like a **stag** in color and certain other dispositions; humble as a **leopard**; lazy as a **bear**; precious and hoary as an **elephant**; vile and dull as a **donkey**; rebellious and wicked as a **king** <note>The Latin here says "rex" (king), though in other versions of this text, this trait is usually assigned to an animal like a goat or owl; the scribe may be making a pointed political observation or it may be a transcription error from the original manuscript.</note>; obedient and humble as a **peacock**; talkative as an **ostrich** <note>original: "struno," a variant of "struthio."</note>; useful as a **bee**; dissolute and wandering as a **goat**; untamed as a **bull**; recalcitrant as a **mule**; mute as a **fish**; rational as an **angel**; lustful as a **pig**; malicious as an **owl**; useful as a **horse**; and harmful as a **mouse**. And universally, there is no animal, nor vegetable, nor any original thing, nor mineral, nor heaven, nor planet, nor zodiac sign, nor any being among all beings having any unique property, but that it is found in man. And for this reason, he is called the **minor world**. <term>minor mundus: More commonly known today as the **microcosm**. This is the ancient and medieval belief that the human being is a "little universe" that reflects the structure and variety of the entire cosmos (the macrocosm).</term>

---

->Annotation.<-

Call man a "minor world." On this matter, see, if you please, the seventeenth chapter of the eighth book of Aristotle’s *Physics*, and Averroes <note>Averroes was a 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were essential to medieval European university education.</note> on the same, section 7; also Johannes of Ghent in the book *On the Movements of Animals*, question 9; and Robert of Lincoln <note>Robert Grosseteste (c. 1175–1253), a famous English statesman and scientist-bishop.</note> declaring this matter in his said short treatise.

# That one should not trust those who do not believe your law.

And never place your hope in a man who does not believe in your laws.
And beware lest there happen to you what happened to two men
who are said to have been companions on a journey: one of whom was 
an **oriental magus** <note>original: "magus orientalis." In medieval literature, a "magus" often refers to a Persian sage or follower of Zoroastrianism, here representing a character guided by natural law and universal kindness.</note>; the other truly a **Jew**. The magus was riding 
a mule which he had raised in his own pasture: which carried 
all the necessaries that a traveler uses and requires. The Jew, 
however, went on foot: having neither food nor other 
necessaries. While they were talking on the road, the magus said to the Jew: 
"What is your law and your faith?" The Jew replied: "I believe that in 
heaven there is one God: whom I worship, and I hope for the good 
 of my soul and a **reward** <note>original: "remunerationem."</note> from Him, and for those who agree with me 
in my law. And my faith and my belief is this: that if anyone 
differs from me in my faith and law, it is lawful for me to shed 
his blood and take his money, and to kill his people—namely 
his wife, parents, and ancestors. Moreover, it is a curse upon 
me if I keep faith with him, or help him, or if I show 
mercy to you, or spare you." Consequently, he said to the magus: "Show 
me <note>The Latin text here says "meam" (my), but in context, it clearly means "tuam" (your).</note> your law and your faith; therefore, make me 
certain of your law and faith." To which the magus replied: "My faith and 
belief and law are these: in the first place, I wish for good for myself 
and the children of my kind; and I wish no evil to any creature of God—neither 
to those following my law, nor to those differing from me 
in the same. And I believe **equity** <note>original: "equitatem," referring to fairness or natural justice.</note> and **mercy** should be maintained toward 
any living thing. No injury pleases me, and it even seems to me 
that if any evil happens to any living thing, that evil 
touches me and disturbs me. I desire prosperity, health, 
safety, and happiness to happen to all men universally." Then the Jew said to him: "What if an injury and offense is done to you?" 
The magus said: "I know that in heaven there is a God: just, good, and wise; 
to whom nothing is hidden, nothing is secret, and nothing at all is concealed 
of those things that lie hidden in His creatures; who rewards the good 
according to their goodness, and the wicked and **transgressors** according to 
their transgressions." To which the Jew said: "Why then do you not keep your law—"

<summary>This page presents a moral dialogue between a "Magus" and a "Jew" traveling together, contrasting a philosophy of universal mercy and divine justice with a narrow, exclusionary legalism. The story serves as a cautionary tale for rulers about understanding the moral foundations of those they trust.</summary>

...ren of my race. <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page.</note> Why do you not confirm your faith with deeds?" The **Magus** asked, "How shall this be done?" To which the **Jew** replied: "Look, I am of your own kind <note>original: "generis tui." The Jew appeals to their shared humanity to manipulate the Magus.</note>. And you see me walking on foot, exhausted and starving, while you are a rider, well-fed and at ease." To him the Magus replied: "It is the truth." And descending from his mule, he opened his **saddlebag** <note>original: "manticam."</note>, and gave him food and drink. Then he had him ride the mule. After the Jew had secured himself well by riding, he dug in his spurs in a hurry, leaving the Magus behind.

Then the Magus began to cry out and say: "Wait for me! For I am weary." The Jew said: "Did I not explain my law and its conditions to you? And I wish to confirm it in the same way." Then he hurried the mule even more. And the Magus, following his tracks, said: "O Jew, do not abandon me in the desert, lest perhaps I be killed by lions, or die of a wretched hunger or a painful thirst. Therefore, act **mercifully** <note>original: "misericorditer."</note> with me, just as I acted with you."

The Jew, however, did not look back, nor did he pay attention to his words; nor did he stop until he was hidden from his sight. When the Magus truly despaired of catching up, he remembered the perfection of his law and his faith, and what he had said to the Jew: namely, that in heaven there is a **Just Judge** <term>Just Judge: A common medieval epithet for God (Iustus Iudex), emphasizing that while human laws may fail, divine providence ensures moral balance.</term>, to whom nothing is hidden and no secret among His creatures lies concealed. Therefore, lifting his head toward heaven, he said: "My God, You know that I have believed in You and in Your law, and I have honored You in Your commandments as You ordered. Therefore, confirm my praise of You against this Jew."

After these words, the Magus had not gone very far when, behold, he found the Jew cast down and thrown from the mule, with a broken leg and an injured neck. The mule, however, was standing opposite him; seeing the Magus, she recognized her master and approached him because of his past care for her. Therefore, mounting her and leaving the Jew in the pains of death, he began to ride away.

The Jew, however, cried out: "Dearest brother, by your God, have mercy on me! I am broken and I am dying, and I need compassion. Have mercy and keep your law, which has granted you the victory by which you have overcome me." Then the Magus began to reproach him, saying: "You have sinned wickedly against me and abandoned me without mercy." To which the Jew replied: "Do not rebuke me for a thing...

<meta>This page continues a medical treatise, likely the pseudo-Aristotelian "Secret of Secrets," discussing the health of specific body parts and universal remedies suggested by wise men of different nations.</meta>

->Concerning the third part of the body.<-

When waste products <term>waste products: medieval medicine used the term "superfluities" to describe excess bodily fluids or undigested food that caused illness</term> have gathered in the belly, these are the signs: pain in the knees, swelling, stiffness, and gases moving through the body. Therefore, the person feeling these things ought to purge themselves with certain gentle laxatives <note>original: "subtilibus mollificatiuis," literally "subtle softening agents"</note> and follow the health regimen that we previously described regarding the chest. Whoever does not do this will fall into pain of the joints and the back, diarrhea <note>original: "fluxum ventris," flow of the belly</note>, the corruption of digestion, and blockage of the liver.

->Concerning the testicles.<-

The testicles are the fourth part of the body. When, therefore, waste gathers in them, this sign follows: a loss of appetite <note>original: "tepe petitus," likely a corruption of "appetitus deperit"</note>. It is necessary for the person who feels this to take the herb called chamomile and the herb called melion <note>likely lemon balm or honey-leaf</note>, and take their roots; place the roots and herbs in a fragrant white wine and take some of it every morning. It should be tempered with water and honey. He should also abstain from excessive eating. If he neglects this medicine, he may fear pain in the testicles and the lungs, and the danger of the stone <term>the stone: painful mineral deposits in the bladder or kidneys</term>.

Indeed, it is read in the histories of the ancients that a powerful king gathered the best physicians—the Indians, the Greeks, and the Medes <note>original "Ade," referring to the Medes of Persia</note>—and commanded them that each should strive to create a single medicine of such quality that, if a man used it, it would benefit him so much that he would need no other medicine.

Truly, the Indian physician indicated and said: taking two mouthfuls of water every morning makes a man healthy, and he will not need another medicine.

The Mede, however, affirmed that on an empty stomach it is very beneficial to take grains of millet.

I, however, Aristotle the Greek, say: he who follows my teaching and does not have heaviness in his belly need not fear <unclear>disease</unclear> <note>original "guram," an obscure term possibly referring to a specific throat ailment or a corruption of "gulam" (gluttony)</note> nor lethargy. And he who eats seven drams <term>dram: a small unit of weight, roughly a teaspoonful</term> of good, sweet raisins every morning will not fear any kind of illness...

<summary>This page details the symptoms and treatments for ailments of the belly and testicles, concluding with a legendary consultation between a king and physicians from India, Persia, and Greece regarding universal health remedies.</summary>

...extremities <note>original: "mitatibus", likely "extremitatibus"</note> of **phlegm** <note>One of the four bodily humors, associated with cold and moisture; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness</note>. His memory is improved, his intellect is enlightened, and he who was of a good **complexion** <note>original: "complexionis"; the specific balance of humors that determines an individual’s physical and mental health</note> at the appropriate time will read this. He shall not fear a **quartan fever** <note>original: "febrem quartam"; a form of malaria that causes a fever every four days</note>. And he who eats figs with nuts and a few leaves of **rue** <note>A bitter herb highly valued in medieval medicine as a defense against poison and plague</note>: poison shall not harm him on that day.

### On the protection of natural heat

O supreme king, strive by all means to guard and retain **natural heat** <note>original: "calorem naturalem"; the vital energy or "inner fire" believed to drive digestion and life</note>. As long as the heat and **natural humidity** <note>original: "humiditas naturalis"; the essential moisture of the body that acts as fuel for the natural heat</note> are balanced in a person, the heat is tempered and strengthened, because health consists in these two things. It should also be known in this place that the failing and corruption of the body comes from two causes: one natural and the other against nature. The natural cause comes from the resistance and contradiction of opposing qualities, namely when **dryness** dominates in the body. Corruption against nature, however, comes from some accidental cause: whether from war, or from being struck by a stone, or from other strokes of fortune, or from illness and poor advice.

### On the qualities of foods

Of **foods** <note>original: "Libariorum", likely a transcription error for "Cibariorum"</note>, some are light, some are coarse, and some are in between. Light foods generate light blood, clear and good; these include pottage <note>A thick soup or porridge</note>, well-nourished young chickens, and fresh **soft-boiled eggs** <note>original: "fofibilia", likely a transcription error for "sorbilia", meaning eggs cooked lightly enough to be sipped</note>. Coarse foods are truly beneficial for "hot" men <note>Those with a choleric or sanguine temperament</note>, those who work while fasting, and those who sleep after a meal. Medium foods generate neither inflammation nor **superfluity** <note>An excess of humors or waste products that the body cannot process</note>; such are the meats of lamb, ram, and **wether** <note>original: "castrating"; a castrated ram, considered easier to digest than a whole ram</note>. All meats that are hot and moist seem to deceive in these cases: when they are roasted, they acquire hardness, heat, and dryness. Therefore, when such meats are roasted, they should be eaten quickly, and then more...

<summary>This page discusses the preservation of health through the balance of "natural heat" and "natural humidity," while categorizing different foods (light, coarse, and medium) by their effects on the body's humors.</summary>

<keywords>natural heat, natural humidity, phlegm, quartan fever, dietetics, rue, meat qualities, medieval medicine, humors</keywords>

...those who display sweet spices in them are praiseworthy. <note>The author is likely referring to the preparation of dishes or the presentation of food by cooks.</note> Regarding foods that generate <term>melancholy</term> <note>In medieval medicine, melancholy was one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; it was considered cold and dry, and an excess was thought to cause sadness or illness.</note>—such as beef, meat from cattle, and goose—these are meats that are coarse, dry, and rough. On the other hand, other animals besides these have "subtle" <note>original: "subtiles"; meaning light, easily digestible, or refined.</note> meats; they are small and are nourished in watery, humid, and shady places; their meats are better and healthier. The same is to be said regarding fish.

## Concerning fish.

It should be known, therefore, that a fish is of "small substance" <note>meaning light or low in density</note>, has thin skin, and is easy to chew if it is nourished in running <term>brackish</term> <note>original: "salmaticis"; salty or marshy waters where fresh and salt water mix.</note> waters; such a fish is better and lighter than one born in the sea or in fresh water. One born in the sea is healthier than one born in fresh water. One must, therefore, beware of those fish which are of "large substance" and have hard skin, for such are usually poisonous. And let these remarks on fish suffice for the present, because in the book I composed concerning <term>pottages</term> <note>original: "pulmentis"; thick soups or stews.</note> and medicines, you will find this matter determined in great abundance.

## Concerning waters.

It should be known: that "subtle" <note>thin or light</note> waters are useful to every living thing, both to animals and to plants. Remember that I have taught <note>original: "docui" (approximated from "vocuire");</note> you sufficiently regarding waters, and I showed you that all waters—whether sweet or bitter—drew their origin from the sea, and I have made a clear demonstration of this to you. Now it must be understood that the lightest and healthiest waters are those that are flowing near cities. When the earth is pure and the banks do not abound in <term>fumosities</term> <note>original: "fumositatibus"; heavy vapors or damp mists thought to carry disease or "heaviness" in medieval science.</note>, the water of that place is light, excellent, and praiseworthy. However, water arising in stony ground and which abounds in "fumosities" is heavy, unhealthy, and harmful. And water in which there are frogs and serpents and other poisonous animals is...

High-ranking nobles <term>proceres</term> <note>original: "proceres"; refers to the leading men or chiefs of the realm.</note> are the expansion and multiplication of the kingdom: through them the court is honored and the kingdom is organized into its various ranks. Therefore, the best organization is found in their ranks and arrangements, so that the status of those nearby and those remote among their number is not hidden. This is a necessary organization and a leadership based on number, which is spoken of and referred to as a number within a number. I say, therefore, that a fourfold organization is necessary: since every place on earth is fourfold in its distinctions: namely, behind and before, right and left. Thus, the divisions of the world are simply four: East, West, South, and North. Let there be, therefore, for each <term>preceptor</term> <note>original: "praeceptori"; a high commander or governor.</note> a fourth part of the kingdom under his rule. And if you wish there to be more, let there be ten, because ten and four are perfect numbers. For within the <term>quaternary</term> <note>original: "quaternario"; the number four. This refers to the Pythagorean idea that 1+2+3+4 = 10.</note> there is one, two, three, and four. If you add these together, they make ten. The number ten <note>original: "denarius numerus"</note> is the perfection of those things which encompass the four and will surround them in counts.

Therefore, ten <term>vicars</term> <note>original: "vicarij"; deputies or lieutenants.</note> follow each preceptor; and ten <term>leaders</term> <note>original: "ductores"</note> follow each vicar; and ten <term>deans</term> <note>original: "decani"; leaders of ten men.</note> follow each leader; and ten men follow each dean. Therefore, all the men in the entire force are 50,000 <note>original: ".L. milia"</note> fighters. Thus, when you require the service of 10,000 men, you shall command one preceptor to be summoned to carry out the expedition; ten vicars will then follow him, and with each vicar will come ten leaders, and with each leader ten deans, who make one thousand. And with each dean will come ten fighting men. They will, therefore, make a total of 10,000 fighters.

If you require one thousand, you shall command one of the vicars; and ten leaders will come with him, with ten deans for each leader, and ten fighters for each dean. These are, in total, one thousand warriors. And if you require one hundred warriors: you shall command one of the leaders, with whom ten deans will come, and with each dean ten fighters, who all together make 100. And if you require ten warriors, you shall command one dean, and ten warriors will come with him. In this way, you will be spared the cost of provisions, your burden will be lightened, and you will achieve what you desire for your purpose. Furthermore, your labor regarding the nobles will be diminished, because...

### Annotation.

You will find wonderful praises of the <term>Quaternary</term> <note>The number four; in Pythagorean thought, the numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4 sum to 10, representing the "Tetractys" or the hidden logic of the universe.</note> in Marsilio Ficino <note>A famous 15th-century Italian scholar who revived Platonism.</note> in his argument on Plato's *Cratylus*. Indeed, that which the author expressly assigns in this place can also be read in Ammonius’s <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> commentary on the *Categories*, where he says: being about to hand down to us the division of things into ten, he first explains their completion in four; for a number beginning from unity, if it is added up as far as four, makes ten: one, two, three, and four, and they become ten.

### Concerning the horn.

**Y**ou ought also to have with you that instrument which Themistius <note>Likely referring to a legendary mechanical or acoustic device; in medieval lore, such "horns" were often attributed to Alexander the Great’s engineers.</note> made for the use of the army and for causing harm. It is a terrifying instrument which is divided in many ways, because you may perhaps need to visit your entire province and your kingdom, and to gather your subjects, your nobles, and your warriors on the same day—or even more quickly, or in another way as a great and numerous army requires. The sound of this instrument can be heard for sixty miles.

## Concerning oxymel and the best remedy for drunkenness.

O Alexander, let it never weary you to take oxymel <note>original: "syrupum acetosum," a mixture of honey and vinegar used as a medical base</note> in the morning on an empty stomach, and also when not fasting: namely, when the humors <note>The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—believed to govern health and personality</note> are restored and abundant, and phlegm dominates, for it is very healing. A wise man indeed praised good wine when he said: it is a wonder how a man can fall ill or die whose food is bread of the finest wheat, and commendable meats, and the drink of a good vine. And he uses them temperately: abstaining from excessive eating, drinking, sexual intercourse, and labor. Or how sickness happens to a man: for it is necessary that he who becomes intoxicated by wine taken beyond measure should wash himself with warm water, and sit by rivers of running water, and have willows and myrtle; and he ought to anoint his body with prepared sandalwood, and fumigate with cold and fragrant incenses. This is indeed the best medicine for drunkenness.

If anyone proposes to entirely give up the drinking of wine, he must not...

...and wooden towers <note>original: "turres ligneas," these were mobile siege towers used to overlook enemy walls</note> with which archers are armed, shooting flaming missiles. And if you see them fearful or losing heart: comfort their hearts and lead them toward perseverance until the end. Also, arrange your battle lines as we have previously set out, as follows. Therefore, station on the right the class of strikers and attackers; on the left, however, the class of spearmen; and in the heart or center, the class of those throwing flaming torches, and those throwing or shooting [arrows], and those making horrible noises and diverse movements. And let the place where you fight the adversaries always be an elevated one; for when they perceive this, they will stand [firm] and strive with insistence. Beware, beware then of betrayal, especially when you see your own men wavering. And toward that part where you see the adversaries wavering, direct your battle line and assault; and with all these things, practice perseverance, for this is the source of victory.

Whence it is said that victory does not come over any people unless they are first conquered by cowardice <note>original: "pusillanimitate," literally "smallness of soul" or faint-heartedness</note> in their own hearts and by the feelings of fear that come upon them. Moreover, multiply your ambushes and station ambushers with terrifying sounds; these are among the things that overcome and conquer. This is a certain precaution that leads to the goal and is one of the foundations of battles. And have specific places prepared within the army's positions to provide help at bridges and other necessary points to protect your leaders. 

Also increase the number of animals carrying fortifications and fighting towers <note>original: "castra," here referring to the wooden structures or "howdahs" placed on the backs of large animals</note>, such as elephants, because they are very terrifying; and very fast animals such as dromedaries <note>One-humped camels prized for their speed in desert warfare</note>, in which there is hope for flight in case of necessity, like a moving fortress. If, however, you must attack those in fortifications, use instruments that throw stones, such as siege engines <note>original: "machinis," a general term for catapults or ballistae</note>, and increase their number according to the urgent need. Add also instruments for piercing and for shooting arrows and poisoned projectiles. And if you can reach the spring from which your enemies draw drinking water, pour various corrupting poisons into those places, and you will dig the graves of your enemies. Observe perseverance in all things, which is greatly commend[ed]...

...commendable; and it is a thing that contributes to your purpose. And you should never pursue a fleeing enemy. Do not hasten in your works beyond measure. ...stay there for a short time. And finally, in the fourth room, you must wash yourself. And when you wish to leave, you should maintain a balanced temperature <note>The OCR reads "odium," likely a typo for "modum," meaning moderation or balance.</note> by staying for a short while <note>original: "mozalum," likely "morulam"</note> in each of the chambers. The bathhouse should be built in a high and windy place; it should have large furnaces that produce great and wide flames, and an abundance of water. One must use scents appropriate to the current season. Specifically, in the spring and summer, use a double, triple, or quadruple amount of ointment; in the autumn and winter, use a double amount.

Once you have completed all these things and have been delicately washed and perfumed, go to the other rooms—but not immediately. Then anoint the body with certain ointments that suit the specific seasons. Then, proceed gradually as we have taught in the preceding sections. Finally, wipe yourself until you are softened.

* If you are thirsty, drink syrup of roses and take an electuary <term>electuary: a medicinal paste or conserve made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term> with musk. Then, stretch your arms out for a little while. Then, after a short pause, take prepared food with tranquility and drink good wine, tempered with as much water as you are accustomed to drink. Do not take too much, but drink in moderation. Then, perfume yourself with incenses suitable for that time. Afterward, rest in fine beds and take a good portion of sleep, for it is very helpful. Then, you should continue the rest of the day in joy and peace. For this is indeed the path of health and of nourishment...

<meta>This page transitions from the preceding physical hygiene instructions to a philosophical discussion on physiognomy. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a famous anecdote about the physician Hippocrates and the physiognomist Philemon, used to justify the study of physical appearances as a window into character.</meta>

...his features <note>The text begins mid-word with "ciones," likely completing "dispositiones" (dispositions) or "complexiones" (physical constitutions) from the previous page.</note>. He looked at the arrangements and physical combinations of the facial features, compared part to part, and said: "This man is lustful, a deceiver, and loves carnal pleasure." Because of this, his followers wanted to kill Philemon, saying: "O fool, this is the face of the worthiest and best man in the whole world!" 

But Philemon calmed them and corrected them, saying: "This is the face of the wise Hippocrates. And what you asked of my science, I have shown; and I have stated what I perceive according to it." Therefore, when they came to Hippocrates, they told him what they had done, and how Philemon had responded and given his judgment. To them Hippocrates said: "Philemon certainly spoke the truth; he did not miss a single letter <note>original: "literam"; an idiom meaning he was perfectly accurate in his reading.</note>." 

"Nevertheless," Hippocrates continued, "since I recognized and considered these traits to be shameful and worthy of rejection, I established my soul as king over them. I withdrew my soul from these urges and triumphed over the appetites of my spirit." This, therefore, is the praise and wisdom found in the works of Hippocrates: for philosophy is nothing other than abstinence and victory over base desires <note>original: "concupiscibilium," referring to the part of the soul driven by desire.</note>. I therefore set down for you rules from this science of <term>physiognomy</term> <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note>, along with brief and sufficient principles, which will grant you great value through the nobility of your nature and the purity of your knowledge.

### Four Annotations

*First.* What the subject of <term>physiognomy</term> might be, to which part of philosophy it may be reduced, what method of investigation it uses, and how it differs from <term>chiromancy</term> <note>original: "Chiromantia," the art of palm reading.</note>. Achellinus <note>Likely Alessandro Achillini (1463–1512), an Italian philosopher and anatomist.</note> and Alexander, in their inquiry concerning the subject of physiognomy and chiromancy, attempt to clarify this. However, Giovanni Francesco Pico <note>Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola (1470–1533), a philosopher known for his skeptical stance toward the "predictive arts."</note> tries to intentionally overthrow this faculty, just as he does all other predictive arts.

*Second.* The conjunction "And" <note>original: "Et"</note>, restored from the most ancient manuscripts, shows that this treatise on physiognomy is a partial section of a larger volume and not a complete book in itself.

<summary>This page recounts a famous anecdote where Hippocrates confirms a physiognomist's unflattering reading of his character, arguing that wisdom consists of using the soul to overcome one's natural base instincts. It also introduces scholarly annotations regarding the definition of physiognomy and its relationship to other sciences like chiromancy.</summary>

<keywords>physiognomy, Hippocrates, Philemon, chiromancy, Alessandro Achillini, Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola, philosophy, self-mastery, character analysis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on physiognomy—the art of reading character from physical features—by providing scholarly citations for the story of Hippocrates and Philemon mentioned on the previous page. It then transitions into a more medical discussion regarding the development of the fetus and the significance of skin color.</meta>

Third. The story which the Author relates concerning Hippocrates and Philemon is recounted almost word-for-word from this same source by Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a prolific medieval philosopher and scientist.</note> in the second tract of his first book *On Animals*. Cicero narrates that a similar thing happened to Zopyrus when he offered a judgment on the image of Socrates, both in his first book *On Fate* and in the fifth book of his *Tusculan Disputations*. Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A famous commentator on Aristotle from the late 2nd century AD.</note> also confirms this in the fourth chapter of his book *On Fate*, saying: "Indeed, when Zopyrus the <term>physiognomist</term> <note>Someone who judges character from facial features.</note> said certain absurd things about the philosopher Socrates—things quite remote from his actual conduct in life—and was ridiculed by Socrates' students for them, Socrates said that Zopyrus had not lied. For he said, 'I was indeed of such a kind by the inclination of my nature, had I not attained a better nature through the study of philosophy.'"

Fourth. While he says that philosophy is nothing other than abstinence and victory over base desires <note>original: "concupiscibilium," referring to the desiring part of the soul.</note>, he seems to suggest that definition of philosophy which the Divine Plato assigned in the *Phaedo*, saying that "Philosophy is the meditation on death." For according to that sense which the definition assigned by our Author carries, it is explained not only by the Greek authors—such as Ammonius, David, Psellus, Blemmida, and Pachymeres, who intentionally interpret six definitions of philosophy—but also by one of our own, far more clearly than all the Greeks: Aurelius Macrobius, in the first of the expositions he left regarding Cicero's *Dream of Scipio*.

### On color.

**Know therefore that the <term>matrix</term> <note>The womb.</note> is to the <term>embryo</term> <note>original: "embuoni," a medieval spelling of embryo.</note> like a boiling pot used in cooking: thus, whiteness, a leaden color, and excessive yellowness are signs of diminished <term>decoction</term> <note>The "cooking" or maturation process of the fetus through internal heat.</note>. Just as this deficiency occurs in the creature, it will likewise be diminished from its own nature.**

### Concerning the Gaze.

<margin>6</margin>
When you see a man who laughs at you frequently, and when you look at him he is frightened and blushes because of this—and especially if he sighs against his will and tears appear in his eyes—this man loves and fears you. If the opposite is true, he is envious and despises you.

### Concerning Balance.

<margin>n</margin>

He should have a <margin>ui</margin> moderate tone of voice, neither too high nor too low. ### Concerning the Hair.

<margin>ba</margin>

### Concerning the Eyebrows.

<margin>3</margin>Eyebrows that have many hairs signify a lack of skill in speaking; and when the eyebrows extend all the way to the temples, the one possessing them is impure. One whose eyebrows are thin, well-proportioned in length and shortness, and large: he is easily impressed and quick to understand.

### Concerning the Nose.

<margin>1</margin>When the nose is thin, its owner is very prone to anger; and he who has a long nose extending toward the mouth is bold and honest. A <term>snub nose</term> <note>original: "nasus simus."</note> indicates an impetuous person; and he whose nostrils are heavily built or hard is quick to anger. When the nose is wide in the middle and slopes toward the tip, the person is wordy and a liar. That nose is more balanced which is of moderate length, and whose width is moderate at the end, and whose nostrils are not very large.

### Concerning the Face.

A flat face lacking any fullness signifies a quarrelsome person of poor complexion, injurious and impure. One who has a face of moderate proportions in the cheeks and temples, leaning toward plumpness, is truthful, loving, intelligent, and wise; or [full of] <unclear>vitality</unclear>.

### Concerning the Voice.

And he who has a deep <note>original: "grossam" (thick or heavy).</note> and sonorous voice is warlike and eloquent; but he whose voice is medium in its thinness and thickness is wise, provident, truthful, and just. ### Concerning the Movement of the Hands.

He who moves frequently and speaks with the motion of his hands is impure, eloquent, and a deceiver. However, he who refrains from moving his hands is perfect in intellect, of good disposition, and of sound counsel.

<meta>This page continues the medieval physiognomic study, which links physical traits to moral and intellectual character. The text transitions from describing the neck and torso into a detailed analysis of the limbs, hands, and feet, concluding with the ideal physical markers of a good memory and balanced nature.</meta>

[...is] proud and lustful <note>original: "amans coitum" (loving sexual intercourse)</note>. A moderate belly and a narrowness of the chest signifies a high intellect and good counsel; however, a broad chest and thickness of the shoulders and back signifies probity <term>probity: moral integrity and honesty</term> and boldness, along with a sound intellect and wisdom. A thinness of the back signifies a man of a discordant nature. A moderate chest and back of good quality is the best and most proven sign. Raised shoulders are a sign of a harsh nature and unfaithfulness.

### Concerning the arms.

When the arms are so long that the hands reach the knees, it signifies boldness along with honesty and generosity. When they are short, it is a sign of one who loves discord and is ignorant.

### Concerning the signs of the palms.

Long palms with long fingers signify that their master is well-disposed toward many arts, especially the mechanical arts <note>In the medieval university system, "mechanical arts" referred to practical, manual crafts such as weaving, navigation, or agriculture, as opposed to the theoretical "liberal arts."</note>, and is wise in his works; it is also a sign of good governance. Thick and short fingers signify foolishness and stupidity.

### Concerning the signs of the feet.

Fleshy and thick feet signify foolishness and a love of causing harm <note>original: "amorem iniurie" (a love of injury/wrongdoing)</note>. Small and light feet signify boldness and strength. Thinness of the shins <note>original: "tibiarum," referring to the lower legs or shins.</note> signifies ignorance, while their thickness signifies boldness and strength. Wide shins and ankles signify physical strength. A great deal of flesh on the knees signifies a weakness of virtue and a certain softness of character.

### Concerning the signs of the steps.

He whose steps are wide and slow will prosper in all his deeds. He whose steps are short is impetuous and suspicious, powerless in his works, and possessed of a bad will.

### Concerning the signs of a good nature.

That man possesses an excellent memory and is well-composed in his nature who has soft and moist flesh, which is a medium between roughness and smoothness, and who is neither too tall nor too short.

<meta>This page concludes a pseudo-Aristotelian treatise on physiognomy, the study of how physical appearance reveals character. It provides a final description of an ideal or specific personality type and then offers a methodological warning to the reader: do not judge based on a single trait. The text ends by distinguishing the general study of the body (physiognomy) from the specific study of hand lines (chiromancy).</meta>

[...he is] pale: turning toward redness: of mild appearance: with hair that is straight and of medium length: with large eyes turning toward roundness: of a medium and measured head: with a neck of proportionate size: well-disposed: whose shoulders decline a little: lacking fleshiness in the legs and knees: of a clear voice with a balance between thinness and thickness: with long palms and long fingers turning toward thinness: of moderate laughter and bowing <note>original: "fletionis" (bending or bowing); in this context, it likely refers to physical gestures of humility or the way one carries their body while laughing.</note> and derision: whose appearance is as if mixed with joy and pleasantness.

## Concerning the general rule in the aforementioned signs.

However, you must not hasten your sentence and your judgment based on only one of these signs: but rather collect the testimonies of all the signs together: and when they give you diverse and conflicting signs: always incline toward the better and more probable part. <note>The author encourages a holistic approach, warning the reader to look for a "preponderance of evidence" rather than relying on a single physical quirk.</note>

</margin>

In this Physiognomy, the Author covers physiognomic signs from the head down to the feet, except for the lines of the hands. This omission should by no means be suspected to have happened by accident from such a great philosopher, as Acuillinus <note>Giovanni Aquila (Johannes Aquillinus), a 15th-century physician and writer who commented on Aristotle.</note> asserted; especially since in the first book of the *History of Animals* <note>Aristotle’s foundational work of biology.</note> he mentioned the lines of chiromancy <term>chiromancy: the practice of palm reading or interpreting the lines of the hand</term> saying, "the palm is distinguished by incisions as indicators of life." The same is found in the 35th of the *Problems*, the tenth problem, and also the 49th problem of the tenth book of *Problems*. For Physiognomy, as declared in this treatise, does not include the lines of the hands for one contemplating Chiromancy, but is distinguished from it.

---

->Here ends the Physiognomy of Aristotle to Alexander.<-

# OF ALEXANDER THE GREAT TO
## Aristotle, concerning the wonders of India.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'S' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate foliage and scrollwork typical of the early 16th-century press.</image-desc>

ALWAYS mindful of you, even amidst the uncertain dangers of our wars, my most dear teacher <note>original: "charissime pręceptor"</note>, and most welcome to me after my mother and my sisters. Since I knew you were devoted to philosophy, I thought I should write to you about the regions of India, the state of the heavens, and the countless kinds of serpents, men, and wild beasts, so that through the knowledge of new things, something might be added to your study and genius. Although in you wisdom is perfected, and your system of teaching—which is appropriate for both the matter at hand and for your age and future times—demands no help, nevertheless, so that you might know the deeds of the one you love, and so that you might not be without knowledge of anything unusual, I thought I should write to you of the things I saw in India, gathered through the greatest labors and dangers of the Macedonians. For these things are worthy of memory, collected in many and various ways, just as I witnessed them. I would not have believed there were so many wonders <note>original: "prodigia"</note> had I not first weighed them all myself, presented before these very eyes. The land is wondrous; it is as much a mother of good things as it is a scorner and patient common bearer of evil things, of wild beasts, fruits, metals, and animals. If a man were permitted to behold all these things, names themselves would scarcely keep pace with such a variety of objects.

But I shall speak of these things which I first learned, taking care that I am not found worthy of fables or base lies. You are also not ignorant of the nature of my mind, of which you were the teacher: that I am accustomed to guard so much fairness and to speak of everything more sparingly than they were actually performed. And so, I hope you recognize that I assert nothing as one chasing or boasting of the glory of our warfare—which I wish had been less laborious for us, so that it would not have been necessary to learn through the experience of so many things. I give thanks to the courage of the Macedonian youths and to our invincible army, which, through my patience, has persevered so that [the wealth] of kings [and] of things...

...should be called. Having sent this title of mine to you, my dearest teacher <note>original: "charissime pręceptor"</note>, I do not doubt that you will rejoice. I would fail in my duty and stray from the devotion I owe to both you and myself if I did not write to you—just as I do to my mother Olympias and my sisters—concerning the individual advantages of my kingdom, which I consider to be shared between you and them. If I did not do so, you would seem to judge us too simply or poorly. In my previous letters, I had informed you about the eclipses of the sun and moon, and about the movements of the stars and the signs in the sky; these I sent to you arranged with great care. To these I will now add these new accounts, committing everything to paper to expand the history.

Whatever you read here, I know they are such things that it was fitting for your Alexander’s care to embrace. In the month of May, the Persian King Darius was defeated at the river Ganges <note>The medieval text inaccurately places the defeat of Darius III at the Ganges; historically, his final defeat by Alexander was at Gaugamela, far to the west of the Ganges</note>. I have already informed you in a previous letter how we received the surrender of that whole region and established our governors and administrators over the provinces of the East, enriched with much royal wealth. Now, so that I may not be repetitive in my writing, I pass over those prior deeds which are already known.

At the end of the month of July, we arrived at Phasiace <note>a legendary or corrupted place-name in the Alexander Romance tradition</note> in India. There, after King Porus was defeated with wondrous speed, we gained immense riches and were filled with the treasures of his palace. But so that you may know the things that seemed to me worthy of memory, I recall certain details; and because it was so memorable, it seemed right to me to describe his innumerable army. In it, besides the masses of soldiers, there were sixteen thousand and eight hundred scythed chariots <note>war chariots equipped with blades on the wheels</note>, and four hundred elephants <note>original: "caprifisque elephantis" — a curious term, possibly a corruption referring to the size or type of the beasts</note> which carried towers placed upon them, containing armed men <note>original: "incularouibus" — likely a corruption for "inhabitants" or "soldiers"</note>. We invaded the royal city of Porus and his palace by force of arms. In it, we counted four hundred and forty solid golden columns of immense thickness and thirty feet in height, with their capitals. The walls were gilded with plates the thickness of a finger. When I try to estimate their value compared to other places, I am at a loss for words. I marveled at a vine made of solid gold with golden branches hanging between the columns. On it were golden leaves, and clusters of crystal and fiery-red grapes were interspersed, set apart by emeralds. The chambers and beds also, all of which were decorated with mar- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "garitis" completes the word "margaritis," meaning pearls</note>

...were adorned with pearls <note>original: "margaritis"</note>, large single pearls <note>original: "unionibus"</note>, and carbuncles <note>glowing red gems, likely rubies or garnets</note>. The ivory doors shone with a wonderful whiteness, and the ebony paneled ceilings gleamed. There were also vaulted ceilings <note>original: "testudinibus"</note> of cypress wood; laurel trees were placed in prominent spots, and in the baths, there was solid [gold]. Golden foliage, along with golden mixing bowls and statues and countless treasures, adorned the doors and walls of the house; innumerable designs in various colors wandered across the surfaces. Among the golden plants were statues with claws and beaks, wearing gilded tunics, earrings, and coverings that bore pearls and large single pearls. We also found many vessels for drinking and for garlands <note>original: "sertaria"</note>, as well as many others made of gems, crystal, and amber <note>original: "electrina"</note>; we found many golden items and rare silver ones.

Having brought these things under my power, I pursued Porus. Desiring to see interior India, I had reached the Caspian Gates <note>In the Alexander Romance, geography is often legendary; these "gates" represent a pass into the furthest reaches of the East</note> with my entire army. While I was admiring the prosperity of these most fertile regions, I discovered certain places worthy of note, being carried away with joy. However, the inhabitants of that region had warned us beforehand that we might fall among serpents and kinds of rabid wild beasts, which lived in great numbers in these valleys, plains, forests, and mountains, hidden in the thickets of the groves and fields.

But I, in order to first overtake Porus as he fled from the battle before he could retreat into the desert wilderness at the end of the world, preferred to choose shorter and safer routes. Therefore, having taken three hundred and fifty <note>original: "Tercentisquinquaginta"</note> guides who knew the shortcuts of the routes, I set out in the month of August through sands burning with the sun and places parched of moisture. I promised rewards to these men, who were skilled in the regions and were leading us through unknown places of India, if they would bring me with my army unharmed into Batriace <note>possibly a corruption of Bactria</note> and deep into the territory of the hidden Seres <note>The "Seres" were the people of the Silk Road, often identified with the Chinese; Europeans once believed they harvested silk from trees</note>. This nation makes clothes by brushing down a downy fuzz from the leaves of trees to create a "forest wool." But those guides, showing more favor to the enemy than to themselves, wished to betray us. They were proceeding to lead us, who were ignorant of the regions, into deadly kinds of serpents and rabid wild beasts and monsters.

That they had planned this was evident from the events themselves. When I, seeing that this had happened on my part—I who had rejected the <unclear>many</unclear> somber warnings of my friends and of the Caspian men who...

<meta>This page continues the legendary letter of Alexander the Great to his tutor Aristotle. Following the descriptions of the opulent Indian palaces, Alexander details his march into the interior, his army's immense wealth, and the beginning of their trials in the desert.</meta>

They had warned me not to hasten so quickly to conquer, lest the victory be snatched away from me without any warning <note>original: "nullo-vō lo", likely "nullo dolo" (by no deceit) or "nullo solo" (on no ground/suddenly)</note>. Therefore, I commanded all the soldiers that they should follow the column clad in their armor.

¶ Because they were carrying with them no small amount of plunder, consisting of gold and the loot of pearls <note>original: "Margaritarum"</note>, it was to be feared that the enemies might secretly lie in ambush for the victors to take back their belongings. Indeed, the soldiery was so enriched that they could scarcely carry the weight of the gold. There was also the significant weight of the arms: all of which I had encased in golden plates, so that the entire column followed in it, as if bright with starlight and splendor, with radiant gold and insignia, with standards and banners. It was a spectacle among diversities to behold such an army, because it stood out among all others for its decoration as much as its strength. 

I certainly, looking back at my good fortune and the remarkable number of youth, was refreshed with immense joy. But as fortune usually clamors against things in prosperity: it happened that we suffered from thirst. When we could now scarcely endure it, a soldier named Reuerus <note>In other versions of this legend, this soldier is often named Zephyrus</note> brought to me water found in a hollow stone in a golden helmet; he himself was thirsty, but looking out for my life more than his own. Having called the army, I poured it out openly so that the soldiers would not begin to thirst more while I drank. Having praised Reuerus's benevolence toward me, I decorated him with worthy gifts. 

This act made the army more tranquil in spirit, and I resumed the journey begun; not far off in the desert places a river appeared to me, whose banks were covered with reeds sixty feet <note>original: "sexagenorum"</note> high. These reeds were pliant <note>original: "vinosum", likely "vimosum" meaning willow-like or pliant</note>, yet surpassed oaks in thickness; the inhabitants used this wonderful material for constructing buildings. 

Then I, since the beasts of burden and the army were thirsty, immediately ordered the camp to be set up by the surveyors. While it was being set up, I myself, desiring to relieve my thirst, tasted the water of the river, which was more bitter than hellebore <term>elleboro; a plant famous in antiquity for its intense bitterness and use as a purgative</term>. Neither man could <unclear>drink</unclear> it nor any beast swallow it without torment. It distressed me more for the sake of the mute animals than for our own need, knowing man to be more durable in all things than cattle. For indeed, there were about a thousand elephants of huge size who were carrying the gold; there were four hundred...
