All the four-horse chariots were scythed <note>original: "falcate"; these were war chariots equipped with curved blades on the wheel axles to mow down enemy infantry</note>: moreover, there were one thousand two hundred two-horse chariots. There were two hundred troops of cavalry and seventy thousand infantry. There were two thousand camp mules under harness; around two thousand for carrying the baggage; moreover, there were two thousand dromedary camels and water buffalo, and two thousand yoked beasts and oxen which carried the grain. A huge number of herds for the purpose of providing daily meat followed behind. Furthermore, the luxury of our victories had allowed us golden bridles even upon the horses, mules, camels, and elephants. 

But then, the very animals, unappeased <note>original: "inexpiata", here meaning their thirst was unquenched</note> by water, could scarcely contain themselves. The soldiers also, now by licking their iron tools <note>likely to catch condensation or for the cooling sensation of the metal</note>, now by tasting oil, were trying in some way to delay or take away their dire thirst. We even saw many who, setting aside their shame, were drinking their own urine, tormented by the ultimate necessity. This matter tormented me in two ways: first, I was more anxious about the state of the army than about my own danger.

I ordered, however, that they should follow the column in arms: I declared a law that I would punish anyone who was caught in the battle line not dressed in his proper insignia and arms. At which they themselves wondered: why was it necessary, where no enemy appeared, to march in arms in such great thirst? But I knew that our journey was through places full of beasts and serpents <note>Alexander's letters often emphasize the monstrous wildlife of the East</note>, and I acted lest we be broken by a sudden danger, and lest we be surrounded unawares or struck by an unexpected peril. 

Following the bank of the river, therefore, at the eighth hour of the day <note>approximately 2:00 PM</note>, we reached a town which was built in the middle of the river on an island made of those reeds which we described a little earlier <note>these "reeds" were said to be as large as trees in the Alexander legends</note>. There we noticed a few half-naked Indian men, who, when they saw us, immediately hid themselves within the roofs of their dwellings. I desired their presence so that they might show sweet water to us who were ignorant of the land. When no one appeared, I ordered a few arrows to be shot into the city, so that if they were unwilling to come out of their own accord, they might be brought forth by the fear of war. Then, with everyone hidden even more because of fear, and no one appearing for a long time, I sent twelve soldiers from the Macedonians with light arms to swim across the river. And so...

### The Hazards of the River and the Great Thirst

They had swum across a fourth part of the river when a horrifying thing to behold was suddenly seen by us: hippopotamuses <note>original: "hyppopotami"</note>, larger than the bodies of elephants, rushed out from among the deep whirlpools of the waters. I was then angry at the guides who were leading us into these traps, and I ordered one hundred and fifty of them to be thrown into the river. Once these men had been pushed in and were swimming, the hippopotamuses rushed them in turn and inflicted a just punishment upon them. But as an even greater

# The Night of the Serpents

The **elephants** <note>Alexander’s army famously encountered war elephants in India, but here they are kept as prized assets in the center of the camp.</note> were stationed in the middle of the camp, so that they could be more easily controlled if any nighttime accident, panic, or new commotion should arise. When fifteen hundred fires had been lit around the outside—for the forest provided as much wood as we desired—and then at the signal of the trumpet, I ordered the soldiers to begin taking their food. About two thousand golden lamps were lit. 

At the first rising of the shining moon, suddenly, with the stingers of their tails raised, countless **scorpions** flocked toward the camp from the open lands of India. It was uncertain whether they were drawn by our commotion or by thirst, as they were seeking their usual watering place. They were, however, quite ready to cause harm. These marvels were followed by a sudden, immense multitude of snakes appearing along the path, marked by various colors. For some had reddish scales; some were black or white; and others appeared to look like gold. 

The entire region hissed, causing us no small amount of fear. But immediately, we formed a wall with the camp's shields and held long spears in our hands; we skewered these evil pests with the sharpest points of our spears, and at times we killed even more of them with fire.

This business kept us anxious in this labor for nearly two hours. Once they had drunk their fill of water, the milder snakes began to leave; but the larger ones sought out our shelters <note>original: "latebras"</note> with great excitement.

Around the third hour of the night <note>approximately 9:00 PM</note>, just as we were hoping for some rest, crested **Indian snakes** with two or three heads—somewhat larger than the thickness of columns—emerged from the nearby mountain caves to drink water. They wore down the ground with their scales as they moved, and they flicked their jaws with triple-forked tongues, their chests raised high and their eyes sparkling with venom. Their very appearance was deadly. We fought with these for no more than one hour, with thirty of his own <note>likely referring to personal attendants or servants</note> and twenty thousand <note>original: "viginti milibus"; this is likely an exaggeration common in the "Alexander Romance" literature, or a scribal error for "militibus" (soldiers).</note> of our men lost. I begged the **Macedonians** not to yield to these misfortunes, nor to lose heart in such dangers, even though their endurance was already being pushed to the limit. I myself drove everyone to the task.

After the snakes, a massive multitude of crabs, the color of saffron <note>original: "croco-" - the text cuts off here, likely describing "crocotis," a saffron or yellow color.</note>...

<summary>Alexander the Great describes a terrifying night in an Indian camp where his army is attacked by hordes of scorpions and multi-headed giant snakes. Despite staggering losses, Alexander exhorts his Macedonian troops to maintain their courage against these monstrous threats.</summary>

...covered in the skins of those creatures, they came to the camp. These monsters, armored and resistant to iron, were seen in large numbers by the fires; they eventually threw themselves into the pond. By now, we were weary from our watches, and at the fifth hour of the night, the trumpet signaled that it was time to rest. 

But then there appeared **white lions**, comparable in size to bulls, let out a great roar and shook their upright necks; with their manes held high like lightning bolts, they charged at us. They rushed upon our waiting hunting spears <note>original: "venabulis"</note>, and such a sudden uproar arose while the dark night howled. No less terrifying were the **bears** of immense size, fearsome with their bristling fur like a palisade, mixed with spotted lynxes and tigers; and battles were joined with horrible panthers. This was a plague unlike any other. 

Furthermore, an immense multitude of **bats**, equal in size to pigeons, flew into our mouths and faces; they possessed teeth like those of humans, with which they ground up the limbs of the soldiers. 

One beast of a new kind also appeared, smaller than an elephant but armed with three horns on its forehead, which the Indians are accustomed to call the **Odontotyranos** <note>literally "Tooth-Tyrant"; a legendary rhinoceros-like creature from medieval bestiaries.</note>. It bore a head like a horse and was black in color. Without even pausing to drink the water, it stared at the camp and suddenly charged us. It was not slowed down by the intense heat of the fires we had built. When I deployed a unit of Macedonians to withstand it, the beast killed about twenty men and trampled fifty-five more, leaving them useless for combat. Yet the beast itself was finally killed by the right hands of the soldiers with their spears. 

Then, before the time of dawn, pests came from the sky, white as snow and shaped like frogs; with them, **Indian mice** as large as foxes entered the camp. The pack animals, when wounded by their bite, died immediately, though the same bite was not fatal to humans. 

As light approached, however, the **night-ravens** <note>original: "noctucoraces," likely referring to a type of large owl or mythical bird.</note> arrived. These birds had faces similar to humans, but exceeded them in body size. They were tawny in color with black feet and beaks. They crowded the bank of the pond, causing us no harm; instead, they pulled the usual fish out with their talons and ate them. We did not dare to scare or drive these birds away, and they left our sight of their own accord.

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter of Alexander the Great to his teacher Aristotle, moving from the miraculous and terrifying beasts of the Indian wilderness to the human conflicts of his campaign. It recounts the punishment of treacherous guides and Alexander’s famous ruse where he disguised himself to spy on King Porus.</meta>

I ordered those most wicked men who were leading us into traps to be punished by the breaking of their legs <note>original: "crurifragio," a brutal Roman and ancient method of execution or crippling</note> and to be consumed alive at night while still breathing by the serpents, just as they had wished to consume us. I also commanded that their hands be bound together <note>original: "confingi," likely meaning pierced or shackled</note> so that they might undergo a punishment well-deserved for their deeds. Then, having held an assembly <note>original: "contione," a formal address to the troops</note> to encourage my soldiers to be brave and not to yield like women to adverse misfortunes, I suddenly ordered the camp to be moved at the sound of trumpets toward the gusts of a new wind. There, having learned of new wars being conspired by the gathered madness <note>likely "furiis," meaning the frantic rage or frenzy</note> of the Indian barbarians, I gave orders to my soldiers, who were of great spirit, for whom a history of victories and good fortune provided abundance.

Therefore, having left those dangerous places behind, we finally trod a protected path and arrived in the lands of the **Bactrians** <note>a region in modern-day Afghanistan and Uzbekistan, famous in antiquity for its wealth</note>, rich in gold and wealth. We were received with kind provisions, as they bordered the territory of the Persians. I decided to stay twenty days to repair the camp for war. After eight days had passed, followed by a journey of four days, we arrived where **Porus** <note>the Indian king who famously fought Alexander at the Hydaspes</note> had settled with his gathered army, having decided to personally submit to my rule through battle. 

For Porus openly granted us the opportunity for provisions, not behaving like an enemy. Eager to know me, he repeatedly questioned my soldiers as they went back and forth, asking where I was or what I was doing. Since they gave uncertain answers—and because all things were reported to me, the Great King of the Macedonians—I donned a soldier's uniform. Setting aside my royal attire, I entered his camp as if I were a buyer of wine and meat. Porus, inquiring about current events, asked me what Alexander was doing or what his age might be. Mocking him with a lie about my age, I said, "Like an old man, our leader warms himself in his tent by a lit fire."

Then he became lively with joy, because he thought he was about to engage in war with a decrepit old man, while he himself was young and puffed up by rumor. "Why then," he said, "does he not respect his own age?" I replied with the same purpose that I did not know what Alexander was doing, since I was merely a common soldier from the Macedonian army. Then he handed me a letter full of threats, promising a reward if I delivered it to King Alexander. Swearing to him...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Alexander the Great’s campaign in India. Following the previous page’s account of his encounter with King Porus, Alexander describes his reaction to Porus's correspondence and his subsequent exploration of the easternmost reaches of the known world, where he encounters legendary monuments and terrifying wildlife.</meta>

...it was dreadful to think that these letters might fall into his hands. Having returned to the camp, before I had even read it—and certainly after I read the letter—I dissolved into great laughter. I have sent a copy of it to you, to my mother, and to my sisters, so that you may marvel at the pride and the now-humbled audacity <note>original: "temeritatem," implying a reckless or foolhardy boldness</note> of the barbarian. 

I proceeded toward the East from the Indian villages, and having overcome them just as I wished, I established the kingdoms snatched from **Porus** by force of arms. When this unexpected honor <note>Alexander famously restored Porus to his throne as a tributary king after defeating him</note> was granted to him, he revealed his treasures to me, which I did not know existed. From these riches, he enriched me, my **Companions** <note>original: "Comites," the elite inner circle and cavalry of the Macedonian army</note>, and the entire army. Having turned from an enemy into a friend, he led the Macedonians to the monuments <note>original: "trophea," victory markers or memorials</note> of **Hercules** and **Liber** <note>the god Bacchus or Dionysus; ancient tradition held that these gods had conquered India in the mythical past</note>. 

In the furthest reaches of the East, golden statues of both gods had been set up. Desiring to know whether they were solid gold, I bored into them. When I saw that they were indeed solid, I filled the holes with the same metal. I then appeased Liber and Hercules by slaying sacrificial victims. 

Intending to progress further to see if we might perceive anything else memorable, we heard of nothing except deserted plains reaching toward the **Ocean** <note>the Greeks believed a single world-encircling Great Ocean surrounded all landmasses</note>, as well as forests and mountains in which elephants and serpents were said to dwell. I was then advancing toward the sea, wishing if I could to sail around the whole world. Behold, I came upon a river which the inhabitants of those places affirmed was dark and full of shallows. They claimed that the crossing should be attempted by no one, as not even the daring of Hercules or Liber, the most excellent of gods, had ventured there.

Wishing all the more to seem greater than them, and desiring to follow those sacred footsteps with the endurance of a mortal, I—having been honored and praised there—decided to scrutinize the left-hand part of India. I did this so that nothing in those unknown places would be hidden from me; King Porus did not object, lest he seem to be hiding the secret riches of his kingdom.

There was a dry marsh, abundant with mud, through which, when I attempted a passage, we encountered a beast of a new kind. It had a serrated back and two heads: one resembled the **Moon** <note>possibly referring to a crescent shape or a pale, glowing quality</note> with the chest of a **hippopotamus**, while the other was most similar to a **crocodile** and armed with hard teeth. With one blow, this head suddenly killed two soldiers. We could hardly crush the creature even with iron hammers, and we were unable to pierce it with clubs. We marveled for a long time at its strange nature. From there, we arrived at the furthest forests of the Indians, where, when the camp...

<meta>This page continues Alexander the Great's firsthand account of his travels in the East. Having just visited the monuments of Hercules and Bacchus with King Porus, Alexander now describes the logistical challenges of the march and the strange creatures they encountered at the edges of the world.</meta>

...I established a camp nearly fifty stadia <note>an ancient unit of distance; fifty stadia is roughly 5.7 miles</note> in length and width. 

We had begun to want to remain near the river **Buemar**, and just before nightfall, at the eleventh hour of the day, scouts and common soldiers all arrived at once to announce that we should take up arms with great speed: for immense herds of elephants were coming out of the woods to overwhelm the camp. I therefore ordered the **Thessalian** <note>original: "thecalis," likely a corruption of "Thessalis," the elite cavalry from northern Greece</note> horsemen to mount their horses and to take with them those whom we knew were trained to meet the charge of elephants if the beasts became fearful. I then ordered others armed with spears to follow the cavalry, and commanded all the trumpeters to advance in the front line on horseback; all the infantry were to remain in the camp. 

I myself, proceeding with **King Porus** and the cavalry, saw swarms of beasts stretching their trunks <term>promuscidibus|trunks</term> towards us. Their backs were black, white, red, and some were of various other colors. **King Porus** affirmed to us that it was the custom of these beasts to crush soldiers in war, but that they could easily be turned away if the horsemen did not stop striking them. Without delay, the trembling elephants were turned back; many more than in the previous battle began to seek flight. Astonished by the trumpets of the men and the grunting of pigs <note>it was a common ancient belief that elephants were terrified of the high-pitched squealing of pigs</note>, the cavalry pursued them, and by hamstringing them, we killed about nine hundred and eighty. Having deceived the beasts with the horsemen, I returned to camp burdened with remarkable spoils.

I then ordered the rampart to be surrounded with shields and breastplates, lest any injury be brought at night by the violence of elephants or other wild beasts. Soon all was quiet until dawn. Then, at the first light of day, we set out into other regions of **India**. In an open field, we saw men and women who were hairy in the manner of wild beasts, naked over their whole bodies, and eight feet tall. The Indians call these **fauns**. These were more accustomed to rivers than to land, living only on raw fish and draughts of water. When we wished to approach them, they submerged themselves in the whirlpools of the nearby **Esigmariti** river. 

We then found woods full of **Cynocephali** <note>literally "dog-heads"; a legendary race of people with the heads of dogs</note>, who tried to provoke us and then fled after shooting arrows. We found those places to be deserted; the Indians reported that nothing else worthy of sight remained further on.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Alexander the Great’s letter to his teacher, Aristotle, regarding his campaigns in India. On the previous page, Alexander’s army had successfully fended off a massive herd of elephants using trumpets and the squealing of pigs.</meta>

Therefore, I ordered the military standards <note>original: "signa"; these were the banners carried at the front of a legion to signify its direction</note> to be turned back toward the west, so that we might pitch camp near a watering hole twelve miles away. All the tents were properly erected and large fires were lit. Suddenly, a wind of such great force—the East Wind—arose that it threw all the tents and the headquarters into confusion and overturned them. We were greatly stunned; the pack animals were harassed even more violently by flying sparks and firebrands, which burned their backs as they landed. Then horror seized the soldiers, for this happened at night; they feared it was the wrath of the gods because the month of October and the autumn season were pressing upon us. Scarcely at last, having gathered our baggage once more, we found a campsite in a more open valley. 

With all things set in order, I commanded the soldiers to eat their dinner. For the blowing of the East wind had ceased, and a great cold was increasing as evening fell. Soon, snow began to fall in the manner of wool fleeces <note>a common ancient metaphor for thick, heavy snowflakes</note>. Fearing that the snow would pile up and bury the camp, I ordered the soldiers to trample it down, primarily so that the snow would melt under the constant movement of their feet. The fires, which were almost extinguished by the snow, were kept burning as much as possible. 

One thing, however, was our salvation: in a single moment, these snows were washed away by a heavy rain. This was immediately followed by a dark cloud, and burning clouds were seen falling from the sky like torches, so that the whole plain burned with their fire. The soldiers feared to say that the wrath of the gods was weighing on us because I, a mere man, had attempted to surpass the tracks of Hercules and **Liber** <note>the Roman name for the Greek god Dionysus; both Hercules and Dionysus were said to have campaigned in India before Alexander</note>. I ordered the soldiers to use torn clothes to beat back the fires. A clear night was immediately returned to us as we prayed. Then the fires were lit anew, and the men ate their meal in safety. 

For three continuous days without bright sun, a great misfortune <note>original: "noria," likely a corruption of "noxa" (harm) or "moria" (death)</note> befell us as a threatening cloud hung over us. After I had buried about fifty soldiers who had perished in the snow, I ordered the camp to be moved. We saw high promontories reaching to the sky near the Ocean in **Ethiopia** <note>in the ancient world, "Ethiopia" was often used vaguely to describe lands at the edge of the world, including parts of India</note>. We also saw the **Ethnean** mountains <note>original: "ethneos"; likely a reference to volcanic peaks similar to Mount Etna</note> at the cave <term>antrum|cave or grotto</term> of **Liber**, where we sent those who were lost. It was said that those who entered the cave of the god would die of a fever on the third day. We proved this to be true by their deaths, because I entered despite the religious prohibitions and without bringing gifts...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Alexander the Great’s legendary letter to his teacher, Aristotle. After traveling for ten days through difficult terrain, Alexander arrives at the site of the legendary Trees of the Sun and the Moon.</meta>

...about to see wonderful spectacles among the discoveries, we took up the journey led by unlearned old men. To guide us, they led us through trackless wastes, lacking and in want of water, and through so many places full of serpents and wild beasts, all the way to the nearby seat of the oracle. Concerning these wild beasts and serpents, I did not think I should write to you, because they were innumerable and were called by Indian names.

When we approached the region we sought, we saw some women and men covered in the skins of panthers and tigers. When we asked them what kind of people they were, they said in their own language that they were Indians. The place was generous with incense and abounded with an immense amount of <term>balsam (opobalsamum)</term> <note>A fragrant, resinous juice highly prized in antiquity for medicine and perfume</note>, which grew plentifully on the branches of their groves; and the inhabitants of that region were accustomed to eat it. 

When we came upon the aforementioned sanctuary, known to many, the priest of the oracle appeared. He was ten feet tall, with a black body and canine teeth <note>original: "dentibus caninis," likely meaning prominent fangs</note>. His ears were pierced, and from them hung <term>pearls (uniones)</term> <note>The Latin term "unio" refers to a large, unique, and very high-quality pearl</note>. He was dressed in skins. After he had greeted us according to his custom, he began to ask why I had come. I said that I was desiring to see the sacred trees of the Sun and the Moon. 

Then the barbarian said: "If you are free from sexual contact with boys or the touch of women, you shall enter the divine place." He then commanded my friends and fellow soldiers who were with me—about three hundred in number—to lay aside their rings and clothes, along with their shoes. I obeyed the man in all things, and we complied with the religious practice. At the eleventh hour of the day, the priest waited for the setting of the sun. For he affirmed that the Tree of the Sun speaks and gives answers in the morning at the first rising of the <note>original: "iubaris," referring to the radiance or first light of the sun</note> rays. 

Likewise, he told that the Tree of the Moon kept the same times during the night. This matter seemed to me more like a lie than the truth. Therefore, I began to walk through the entire grove, which was within an enclosure built with modest craftsmanship. I saw balsam with a wonderful scent flowing most abundantly from all the branches of the trees; captivated by this scent, I myself, while walking, began to pluck some from the bark, and my friends did the same. In the middle of the place, however, the sacred trees stood, with various kinds of foliage surrounding them. These trees...

<meta>This page continues Alexander the Great’s account of his journey to the Trees of the Sun and the Moon. After being vetted by the priest for ritual purity, Alexander and his companions are led into the sacred grove.</meta>

The trees were one hundred feet high: and the Indians call them <term>belbionas</term> <note>original: "belbionas", a local Indian name for these legendary oracular trees</note>. While I was marvelling at them and saying that they had grown to such a size because of frequent rains, the priest for a second time affirmed to me that never in these places does rain fall, nor does any wild beast, bird, or even a serpent approach. He affirmed that those trees were consecrated in ancient times by the ancestors of the Indians to the Sun and the Moon, and that they are moved to shed most abundant tears during an eclipse of the sun or moon, fearing for the state of their deities. When I prepared to perform a sacrifice and offer victims, I was forbidden by the priest, who stated it was not permitted for either <term>incense</term> <note>original: "thus"</note> to be burned in that sanctuary or for any animal to be killed; instead, he commanded me to kneel and kiss the trunks of the trees, and to pray to the Sun and Moon that they might give me truthful answers.

Therefore, when I was about to do this, I thought the priest should be asked whether the trees would answer me in the Indian or Greek tongue. He then replied that the Tree of the Sun announces future events in both languages, while the Tree of the Moon begins in the Greek speech and ends in the Indian. While these things were being done, we saw from the west the tops of the trees struck by the rays of <term>Phoebus</term> <note>The Greek god of the sun, here representing the setting sun's light</note> with a gleaming mane. The priest said loudly: "Look up, all of you, and let each man think in hidden silence of the matters he wishes to consult. Let no one speak openly." Then I, my friends, and my fellow soldiers looked more closely amidst the density of the groves, lest some person might elude us through some deception. Seeing no trickery intervening at the tops and branches of the trees, as we stood close by, divine oracles occupied our ears. For I was wondering if, having conquered the whole world, I might return in triumph to my homeland, to my mother <term>Olympias</term> <note>The mother of Alexander the Great</note> and my dearest sisters. Suddenly, in a very thin Indian voice, the tree replied:

> Alexander, invincible in wars, as you have determined, you shall be the sole lord of the whole world. But you shall never return alive to your homeland again, for the fates have so decreed concerning your life.

Hearing these things, I was struck to the heart; for it displeased me that I had brought so many people with me to the sacred trees. At the same time, my friends and the companions who were with me wept greatly; and

<meta>This page continues Alexander the Great’s narrative of his visit to the oracular Trees of the Sun and the Moon in India. Following the previous page, where Alexander was instructed on how to approach the trees, he now receives a grim prophecy regarding his death.</meta>

They were saddened; I consoled them partly with threats and partly with gifts, and I commanded them to tell these answers to no one.

Moreover, being about to hear the oracles of the moon again—since the priest affirmed it could happen at midnight when the moon was due to rise—I took with me three most faithful friends: Perdiccas, Arrizanas, and Philotas. I feared no one in that place, nor did anyone fear me, for it was forbidden by divine law for anyone to be killed there.

Entering the place again, we stood near the sacred trees and soon worshiped according to the usual custom. I then consulted the oracle as to where I was destined to die. At the first rising of the moon, as its light struck the <term>horn</term> <note>original: "cornu"; likely referring to the tips of the crescent moon or a horn-like branch of the tree</note> and its brilliance was received, the tree replied in Greek:

"Alexander," it said, "you have already reached the full limit of your life. But in the ninth month of the following year, you shall die in Babylon. And you will be deceived by the one from whom you least expect it."

Then I burst into tears, and my friends standing around me wept too. I did not believe any deceit or crime would arise from them; rather, I believed they were ready to die for my safety. Nor did I ask the oracle anything further about my faithful friends, as if the oracle might warn Alexander to beware of them.

After departing, we returned to our meal. Sick at heart, I went to rest. My friends begged me not to afflict myself with anxiety or fasting, so I forced myself to eat a little food against my will. To be ready for the first rising of the sun, I settled myself in the sanctuary. On the following day, I woke at early dawn and roused my friends, who were still half-asleep. The priest himself was still resting, wrapped in the skins of wild animals. Before him on a table was the fire for a libation, left over from the previous night's meal, and an ivory knife; for they lack bronze, iron, lead, and silver, but they abound in gold. They nourish themselves on <term>balsam</term> <note>original: "oppobalsamo"; a fragrant resin highly prized in antiquity</note> and incense.

These people drink pure water falling in a stream from a nearby mountain. They live by reclining and resting without any pillows, laying only on the skins of wild beasts; they live clothed and <unclear>covered</unclear> <note>original: "conterti"</note> by these. Therefore, once the priest was awakened, I entered the place for the third time to consult the oracle...

...the most sacred tree of the sun, asking whose assassin's hand I should beware, and indeed what end my mother and my sisters were destined to have. The tree spoke in Greek:

"If I were to reveal the plotter of your death to you, you would easily change the looming fates once he was removed. For the three sisters—whose oracles I speak—would be angry with me if I were to weigh and hinder their work: Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos <note>The three Fates of Greek mythology: the Spinner, the Allotter, and the Inflexible one who cuts the thread of life.</note>. Therefore, in a year and seven months, you will die in Babylon; not by the sword, as you suspect; not by gold, nor silver, nor any metal, but by poison. But the priest also warned us to depart, saying that the sacred trees were offended by our weeping and wailing. Then I immediately told all the soldiers that we should return to Porus at the Phasiaces, and I repeated the response to them—claiming it would be auspicious and happy for us <note>Alexander conceals the grim prophecy of his death to keep his troops from losing heart.</note>. Oh, at that time of my life, I did this so that I would not be driven to despair by my fellow soldiers and destroyed in foreign places. 

And these were the ones who heard the voices of the oracle along with me, who are called by these names: Sermition son of Presitis, and Mistouinis, and Eburotes, and Lacon, and Thrasycleon, and Deditus, and Mathon, and Odones, and Fabbzus, and Fumiclus, and Perdiccas, and Philotas, and the Prefect of the Praetorians, Diarotas. But in silence, out of their loyalty and by my counsel, they kept secret what they had heard. 

And now we had departed from the sacred trees, but our nostrils were already being struck by the scent of incense and <term>opobalsam</term> <note>original: "oppobalsami"; a rare and highly prized fragrant resin used in perfumes and medicine.</note>. For the Indians were holding sacred rites for the gods toward the ocean, saying that I too was immortal, since I had been able to penetrate even to the end of the age and the world's boundaries. I thanked them, confirming the high opinion they held of us. 

We then arrived in the valley of <term>Jordanes</term> <note>original: "Jordani"; in the Alexander Romance traditions, this refers to a legendary Eastern location rather than the biblical river.</note> in which serpents lived, having stones around their necks which are called emeralds. These serpents feed on <term>laser</term> <note>original: "lafere", a scribe's error for "lasere"; the resin of the silphium plant, used as a spice and medicine</note> and white pepper. <meta>This page continues Alexander the Great’s letter to his teacher Aristotle, detailing the supernatural and natural wonders of India. Having left the Oracular Trees of the Sun and Moon, Alexander and his army now navigate a landscape filled with bizarre creatures and gargantuan flora on their way toward the Great Ocean. The catchword "m uicina" suggests the next page begins with a word such as "multitudine" or "magnitudine," followed by "vicina" (nearby).</meta>

There are pyramids established of three hundred and five feet which were built by the ancient Indians for this purpose. But these serpents, which I described just a moment ago, fight among themselves every year in the early spring, and many fall down from the mountains. From there we brought out not a few emeralds of the finest quality. We proceeded through great dangers and encountered foul beasts of this kind, having the heads of lions and tails with two claws a foot and a half wide, with which they struck our men and rendered them useless <note>The Latin "inutiles" implies the soldiers were maimed or incapacitated.</note>.

Among these were mixed griffins <note>Mythical creatures with the body of a lion and the head and wings of an eagle.</note> having the beaks of eagles and the rest of their bodies appearing like kites <note>A type of bird of prey.</note>. They leapt at our faces and eyes with remarkable speed. They were about two or three feet in size; they lashed our Cyclopes <note>original: "ciclopeos"; this likely refers to a specific unit of heavy-armed soldiers, though the name is borrowed from the one-eyed giants of myth.</note> in a cruel manner with their tails. Some were struck down with arrows, others with military pikes. In that battle, I lost two hundred and six soldiers to the bites of the beasts of both kinds; we killed about nineteen <note>original: ".19."; the number is small compared to the losses, but common in this manuscript tradition.</note> of them.

From there we reached the river Ocinas, which flowed straight to the Ocean without any bend. It was twenty stades <note>A stade is approximately 185 meters or 600 feet; this river would be roughly 2.3 miles wide.</note> wide to the other bank. Reeds grew along the shores to a height of three hundred feet, one of which thirty soldiers could scarcely carry; indeed, they exceeded the height of the tallest trees. In these reeds, we saw a path cleared by a very powerful east wind. For countless thousands of elephants inhabited that same place, who, for some reason I do not know, did not attempt to tear us apart; otherwise, we would have been most cruelly trampled.

Having collected many tusks <note>original: "dentibus"; ivory from the elephants.</note> and constructed rafts made from the reeds, we sailed across the river. On the further shore lived the Belonarian Indians <note>original: "indibelonarii"</note>, covered in the skins of wild beasts. They were not inhospitable; they placed white and purple sponges into our hands, along with trumpet-shells and a kind of snail shell <note>original: "coclearum"</note> large enough to hold two or three *congii* <note>A Roman liquid measure, approximately 3.2 liters.</note>. They also gave us coverlets and soft tunics made from the skins of seals and sea-calves. Furthermore, there were snail shells used as vessels <note>original: "fertariales"; likely a corruption of "feretrales," meaning portable containers.</note> that were very beautiful. Likewise, worms were pulled from that river, thicker than a human thigh, which are to be preferred in flavor over every kind of fish. They also set before us mushrooms of immense size, redder than twenty [measures of] saffron <note>original: "coro"; likely a scribal error for "croco" (saffron).</note>, and moray eels weighing twenty pounds, asserting that even larger ones existed...

<summary>Alexander describes the hazardous journey through India, including battles with lion-headed beasts and griffins, the discovery of giant reeds on the river Ocinas, and an encounter with the friendly Belonarian tribe who provide strange delicacies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Letter of Alexander the Great to Aristotle," following the account of the army's journey through the treacherous landscapes of India. The text shifts from descriptions of monstrous animals to the bizarre humanoid inhabitants of the riverbanks and the final military push toward the kingdom of Porus.</meta>

...in the vicinity of the ocean itself, which was twenty-three miles away. There were, moreover, parrot-wrasse fish <note>original: "scaruos"; the scarus was a prized fish in antiquity, often identified as the parrotfish or wrasse.</note> weighing one hundred and fifty pounds, which were caught in the deep pools using ivory vessels, lest they shatter the fishing reeds with their bite. 

There were also hairy women who lived on fish and remained submerged in the waters. These women would suffocate men of those regions who were unknown to them as they swam in the river, either by holding them down in the depths or by dragging them into the reeds. Because these women were of wondrous appearance, they would greedily tear their captives apart in their passion, or else exhaust them with their sexual lust. We captured only two of them, who were as white as snow and resembled nymphs, with their hair spread out across their backs. 

In the Ganges River there were many admirable portents; I did not think I should write to you about them, lest I seem a teller of fables—except to say that near the river, the North wind blows, and the waters of the Euphrates alone go out to the vents of the South wind. The banks of these rivers are so far apart that one cannot be seen from the other due to the sheer width of the water. From there, we arrived at a small fortress inhabited by Indians.

This race is said to be the most just of all nations; among them, neither homicide, nor adultery, nor perjury, nor drunkenness is committed. They are nourished only by bread, vegetables, and water. There we received warnings from the most excellent elders, who, leading us rightly through the Caspian Gates <note>A famous mountain pass, historically associated with the Caucasus but often shifted to the borders of India in legendary accounts.</note>, brought us back toward Porus <note>The Indian King who famously resisted Alexander at the Battle of the Hydaspes.</note> in Fasace. 

Because I did not listen to these warnings—always wishing and suspecting to follow the blasts of the Northwest wind <note>original: "curi vẽti"; likely a corruption of "Cauri," the Northwest wind.</note>—we fell upon wondrous beasts. From their heads, iron-hard bones protruded like sharpened swords from the crown, and they rushed against our men in the manner of bulls. Then these invincible beasts pierced the shields of many soldiers with their horns. After about eight thousand four hundred and fifty of them were killed, my army finally reached Porus with the greatest labor and danger, and amidst the fear of the soldiers. 

There, I commanded my legate, whom I had placed in charge of the garrison, a man named Alticon, to record the aforementioned Babylonian [affairs] of Porus. I ordered him to make two solid gold pillars, twenty feet high, and to write upon them all that had been achieved. These were to be placed in the furthest part of India, beyond the trophies of Bacchus <note>original: "Liberi"; Liber was the Roman god of wine, identified by the Greeks and Romans with Dionysus, who was legendary for having conquered India before Alexander.</note> and Hercules, which numbered one hundred. I also commanded that my own pillars, also of gold, be established ten feet higher than theirs. These works of wondrous cultivation will be a miracle for future ages, dearest teacher, and will cause no small admiration...

# ¶ The Secrets of Hippocrates begin auspiciously.

Hippocrates said: when, on a day of severe acute illness <term>acute illness: a disease with a rapid onset and short course</term>, a black abscess <note>original: "apostema"</note> appears behind the ear, the patient will die on the seventeenth day.

And when an abscess appears in the joints or in the feet of a person with dropsy <note>original: "idropici"; a historical term for swelling caused by excess fluid, now known as edema</note>, they will be cured one month from the day it appeared.

If, however, an abscess appears above the nose like green or black spots <note>original: "lenes"</note>, the person will die at the end of the year from the day it appeared.

And when a feverish person has pain in the left hypochondria <term>hypochondria: the upper part of the abdomen, just below the ribs</term>, and an abscess appears in that place and then suddenly disappears, they will die on the third day.

And if there is a pustule of a black color or the color of a peacock <note>original: "pavonis coloris"; likely referring to an iridescent blue or green bruise</note> on the big toe of the left foot, know that it is the plague.

And if the patient becomes delirious <note>original: "alienatus"</note> before the third day, they will die on the seventh.

And if there are black burns or pustules on the tongue of a feverish person on a non-critical day <note>original: "die non cretico"; in ancient medicine, "critical days" were specific intervals, such as the 7th or 14th day, when a disease was expected to change for better or worse</note>, they portend death; and the sign of this is that the person becomes delirious after pains throughout the whole body.

Heart trouble <note>original: "Cardyaca"</note> immediately after sleep signifies an abundance of humors in the body and portends sudden death.

<margin>I</margin>
When an abscess as large as a hazelnut emerges on the eyelid of a feverish person, if it does not subside in feeling and weight, they will die <note>The Latin "mouetur" (moves) is likely a transcription error for "morietur" (will die), consistent with the surrounding entries</note> on the thirtieth day.

One who talks much in their sleep is predicted to become apoplectic <note>original: "appopleticum"; referring to a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness</note>.

<margin>P</margin>
A lack of thirst during an acute fever, or coldness of the feet, portends death on the tenth day.

<margin>ri</margin>
When a black abscess emerges suddenly on the tip of the nose with strong pain, the person will die in two months.

One who loses their sense of smell without having a cold <note>original: "reuminate"</note> is predicted to have a fever.

<margin>ra</margin>
Pain in the joints in one's youth without a cause signifies a short life.

<margin>oc</margin>
Trembling of the knees after sleep, even while strength remains,

One who desires to eat at unusual hours: poisonous humors in his stomach are predicted.

One who studies books extensively against his own will: sleeplessness, or mania, or melancholy <term>melancholy: a condition caused by an excess of black bile, leading to depression or madness</term> is predicted.
One whom harmful foods quickly injure: it signifies a weakness of the vital organs <note>original: "membrarum principalium"</note>.
Those who can endure much thirst are of a strong constitution <note>original: "complexionis"; the balance of humors in the body</note>.
Gullibility signifies a flaw of the intellect: but a moderate amount is more appropriate.
Those who have passed their fiftieth year and suffer from kidney pain: they are not cured. But those things which benefit them most are medicines working by hidden properties and virtues. <note>original: "Cier."; likely a truncated citation or catchword</note>

He who is slow to move is predicted to suffer from pain in the joints.
He whose sweat of the armpits stinks: leprosy is predicted.

# A SUBTLE AND USEFUL TREATISE
->By Averroes on Poisons.<-

Poison comes either from its specific form, or from its complexional form; and this latter is caused either by heat or cold, or by moisture. Chapter 12.

All species of deadly substances fall into two categories, under which there are almost infinite varieties. The first is of those that kill by their **specific form**: <note>original: "forma specifica"; the essential nature of a thing that acts through its whole being rather than just its basic qualities</note> which, although they are composed of the four elements, have a unique manner in their mixture and they break down according to the quantity of their receptors until a single unity is made from them. Thus it is with the species of those that kill by their specific form, which have attained a "true form" from the mixture of elements infused therein, and this form has been made contrary to human life because it opposes his spirit and the heat that governs him. These are such as the juice of the spurge-laurel, monkshood <note>original: "nappellus"; a highly toxic plant also known as aconite</note>, and species of spurge <note>original: "titimali"</note>, and metals, and other stones similar to them.

<margin>
1
1
1
a
</margin>
The second genus is of those that kill by their **complexional form**, that is, by the primary qualities of these things. These are of three species. One is that which kills through its wondrous heat, which extinguishes the natural heat, just as a lamp is extinguished in the presence of a great fire, or because of an intensely hot sun (as seen in the "lion" <note>this likely refers to a specific constellation or astrological influence, or a highly "solar" substance</note>). From this type comes the cashew-nut <note>original: "anacardus"; the marking nut, known for its caustic sap</note>, gums, and similar things. Under this species fall all things that kill because of excessive and superfluous heat, until they are called ulcerating and caustic: such as vitriol <note>original: "vitriolum"; various sulfate minerals</note>, verdigris <note>original: "viride gris"</note>, lime, orpiment <note>original: "auripigmentum"; an arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, Spanish flies <note>original: "cantariades"; beetles used to create blisters</note>, litharge <note>original: "lithargirium"; lead oxide</note>, asphodel, realgar <note>original: "reragal"; a toxic arsenic sulfide</note>, sulfur, and burnt copper. And also others which kill by a bad poisonous quality contrary to nature, such as all purgative species that act by "attraction" <note>purgatives that forcefully draw humors from the body</note>, like scammony, hermodactyls <note>original: "hermodactili"; likely a species of Colchicum</note>, white and black hellebore, and...

### Signs of the Action of Poisons by Specific Form. Chapter 2.

Furthermore, it is necessary to give you the experience by which you may recognize
which of these kinds was ingested through those things that appear
to you in the symptoms of the sick person. The symptoms of the kind of poisons
that kill by their specific form are: great fainting and a darkening of the tongue and
face, coldness of the extremities, dizziness and a murmuring sound <note>likely tinnitus or ringing in the ears</note>, much
sleeplessness: and a distortion of the ears. If all these symptoms
come upon the sick person immediately after a meal, judge that he has taken
a poison that kills by its specific form: but vomiting and sleeplessness
are not specific symptoms, but general ones.

### The Cure of Poisons that Act by Specific Form. Chapter 3.

And let us begin with the universal medicine for the two kinds and let us
say that it is vomiting, so that it may be quickly provoked with cow’s
butter dissolved in water in which sage, dill, and similar things
have boiled. Once this is strained, let the butter be dissolved in it along with olive
oil: and hazelnut oil: and let this be done three or four times and bind the
extremities tightly: because with this you draw the humors and spirits
to them and you likewise lead the power of heat: but saving...

...so that he may be a great prince, and let these aforesaid things suffice for many.

### ¶ Signs of poisons that act through heat. Chapter 4.

When the *Canon* <note>Referring to the *Canon of Medicine* by Avicenna, the standard medical textbook of the Middle Ages</note> describes the medicines that should be given to those who have drunk poison in its "specific form"—that is, in its own nature and before the general administration of medicines—you should know the symptoms that indicate what kind of poison it is. Know, therefore, that every person who is poisoned by a lethal heat-based poison (which is the first of the three types), even if it does not corrode, it causes those who eat it to swell—such as the marking nut <note>original: "anacardus"; the fruit of the Semecarpus anacardium, known for its caustic sap</note>. This happens to such an extent that a person taking these things, even while standing in cold water, actually becomes hot, even though it is not among the corrosive substances like vitriol <note>original: "vitriolum"; a term for various metal sulfates or sulfuric acid</note>. All its symptoms are a strong burning sensation accompanied by thirst. If it is the marking nut alone, the symptoms do not reach the source of the burning immediately, but they produce the greatest thirst. However, if the poison is from the corrosives, there occurs a strong burning and a "boiling" sensation with intense pains harming the stomach and intestines. And similarly for those who have eaten food that is extremely boiling, because they suffer from acidic belching <note>original: "eructi"</note>, vomiting is increased in them. This symptom is common to all, and sometimes strangury <note>A condition of painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination</note> and a most sharp fever happen to them. When you see these symptoms, judge that the poison taken is due to the nature of the temperament <note>original: "complexionis"; the balance of the four bodily humors</note>, namely in a hot and moist quality, or a hot and burning quality.

### ¶ Treatment of poisons that act through heat. Chapter 5.

For the treatment of such people is this: you should provoke vomiting many times according to the order mentioned above. Certainly, because of the great burning, the water in which the butter <note>original: "butyrum"</note> is to be dissolved should not have boiled dill <note>original: "anettum"</note>, nor sage, nor anything similar; for in this case, one should not use any heating substances, but only pleasant, moistening things that take away the heat and dryness that burn the internal organs. Therefore, take the "cold" seeds of lettuce and fenugreek, a little bit of licorice <note>original: "liquiritię"</note>, and jujube plums <note>original: "pruna iuiubę"</note>. Let them boil in water until half has evaporated, then strain it. Afterward, dissolve cow's butter in it and let the patient drink it; then provoke vomiting once or twice. Afterward, take one ounce each of violet sugar <note>original: "zuccari uiolati"</note> and poppy seeds. Let them be dissolved in the aforesaid water with two ounces of sweet almond oil <note>original: "oleo amygdalarum dulcium"</note>. If sweet almond oil cannot be found, put in its place sweet olive oil washed in cold water, or violet oil if he vomits well. If not, provoke vomiting until he vomits. Afterwards, let him wait until he drinks many times the milk of a woman and a donkey mixed with some of the aforesaid oils; and in that milk...

<summary>This page describes the diagnostic signs of "hot" poisons, distinguishing between caustic substances like vitriol and swelling agents like the marking nut, and provides a cooling treatment protocol involving fats and moistening herbs.</summary>
<keywords>toxicology, Avicenna, marking nut, vitriol, strangury, violet sugar, almond oil, emetics, medicinal milk</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page concludes the treatment for "hot" poisons—warning specifically against the use of theriac in such cases—and moves into the diagnostic signs and cures for poisons characterized by "coldness," such as scorpion powder or camphor.</meta>

...if [the previously mentioned remedy] is not found, provide goat's milk, and also for a drink, provide almond milk and the "milk" of cold seeds <note>original: "lac seminum frigidorum"; an emulsion made from grinding cooling seeds like cucumber or melon with water</note> many times. After the vomiting has ceased, cause his bowels to be constricted <note>To stop the purging/diarrhea caused by the poison</note> as we described above. After constricting, cleanse him with cassia pulp, manna, and violet sugar. Let a water be prepared in which are placed: common violet seeds, one ounce of each; jujubes and sebestens <note>original: "siuube sebeften"; types of Mediterranean and Asian plums used for their soothing mucilage</note>, two plums; three drams <note>indicated by the "3" symbol</note> of cleaned licorice; one ounce of pearled barley; and half an ounce [of the seeds]. Let all these be cooked in two pounds of water until it is reduced by half to one pound. After straining, **Take:** cassia fistula pulp, violet sugar, and manna, half an ounce of each. Let them be liquefied in the aforementioned water, which he should drink warm in the morning, not cold. He should not eat until he has been cleansed. Keep this patient from all meat that produces heat, and from all hot and acrid foods. Let him eat very small chickens and drink wine that is well-watered. Let him be managed according to the regimen for those suffering from fever. If he is cured, by God's help, let him abstain from theriac <note>A famous ancient antidote containing many heating herbs and spices</note>, because in this case, it would "add evil to evil" <note>The "heat" of the theriac would worsen the "hot" nature of the poison.</note>.

### ¶ Signs of poisons that act through coldness. Chapter 6.

The symptoms of the second type—namely, those with a cold quality—include substances like scorpion and camphor. There are those who eat the powder of dried scorpions, or drink wine in which they have died, or take it in oil in great quantity or through much grinding. His body becomes extremely cold, both inside and out; his appetite is diminished; his flesh dries up and his eyes bulge. It produces a sensation in his flesh like the pricking of needles, and the victims waste away and grow thin until they die.

### The cure for the same. Chapter 7.

The proper cure for this is vomiting, as was done with those poisons that kill by their specific form. After this, let him drink theriac or mithridatum <note>Another complex poly-pharmaceutical antidote</note> according to the manner described above. Afterward, let him have sweet almond oil with a preserve of sugar and the oil of the same, and let him use that.

->Here ends the work.<-

<summary>This concluding page details the dietary and medicinal regimen for recovering from "hot" poisons and describes the symptoms and treatments for "cold" poisons, ending with a warning that theriac should only be used for cold-natured toxins.</summary>
